An uncertainty-aware surrogate workflow for multi-hole diesel sprays

Author Linan Jiang
Title Data-Driven Penetration Prediction and Probabilistic Impingement Screening
      Based on Mie Scattering Imaging — An uncertainty-aware surrogate workflow
      for multi-hole diesel sprays
Degree programme Electronics and Nanotechnology
Major Microelectronic Circuit Design
Supervisor Prof. Ossi Kaario
Advisors Dr Zeeshan Ahmad, Dr Qiang Cheng
Collaborative partner Wärtsilä
Date 19 May 2026               Number of pages 184                  Language English
Abstract
Pilot diesel injections in multi-fuel marine engines are short, highly transient events.
If the liquid spray reaches the piston crown or cylinder wall before ignition develops,
it can cause wall wetting, lubricant dilution, and poor mixture preparation. This thesis
develops an engineering screening workflow that converts high-speed Mie-scattering
spray videos into penetration measurements and trained surrogate models for early
spray–wall impingement risk assessment. The data consist of room-temperature, non-
reacting, top-view recordings from the Wärtsilä Optical Spray Combustion Chamber,
covering multiple operating conditions and nozzle geometries.
     The workflow combines multi-hole video processing, plume-wise feature extrac-
tion, and a reduced-order trajectory fit that smooths noisy labels and mitigates the
downward bias caused by right-censoring at the camera field-of-view limit. On these
fitted targets, a neural network produced by three-stage curriculum training predicts
the penetration mean together with a heteroscedastic uncertainty estimate, staged so
that disappearance from the image window is not mistaken for physical saturation.
     On 540,367 points from 9 nozzle campaigns under an uncensored evaluation
protocol, the final multilayer perceptron (MLP) model achieves a five-seed mean
root mean squared error (RMSE) of 4.19 mm and mean absolute error (MAE) of
2.98 mm with conservative 1𝜎/2𝜎 uncertainty coverage of 88.8%/99.0%, reducing
RMSE by more than 54% relative to calibrated Hiroyasu–Arai and Naber–Siebers
baselines. Post-training feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM) adaptation mainly
improved probabilistic calibration scores relative to a controlled pooled baseline, in
known-family adaptation experiments. A sparse variational Gaussian process (SVGP)
alternative attains better point accuracy but markedly less conservative coverage; the
two backbones represent different accuracy–coverage trade-offs.
     A simplified geometric projection finally maps the predicted penetration distribution
onto probabilistic wall-impingement screening scores. The workflow is intended as an
early-stage screening tool within its measured operating domain, ahead of—not in
place of—computational fluid dynamics (CFD) simulation and engine testing.
Keywords uncertainty quantification, surrogate modeling, Gaussian process, spray
             imaging, penetration, Mie scattering

Use of AI Tools
This thesis was prepared with assistance from AI-based tools for exploratory code
implementation, script automation, data-screening support, summarising code and
processing workflows, visualisation preparation, figure-preparation support, para-
phrasing, grammar correction, and language polishing. AI tools were used within a
layered and auditable research workflow: code-level outputs were checked through
executable programs, logs, and intermediate files; data and figure-level outputs were
cross-checked against exported tables, diagnostic plots, and numerical results; and
text-level revisions were grounded in the preceding code, data, figures, literature, and
author interpretation.
     AI tools were not used as sources of scientific evidence, citation sources, sources of
experimental results, or independent authors. The research questions, methodological
choices, implementation decisions, interpretation of results, and final thesis text were
reviewed, edited, and confirmed by the author. The author remains fully responsible
for the accuracy, originality, code, analyses, figures, results, and conclusions presented
in this thesis.

1    Introduction
The international shipping industry faces concurrent pressures to reduce greenhouse
gas emissions, diversify fuels, and ensure operational reliability. The International
Maritime Organization (IMO) 2023 strategy and industry order trends indicate that
alternative fuel propulsion systems have gradually transitioned from long-term concepts
to engineering realities [1], [2]. As fuels such as liquefied natural gas (LNG), methanol,
and ammonia are adopted to mitigate carbon emission intensity, marine dual-fuel and
multi-fuel engines have emerged as a vital trajectory in the research and development
of large-scale power systems [3], [4], [5].
    The introduction of alternative fuels also creates new challenges for marine engine
combustion processes. Low-reactivity main fuels are difficult to auto-ignite under
practical compression ignition conditions; the engine therefore still needs a highly
reactive and robust ignition source. While the fuel system becomes more low-carbon
and adaptable due to alternative fuels, the combustion process still hinges on one small
but critical local event: the diesel pilot injection [5], [6]. For injector development,
the key control variable is therefore the spatial development of the pilot spray itself.
Penetration length, cone angle, and morphology set where the ignition kernel forms and
how large it is. That kernel, in turn, governs main-fuel mixing, flame propagation, and
overall combustion efficiency [7], [8], [9]. If the pilot penetration is excessively short,
the ignition kernel may lack sufficient volume or emerge in an unfavorable region;
conversely, if the penetration is overly long, the spray may deviate from the design
intent and elevate the risk of spray-wall interactions. In an industrial development
cycle, the value of a fast, evidence-based penetration predictor is therefore not to
certify combustion performance directly. Instead, it is to reduce the number of
nozzle and operating-condition candidates that must proceed to costly engine tests or
computational fluid dynamics (CFD) simulations, to flag wall-proximity risk early,
and to reuse existing optical spray archives as a low-cost engineering evidence base.
    When this control objective remains unfulfilled, the same pilot injection may also
emerge as a source of operational risk. If the liquid-phase envelope reaches the piston
crown or cylinder liner prior to the formation of an effective ignition kernel, it may
induce wall wetting, lubricating oil dilution, deteriorated mixture preparation, and
elevated unburned hydrocarbon emissions [10], [11], [12]. Consequently, the practical
question is whether the spray’s spatial development stays inside a window that sustains
ignition, mixing, and wall clearance simultaneously.




                                            11
    Existing research and engineering practices have formulated several established
methodologies for assessing pilot injection development: engine testing, CFD, empiri-
cal modeling, and optical diagnostics. Engine testing captures sprays, combustion, wall
interactions, and emissions together in a complete system, while high-fidelity CFD
resolves finer flow detail. Empirical penetration correlations and phenomenological
models can rapidly describe spray development; comparing penetration length against
wall distance naturally yields a geometric criterion for first-order wall impingement
screening [13], [14], [15]. Optical spray experiments offer a complementary path-
way, yielding reproducible observations of the liquid-phase envelope; among these,
high-speed Mie scattering imaging is particularly well-suited for examining liquid
boundaries and macroscopic penetration evolution [16].
    Yet each of these approaches only partially meets the need for rapid, conditionally
parameterized, and experimentally substantiated pilot penetration prediction. Engine
tests are limited by high costs and long development cycles; CFD requires complex
boundary conditions, careful model calibration, and heavy computational effort. A
single high-fidelity Spray A large-eddy simulation can be up to six orders of magnitude
more expensive than a comparable reduced-order spray model [17], and in reacting
cases its cost is dominated by stiff chemistry integration—which alone can consume
90–98% of the run time [18], [19]. Exhaustively sweeping nozzle geometry and
operating conditions at that fidelity is therefore impractical for routine design screening.
Empirical models feature a compact structure and are readily interpretable, yet short
pilot injections make them harder to apply, as the observable window may be mostly
dominated by the start-of-injection and end-of-injection transients [20], [21]. Optical
experiments provide image evidence, yet image post-processing outcomes remain
discrete observation tables rather than predictive models directly applicable to rapid
design iteration.
    Therefore, this thesis targets the intermediate-layer gap between high-throughput
optical spray archives and higher-cost marine engine validation. This gap is shaped by
three constraints: multi-hole nozzles produce nominally similar but physically different
plumes; a finite field of view (FOV) renders a fraction of the late-stage penetration
history as right-censored observations; and the raw Mie intensity is susceptible to
illumination, glare, and multiple scattering perturbations. If the visible end point of
each observed trajectory is naively treated as the physical end point, the model risks
learning that exiting the image window represents physical saturation. In short, what
is missing is neither a novel physical theory of sprays nor an additional single-point
diagnostic measurement, but rather a rapid, reproducible, uncertainty-aware, and
well-scoped pre-screening layer situated between optical experimental archives and
computationally or experimentally expensive validations.




                                            12
    Data-driven surrogate models offer a viable path toward this objective. Recent
studies show that neural networks can predict macroscopic spray characteristics,
such as penetration and cone angle, leveraging operating conditions [22], [23]. In
practice, these surrogates are typically trained as deterministic point predictors on
uncensored laboratory penetration data, so they neither quantify prediction uncertainty
that varies with operating condition (heteroscedastic uncertainty) nor account for the
right-censoring imposed by a finite imaging window. These are precisely the two
requirements an experimentally grounded pre-screening layer must satisfy. Uncertainty
here is not a cosmetic addition: because the screening output is a wall-contact
probability rather than a binary pass/fail verdict, the surrogate must emit calibrated
predictive distributions that can be ranked, not single-point estimates. In this thesis,
multi-batch Mie recordings acquired under identical optical configurations can be
transformed into plume-wise temporal observables, which are then used to train a
mapping from operating conditions to penetration trajectories. Accordingly, this
thesis aims to design and evaluate an auditable workflow that turns large-scale Mie
scattering spray video archives into trajectory predictions usable for design triage. The
workflow is deliberately framed as a design triage tool: it ranks candidate pilot-spray
geometries by Mie-defined penetration and geometric wall-proximity risk, while
leaving ignition quality, combustion efficiency, emissions, and harmful wall wetting to
subsequent engine-representative validation. This thesis pursues that layer through six
methodological stages, carried end to end from a raw optical archive to a deployable
screening surrogate:

   1. Data extraction — recovering plume-wise penetration observables from raw
      Mie video;

   2. Censoring-aware curation — cleaning those observables into data populations
      that respect the finite imaging window;

   3. Target construction — building physics-informed penetration targets from the
      censored trajectories;

   4. Staged training — fitting an uncertainty-aware surrogate through a multi-stage
      curriculum;

   5. Evaluation — testing it under in-domain and out-of-distribution (held-out-
      nozzle) protocols;

   6. Screening deployment — coupling the trained surrogate to a probabilistic
      wall-impingement interface.




                                           13
This staged decomposition is deliberate. Large-scale data acquisition, quality-aware
data curation, target and label engineering, staged training with knowledge distillation,
held-out evaluation, and deployment-time adaptation are the same engineering stages
now documented for large-scale machine-learning and foundation-model development
[24], [25]; the contribution of this thesis is to carry that discipline end to end, at the
scale a single optical-spray screening problem actually demands. Chapters 3 through 5
develop these six stages in turn, and Chapter 7 returns to the same sequence to state
what is validated at each link and what remains open.
    The primary contributions of this thesis include a Compute Unified Device Ar-
chitecture (CUDA)-accelerated video-to-observable workflow, a reproducible archive
and checkpoint contract for rerunning large optical campaigns, a reduced-order fitting
strategy tailored for right-censored penetration targets, a multi-stage uncertainty-
aware learning framework, and a simplified probabilistic wall-proximity screening
methodology. The scope of this thesis is deliberately confined to non-reacting, non-
evaporating diesel pilot sprays under controlled constant-volume vessel conditions.
The model should be seen as an engineering screening tool that works strictly within
its data-supported domain, rather than a universal predictor that is accurate under
arbitrary uncharted operating conditions, a direct engine combustion predictor, or a
fully validated ground-truth model for tangible liquid impingement.
    Chapter 2 reviews the relevant literature across optics, computer vision, parallel
programming, optimization, and machine learning, and lays out the theoretical
groundwork needed for this thesis; Chapter 3 describes the experimental data and
the image-processing workflow; Chapter 4 details the trajectory targets, surrogate
model development, and probabilistic screening; Chapter 5 reports the experimental
results; Chapter 6 explains which components have been validated and where the
current limits of the approach lie; Chapter 7 separates the validated results from the
prototype’s limitations and the directions for future work.




                                           14
2    Literature Review and Technical Basis
The purpose of this chapter is not to preemptively repeat the subsequent implementation
details, but to clarify the origins of these methodological choices. Centering on Mie
geometric observables, short-injection-pulse penetration behaviour, multi-plume reg-
istration, right-censored targets, uncertainty-aware surrogate models, and probabilistic
wall-impingement risk screening, this thesis outlines the support provided by existing
research while highlighting the gaps that have not been comprehensively covered by
any single literature thread. Therefore, this chapter serves as a transitional bridge:
before delving into the specific workflows, it elucidates the logical chain between
existing knowledge and the engineering design decisions in this thesis. Readers with
a primary background in fluid mechanics and mechanical design, who wish to first
familiarize themselves with the terminology of computer vision, robust optimization,
graphics processing unit (GPU) parallel computing, and neural network training
involved in this thesis, are referred to Appendix B.

2.1 Principles of Mie Scattering Imaging and Geometric Ob-
    servables
Mie scattering is widely used for dense liquid-phase spray visualization, a primary
reason being its relatively concise experimental implementation; compared to many
alternative diagnostic techniques, Mie imaging is easier to integrate with high-speed
photography platforms, thereby achieving high temporal resolution [16]. However, a
relatively easy imaging implementation does not imply that quantitative interpretation
is equally straightforward. Mie imaging records the scattering intensity field, a
signal that cannot be directly equated to fuel mass or liquid volume fraction. Its
scattering response depends on droplet size distribution, number density, refractive
index, wavelength, scattering angle relative to the illumination direction, and path
length through the spray volume; in dense regions, multiple scattering and shadowing
effects further alter the image intensity distribution [16], [26]. Therefore, attempting
to directly invert the complete spray physical state from pixel intensities is inherently
an ill-posed problem.
    Different optical diagnostic methods possess distinct response mechanisms to
spray information:

    • DBI (Diffuse Background Illumination): measures path-integrated extinction
      by applying the Beer–Lambert law to a spatially uniform diffuse transmitted-
      light field, yielding a measurement proportional to the liquid volume fraction
      integrated along the line of sight, and is typically suitable for extracting liquid-
      phase contours [27].




                                           15
    • Schlieren and Shadowgraph: Schlieren responds to the first spatial derivative
      of the refractive index field, while Shadowgraph responds to its second derivative
      (the Laplacian); both can image liquid-spray boundaries through the refractive
      index discontinuity at the liquid–gas interface, but their signals are simultaneously
      modulated by gas-phase density gradients and vapour-phase distributions in
      evaporating conditions, making it harder to isolate the pure liquid-phase boundary
      compared to droplet scattering methods.

    • LIF: characterizes vapor distribution or equivalence ratio through fuel or
      tracer fluorescence (liquid-phase LIF and exciplex techniques can also separate
      liquid and vapor contributions [28], but both require fluorescent additives or
      dedicated tracer compounds that impose chemical compatibility constraints,
      which effectively precludes their use with commercial diesel in facilities such as
      the Optical Spray Combustion Chamber (OSCC)).

    • PIV: focuses on velocity fields and turbulent structures.

    • PDPA: provides single-point droplet size and velocity information; recon-
      structing a two-dimensional spray envelope requires spatial scanning, making it
      unsuitable for instantaneous full-field multi-plume characterisation.

While all these methods have their respective values, their alignment with the target
observables differs for the non-evaporating, non-reacting liquid-phase diesel sprays
focused on in this thesis: LIF, PIV, and PDPA lean toward component, flow field,
or single-point size-velocity measurements, which struggle to directly support the
high-temporal-resolution liquid-phase envelope tracking required here; although
Schlieren and Shadowgraph can provide spray shape information, their specificity
for liquid-phase boundaries is not as explicit as that of droplet scattering methods;
DBI and Mie are much closer to the tasks of extracting liquid-phase contours,
penetration, and cone angles that this thesis concerns [16]. The comparison also
has a hardware-cost dimension: time-resolved planar laser diagnostics such as PIV
and planar LIF require high-repetition-rate pulsed lasers to follow an individual
injection event, and with conventional low-repetition-rate pulsed lasers they degrade
to phase-locked ensemble sampling at one frame per injection, losing the single-shot
transient penetration history. Volume-illuminated Mie imaging instead operates with
continuous illumination, shifting the temporal-resolution burden from the laser onto
the high-speed camera and avoiding the cost of a high-repetition-rate pulsed laser
system altogether [16].
    This positioning is consistent with existing research. Early two-dimensional Mie
scattering work conducted inside optical diesel engines treated the jet tip penetration
and cone angle as practical outputs for image analysis, rather than pursuing complete
liquid-phase state reconstruction [29]; Even in recent imaging studies with higher
instrument levels and synchronization precision, liquid-phase spray comparisons still
revolve around penetration and expansion angle metrics [30]. This implies that studies
based on Mie videos should be judged primarily by the quality, repeatability, and
explicit boundaries of the observables they extract.

                                           16
    The data used in this thesis comes from completed top-view multi-hole nozzle
Mie scattering experiments by Wärtsilä OSCC. Compared to common backlit, side-
view, single-hole isolated setups, this experiment uses side-window illumination
and places the injector on the back of the chamber, allowing a single imaging event
to simultaneously cover all spray plumes. This arrangement is more suitable for
preserving the plume-to-plume and shot-to-shot variations required for subsequent
modeling in this thesis. Based on the aforementioned methodological attributes, this
thesis does not interpret pixel intensities as thermodynamic ground truths for direct
inversion, but rather treats them as geometric cues for the liquid-phase boundaries;
The image processing objectives are explicitly restricted to extracting operational
observables such as penetration, cone angle, projected area, and boundary positions,
as such geometric descriptions are more defensible than claims of precise liquid-phase
distributions [16], [31]. Among these, penetration is the core input for subsequent
trajectory fitting, surrogate modeling, and wall-impingement risk screening [13], [14],
[16], [31], [32]. Under this positioning, the slowly varying halos near the nozzle
caused by multiple scattering are not interpreted as local liquid-phase concentrations,
but are treated as background components to be suppressed during post-processing.
    Mie imaging also carries a characteristic physical limitation of multiple scattering.
In the dense liquid core region near the nozzle, the high droplet number density
produces intense forward-scattered and wide-angle scattered light that redistributes
intensity spatially, blurring the sharp liquid-phase boundary and producing a diffuse
halo rather than a clear edge [16], [26], [33]. Berrocal et al. quantified the optical depth
threshold beyond which multiple scattering becomes the dominant image-formation
mechanism in dense diesel sprays, demonstrating that liquid-core regions typically
operate well into this multiply scattered regime. This thesis treats this halo as a
slowly varying background component rather than a local liquid-phase concentration.
The corresponding suppression methods (gradient-domain filtering) and geometric
corrections under line-of-sight integration are discussed in the image processing
workflow in Chapter 3.
    Equally important is that quantitative processing should strive to preserve the
linear response of the camera’s raw gray values, and should not use display-oriented
gamma correction, frame-by-frame display gain, or local contrast enhancement as
measurement inputs [34]. Such nonlinear or adaptive grayscale transformations
alter local gradient proportions, relative threshold relationships, and the cumulative
distribution support ranges used for leading-edge extraction, making boundary positions
no longer determined solely by optical records and fixed post-processing rules.
Therefore, the enhancement steps used later in this thesis are not intended to make
the images more visually appealing, but to extract geometric boundaries from the raw
Mie records under fixed, reproducible rules.




                                            17
     It must be further emphasized that the process from Mie intensity fields to geometric
observables depends on specific measurement rules. The reported “penetration” is
therefore not a universal ground truth, but the result of a specific measurement rule
acting on the optical signal. The Engine Combustion Network (ECN) benchmark
studies provided systematic evidence for this methodological dependency: inter-facility
comparisons of nominally identical injectors (Spray A and Spray D) revealed scatter
in liquid-phase penetration arising from a combination of genuine ambient-condition
differences, distinct optical setups (DBI versus Mie), and post-processing threshold
definitions [35], [36], [37]. Separately, Magnotti and Genzale showed that even
within a single dataset, contrasting metrics such as maximum liquid penetration,
path-integrated liquid volume, and projected spray area can lead to substantially
different model validation conclusions [38], explicitly establishing the optical liquid-
phase boundary as a quantity defined by a specific measurement protocol rather than
an absolute representation of the true liquid-phase distribution. The experimental
conditions in this thesis differ from those ECN benchmark injectors in both nozzle
geometry and ambient thermodynamic state: This thesis uses top-view data of multi-
hole marine nozzles under room-temperature pressurized environments, involving
no fuel evaporation. These non-evaporating conditions make liquid-phase geometric
boundary tracking appropriate for the penetration and cone-angle observables used
here, but they do not turn the optical boundary into a protocol-independent physical
surface. The methodological principles of ECN therefore still apply: Penetration
and boundary values depend on post-processing rules, and this dependency must be
incorporated into the interpretation of the results.

2.2 Empirical Models of Transient Nozzle Penetration Physics
    and Their Limitations
Traditional empirical spray models have long revolved around penetration, because
penetration can compress complex spray evolution into a few physically meaningful
low-order quantities. In the Hiroyasu–Arai framework, the tip penetration is typically
expressed using a multi-stage empirical formulation: it grows approximately linearly
with time before breakup, and transitions to a square-root scaling after breakup. Its
simplified form can be written as
                                         {︄
                                            𝑡,  𝑡 < 𝑡𝑏 ,
                               𝑆tip (𝑡) ∝ 1/2
                                            𝑡 , 𝑡 ≥ 𝑡𝑏 ,

where 𝑡 𝑏 is the breakup time, and the proportionality coefficients are determined
jointly by the pressure difference, nozzle diameter, and fluid properties [13]. Before
breakup the coefficient is controlled by the nozzle exit velocity; after breakup it is
jointly controlled by momentum flux, ambient gas density, and nozzle hole scale.
This thesis adopts a fittable version of this correlation, with its three dimensionless
coefficients recalibrated on the training splits, as a physical baseline; the exact fitted
form and calibration protocol are given in Chapter 4 (Section 4.7).


                                           18
     The Naber–Siebers correlation retains a similar momentum scaling but places
more emphasis on the impact of ambient gas density and spray dispersion angle on
penetration [14]. Because expansion-angle observations are not reliably available
across all samples, this thesis implements a reduced-order, time-delayed form of the
correlation that fits only a gain and a start delay, deliberately foregoing the angle-
correction term rather than relying on training-set or oracle cone angles; the exact
form is given alongside the Hiroyasu–Arai baseline in Section 4.7.
     The third type of reference is not a closed-form spray physics equation, but a
Gaussian process (GP) non-parametric regression baseline [39]. Rather than fitting a
handful of physical coefficients, a GP adjusts kernel hyperparameters—amplitude, per-
dimension length scales under Automatic Relevance Determination (ARD), and noise
level—to learn a more flexible conditional function of the same standardized operating-
condition features. This thesis implements a sparse variational, heteroscedastic version
of this baseline [40], detailed as an alternative surrogate backbone in Section 4.6. The
commonality of these three baselines is that they do not directly read image pixels, but
rather read the penetration observations and test matrix features exported by the same
image processing chain; the difference lies in that Hiroyasu–Arai and Naber–Siebers
restrict the penetration to explicit physical function families, while the GP learns a
more flexible conditional function in the feature space through kernel functions. The
advantage of these three baselines is their ability to provide interpretable or statistically
controlled references for the overall penetration history. Their limitations, however,
mainly emerge during short injection events. Research by Zhou et al. on injection start
transients shows that when sac pressurization and needle valve opening processes are
non-negligible, the early penetration behavior is more complex than classical two-stage
laws; During the start of injection (SOI) phase, penetration initially approximates a
𝑡 3/2 scaling, then transitions to 𝑡 3/4 , and only later gradually approaches a 𝑡 1/2 behavior
[20]. For this thesis, this is not merely general background information, but a direct
constraint: The diesel pre-injection duration is short enough that the early transients
occupy a significant portion of the observable signal. Therefore, any single and rigid
empirical penetration law struggles to comprehensively describe the entire trajectory.
     Penetration studies after the end of injection further illustrate that what changes
across stages is not just the power-law exponent, but also the relevant time variable
itself. Zhou et al. showed that after the end of injection (EOI) the tip penetration
enters a deceleration branch in which the time coordinate shifts from the ASOI clock 𝑡
to a delayed clock (𝑡 − 𝑡𝑖 ), with exponents transitioning from 𝑡 1/2 to (𝑡 − 𝑡𝑖 ) 1/2 and
(𝑡 − 𝑡𝑖 ) 1/4 [21]. The full four-branch piecewise expression is reproduced in Chapter 4
where it is used as a baseline reference. The physical implication is that the effective
time origin itself carries physical meaning: the power-law exponent alone does not
characterise the penetration regime, because the clock from which time is measured
also changes at each transition. In short pre-injections, the finite observation window
may simultaneously contain both SOI-referenced and EOI-referenced clock segments,
and the switching moments are difficult to reliably identify from noisy Mie-extracted
trajectories.



                                              19
    The failure of the quasi-steady picture is not determined solely by fluid mechanics;
the upstream nozzle hardware itself introduces additional dynamics. In common-
rail injection systems, the opening and closing of the needle valve are mechanical
processes of finite duration; before the needle valve is fully lifted, the effective flow
area varies with time, continually modulating the instantaneous injection rate and
the near-nozzle flow field [13], [41]. Internal nozzle flow under high pressure and
potential cavitation further alter the jet structure and atomization state, mechanisms
which classical penetration correlations do not attempt to characterize [23]. For
short-pulse pre-injections, the opening and closing transients themselves may occupy a
considerable proportion of the injection event, rendering the quasi-steady assumption
valid only fleetingly in the middle segment at best.
    This leads to the modelling judgement in this thesis: rigid empirical power laws
remain valuable physical baselines but do not constitute the complete target form
for this dataset, so they are not discarded once they fail as complete predictors but
reused as scaling priors. The Hiroyasu–Arai post-breakup amplitude is repurposed
as an amplitude-scaling prior for the surrogate’s target and feature design—a target-
preconditioning step, not a strict non-dimensionalization—so that the residual pressure,
duration, plume-count, and inclination dependences are left for the statistical model to
learn; Chapter 4 (Section 4.4) develops this scaling choice and its empirical support
in detail. Constrained by physical scaling and auditable preprocessing, data-driven
models can absorb the combined effects of multiple overlapping physical mechanisms
on the observed trajectories without needing to pre-identify and parameterize every
transient segment.




                                           20
2.3 Computer-Vision and Parallel-Computing Foundations for
    Multi-Plume Mie Video Processing
Converting a terabyte-scale archive of 12-bit Mie videos into plume-resolved geometric
observables draws on several mature, independently well-established technique families
rather than a single named method. Nozzle-center calibration is an instance of
noisy circle fitting, for which least-squares circle estimation is a standard tool [42],
[43]. Suppressing multiplicative illumination and glare while preserving a spray-
only foreground is an instance of homomorphic (log-domain) signal processing,
which converts multiplicative disturbances into additive ones to stabilize background
subtraction [44], [45]. Enhancing boundary geometry while suppressing diffuse
low-frequency structure is standard gradient-based edge detection [45], [46], [47].
Registering the repeating, rotationally offset plumes that unfold around a common
nozzle center is an instance of Fourier-domain phase correlation applied to a polar-angle
representation [48], [49]; this registration step is not merely geometric bookkeeping,
since a multi-hole spray is not a mechanical replication of a single-hole spray 𝑁
times—internal sac flows, inter-hole interactions, and plume-to-plume disturbances
alter the final morphology in ways single-hole models cannot capture a priori [50], [51].
Finally, executing these operations—background subtraction, local filtering, gradient
computation, angular binning, thresholding, morphological cleanup—repeatedly over
a terabyte-scale video archive is a dense-array, map-reduce workload well matched to
GPU SIMT parallelization [52].
    Two choices depart from the most literature-obvious alternative and are worth
justifying explicitly here. First, nozzle-center calibration is performed manually rather
than via automated circle detection (e.g., RANSAC or imfindcircles): because
the high-speed camera position is fixed and reused across the entire batch, a single
manual calibration can be inherited by every video, whereas automated detectors on
this dataset are prone to interference from glare, unclosed edges, and local occlusion
that manual verification avoids. Second, boundary and cone-angle estimation is
not defined by a single fixed brightness threshold at the leading pixel: Johnson et
al. showed that threshold-based cone-angle estimation from back-scattered diesel
images is highly sensitive to the chosen intensity threshold and proposed contour-based
alternatives to reduce this subjectivity [53], indicating that boundary metrics cannot
be defined independently of imaging modality and post-processing rules—motivating
the CDF-quantile boundary definition used in this thesis (Section 3.4.7).
    What none of these individually mature techniques supplies off the shelf is a
combined pipeline at this scale and structure: single-jet segmentation tools do not
stably register repeating plumes sharing one nozzle center, and no standard tool
chain jointly performs calibration, illumination suppression, boundary extraction,
and rotational registration over a terabyte-scale video archive under a reproducible,
auditable rule set. Chapter 3 (from Section 3.3 onward) details how this thesis
assembles these building blocks into that pipeline, including the tensorized CUDA
implementation (Section 3.4.9) motivated by the need to re-run the full chain cheaply
as calibration, target definitions, and model stages are iterated.


                                           21
2.4 Surrogate Modelling for Spray Penetration: Existing Prac-
    tice and Remaining Gaps
2.4.1   Why Deterministic, Uncertainty-Aware, and Teacher-Guided Learning
        Stages Are All Necessary

In recent years, data-driven surrogate models have started being used for the rapid
prediction of macroscopic spray characteristics. Existing research demonstrates that
when inputs are compressed into operating parameters, nozzle geometries, and a few
macroscopic descriptors, artificial neural networks can learn the mapping between
penetration, cone angles, other spray geometries, and operating conditions without
explicitly providing closed-form empirical laws. Based on high-speed Mie scattering
experimental data in a constant-volume spray chamber, Cheng et al. used artificial
neural networks to predict key geometric parameters of multi-hole nozzle sprays; their
data inherently included penetration, cone angle, spray tip velocity, area, as well as
plume-to-plume and shot-to-shot variations, thus it can be viewed as an experimental
ANN surrogate model closely aligned with the problem in this thesis [54]. Zhang
et al. subsequently constructed a GA-BP neural network using experimental diesel
spray penetration data and reported that its prediction accuracy surpassed traditional
empirical formulas [55]. In scenarios closer to marine injector design screening, Vaz
et al. further used neural networks to directly predict spray penetration and cone angle
from operating and geometric parameters, supporting rapid design space exploration
for marine injectors [22].
    Collectively, these studies show that compact regressors, multilayer feedforward
networks in particular, can already stand in as surrogates for spray macroscopic
quantities. What such a model must learn, after image processing, is not raw
video volumes or high-dimensional spatiotemporal texture but the low-dimensional
engineering features and temporal parameterizations extracted from it; the task is
therefore closer to low-dimensional nonlinear regression than to an end-to-end video-
understanding problem requiring recurrent, long-memory, or large-scale attention
mechanisms. Across these studies, however, the surrogate is used narrowly: it is a
deterministic point predictor of cleanly measured, fully observable targets, with neither
the right-censoring imposed by a finite field of view nor input-dependent uncertainty
treated as part of the problem.




                                           22
     How to build a reliable data-driven system, by contrast, is precisely the question
that large-scale machine-learning and foundation-model engineering has spent recent
years standardizing. Surveys of that field document a recurring, stage-structured
workflow rather than a single monolithic fit [24], [25]: reliability is engineered through
data-centric curation and quality filtering [56], deliberate target- and distillation-label
construction [57], [58], a staged pre-training–distillation–fine-tuning curriculum, held-
out and calibration-aware evaluation [59], and deployment-time adaptation through
lightweight adapters [60]. The scale (web-scale corpora, billions of parameters) and
the modality (natural-language text) are far removed from a diesel spray; what transfers
is the engineering discipline—that the trained model is only one stage among several,
and that data curation, target design, and calibration are first-class concerns rather than
afterthoughts. The parallel drawn here is methodological, not a claim of equivalence
in scale, data, or domain. Table 1 places the two workflows side by side.
Table 1: The six-stage spray-surrogate workflow of this thesis set beside the docu-
mented large-scale machine-learning engineering workflow it adapts. The correspon-
dence is methodological: the scale and modality differ, while the staged discipline is
shared.

         This thesis (spray surrogate)                 Large-scale ML / foundation-model en-
                                                       gineering
  1      Data extraction: CV + CUDA, Mie video         Web-scale corpus acquisition [61]
         → 1D penetration
  2      Censoring-aware curation; three derived       Data-centric curation and quality filtering
         data populations                              [56]
  3      𝐴-scaling feature; SGQR (sigmoid-gated        Target and distillation-label construction
         quarter-root) penetration targets             [57]
  4      Three-stage MLP with knowledge distilla-      Pre-training → distillation → fine-tuning
         tion                                          [24]
  5      Leave-one-nozzle-out and calibration          Held-out / out-of-distribution benchmarks
         checks                                        and calibration [59]
  6      Family-head / residual adapters and screen-   Serving with adapter (PEFT) fine-tuning
         ing GUI                                       [60]

     Consistent with this workflow view, the surrogate backbone is kept deliberately
modest. A multilayer perceptron (MLP) is adopted: it retains universal function-
approximation capability [62] while offering a practical compromise among training
cost, implementation complexity, and engineering auditability [63]. The choice is
matched to the experimental budget rather than being a mere preference for simple
models (ablations, group-aware holdout checks, leave-one-nozzle-out tests, and
multiple random seeds all require each training run to stay cheap enough to repeat),
and it deliberately makes the backbone the inexpensive, swappable stage, leaving
reliability to the surrounding curation, target-construction, and calibration stages.
An MLP over (x, 𝑡) also gives stable first- and second-order derivatives with respect
to continuous time through automatic differentiation, exactly the representation the
shape-constraint terms below require; recurrent, long-memory, or attention-based
sequence models could in principle be regularized for the same end, but they would
tie supervision more tightly to a chosen time grid and make arbitrary-time distillation
targets and derivative priors less direct.
                                           23
    Read together, the two literatures locate this thesis precisely: existing spray
surrogate work shows that a compact regressor can predict penetration, while the large-
scale machine-learning workflow shows what a dependable pipeline requires around
that regressor. Most spray surrogates, however, treat their targets as fully observable and
report only point accuracy: late-time truncation is rarely modelled as right-censoring,
input-dependent uncertainty is seldom a primary output target, and systematic late-time
under-prediction is largely undiscussed. The constraints in this thesis are stricter:
the visible trajectories of short-pulse pre-injections are dominated by SOI and EOI
transients, a restricted field of view renders a significant portion of trajectories as
right-censored samples, and plume-to-plume as well as shot-to-shot dispersions
are themselves engineering-relevant information that cannot be simply averaged
away. Thus, this thesis does not aim to re-prove that neural networks can predict
spray macroscopic quantities, but rather introduces three targeted components atop
existing pathways: censoring-aware target construction, heteroscedastic uncertainty
quantification, and a distillation-based refinement stage. In this sense, the gap is
not merely the absence of a more expressive neural architecture, but the absence
of a trajectory-level learning formulation that jointly accounts for optical right-
censoring, extrapolative continuation, and the aleatoric plume-to-plume and shot-
to-shot dispersion that remains relevant to engineering screening. The three stages
should therefore be read as a curriculum rather than as three unrelated training recipes.
Curriculum learning formalises the idea of first fitting an easier or more stable target
before exposing the model to the full, noisier learning problem [64]. For this thesis, the
deterministic Stage 1 mean fit is a stabilising step: training a heteroscedastic Gaussian
head from scratch can let the variance branch absorb mean errors, down-weight
difficult samples, and slow or destabilise the mean fit, a failure mode documented
in variance-network and 𝛽-NLL (negative log-likelihood, NLL) studies [65], [66].
Stage 1 therefore first anchors a representative conditional mean; Stage 2 then expands
to the full filtered reduced-order fit archive and learns input-dependent spread; Stage 3
finally reintroduces raw cumulative distribution function (CDF) observations only
where coverage makes direct supervision defensible.




                                            24
2.4.2   Right-Censoring: Why Existing Censored-Regression Tools Fall Short

Among the three, censoring-aware target construction is unavoidable due to the
geometric constraints of the experimental data used in this thesis. The choice of data
is dictated not merely by historical evolution, but jointly by the required observables
and hardware constraints. Under cold, non-evaporating conditions, Mie scattering
is sensitive to forward and side scattering of droplets and provides high contrast at
liquid-phase boundaries, thus matching best with the penetration and cone angle
observables extracted in this thesis; this has been discussed in the optical diagnostic
methods comparison above. More decisively, hardware-level constraints exist: both
Schlieren and DBI require a transmissive optical path through front and rear quartz
windows, restricting the configuration to a single-hole geometry (with other holes
physically plugged). Even if a full window diameter of 180 mm is achievable,
simultaneous observation of multiple plumes is structurally excluded. The top-view
Mie configuration adopted in this thesis enables all 𝑁 spray plumes (𝑁 = 9, 10, or 11,
depending on the nozzle) to be visible simultaneously in a single record; combined
with five repetitions per operating condition, each experiment provides 45, 50, or
55 penetration time series per condition, which is the sample scale necessary for
data-driven modeling of plume-to-plume variations. This configuration subsequently
brings a field-of-view tradeoff that must be explicitly acknowledged: an observation
window diameter of 180 mm (radius 90 mm) leaves an effective horizontal observation
penetration of roughly 80–85 mm after excluding the masked region near the nozzle;
even if an umbrella angle correction is applied to the acquired projected penetration to
recover the 3D axial path length (typical correction factor 1.05–1.2), the resulting
trajectory lengths are still less than the radial distance to the cylinder wall for the
smallest W20DF (bore 200 mm, radius 100 mm) among Wärtsilä’s in-service dual-fuel
diesel pre-injection products. Therefore, the right-censoring observed in this thesis
is not caused by a mismatch of experimental configuration on a specific engine, but
reflects a structural limitation of top-view diagnostics for real-scale engine cylinder
wall observations: the field of view imposes a ceiling below the wall distance, so
trajectories that approach the wall are systematically truncated (39.6% of trajectories
reach the cap, as audited in Chapter 4). This assertion applies to the scenario where
the spray reaches the cylinder wall/piston crown; impacts within the piston bowl are
outside this scope.
    The impact of a finite field of view is not merely cropping the image scope; it alters
the statistical properties of the labels. When the true late-stage penetration crosses
the visible window, the recorded trajectory does not merely contain additional noise,
but is right-censored by the observation boundary. This structure is well-discussed
in statistics: limited or censored dependent variable literature specifically focuses on
problems where observations serve as restricted representations of underlying true
quantities [67], [68]. Although this literature mostly stems from econometrics with a
different context from spray imaging, the core structure is identical: the dependent
variable of interest can only be observed below a certain threshold, and information
beyond the threshold is compressed or lost. In this thesis, this threshold is the camera’s
field-of-view boundary.


                                           25
Figure 1: An example of right-censoring in spray images. Some spray plumes have
crossed the upper boundary of the observation window, thus leaving only blurred
annular edge traces near the upper limit; other spray plumes have not yet exceeded the
observation boundary and remain fully displayed within the visible window.

    Figure 1 provides a representative example from the current workflow. Some
plumes have already crossed the upper boundary of the observation window, leaving
only blurred annular edge traces near the boundary; other plumes remain entirely
within the visible region. If interpreted purely based on surface image readings, the
"terminal penetration" of the former is actually determined by the camera’s field of
view, not by the spray’s physical processes.
    This distinction directly impacts surrogate model training. If the visible endpoints
of censored trajectories are treated as ordinary regression labels, the target values will be
systematically depressed in late-stage regions that should have continued growing. The
model might consequently be trained to interpret "leaving the field of view" as "physical
saturation." In a dataset where censored and uncensored trajectories are mixed, this
label construction produces unstable late-stage gradients and artificially flattens the
penetration curves. The problem here is not inadequate model expressiveness, but
biased supervision signals.

                                             26
     From a statistical modeling perspective, the Tobit model is the classical starting
point for handling censored dependent variables [67]. If the goal of this thesis were
merely to predict a censored scalar at a fixed time, Tobit-style regression would
naturally be a baseline worth prioritizing. However, this thesis deals not with a single
scalar, but with short-duration penetration trajectories jointly shaped by SOI, EOI, and
nozzle transients. For late-stage samples that have crossed the field-of-view boundary,
the Tobit likelihood primarily provides a one-sided constraint that "the true value is
higher than the censoring boundary," but cannot provide shape information on "how
the trajectory should continue to grow after crossing the boundary." When late-stage
uncensored samples are already scarce and conservative extrapolation is inevitable,
such gradients more easily help the model learn "whether it crossed the boundary,"
but are insufficient to stably reconstruct "to what extent it extends after crossing."
Given the data conditions of this thesis, this information structure is unsuitable as the
primary basis for late-stage trajectory reconstruction.
     The broader survival regression literature offers additional tools for censored
outcomes: Accelerated Failure Time (AFT) models parameterise the censored outcome
under distributional assumptions more flexible than the Gaussian Tobit; inverse
probability of censoring weighting (IPCW) approaches re-weight complete cases to
account for the censoring mechanism; and Gaussian process regression with censored
likelihoods has been developed for exactly this combination of GP modelling and
right-censored targets [69]. However, all of these handle censored scalar outcomes
at a fixed time, not censored trajectory shapes. The key challenge here is that “how
the trajectory continues after crossing the boundary” requires shape information that
one-sided likelihood constraints cannot provide; the teacher-guided stage described
below addresses this gap specifically by learning the trajectory continuation from
manually extrapolated synthetic data. That synthetic continuation is not treated as a
hidden ground truth. It is a conservative bridge between two known-biased alternatives:
raw field-of-view-capped endpoints underestimate the continuation, while imposing a
rigid Hiroyasu–Arai-style law can overestimate late short-pulse penetration because it
does not represent the delayed-clock post-EOI deceleration branch [13], [21]. The
reduced-order fit used later therefore inherits empirical penetration scaling, but limits
its role to a smooth and differentiable continuation prior with explicit filtering and
residual diagnostics. Stage 3 then pulls the student back toward raw CDF observations
in the well-covered intervals, so the teacher serves as a censoring-aware anchor rather
than an oracle replacing the measurements.
     The motivation for uncertainty-aware extensions is that not every operating point
or extracted trajectory holds the same reliability. Heteroscedastic Gaussian negative
log-likelihood is a standard approach, allowing the network to simultaneously predict
the mean and an input-dependent variance [70]. In regression form, it corresponds to
the conditional model

                 𝑦 = 𝜇(𝑥, 𝑡) + 𝜀(𝑥, 𝑡),      𝜀(𝑥, 𝑡) ∼ N (0, 𝜎 2 (𝑥, 𝑡)),




                                           27
where the Gaussian distribution should be understood as a practical statistical assump-
tion rather than a claim that spray physics truly obeys a Gaussian distribution at every
instant [63], [70]. What this thesis truly needs is a way to express the synthetic residual
of unresolved factors and separate this residual from the conditional mean trajectory;
otherwise, the model would be forced to squeeze all inter-plume and cycle-to-cycle
variations into a single mean function.
    However, relying solely on heteroscedastic regression does not completely eliminate
the systematic underestimation caused by right-censoring. If raw partially observed
trajectories are directly used for supervision, even if the network can output variance, its
late-stage mean might still be pulled down by the field-of-view boundary. Therefore,
this thesis further adopts a teacher-guided distillation-style refinement: First, a
heteroscedastic teacher model is trained on the aforementioned "manually fitted and
conservatively extrapolated" synthetic trajectories, causing it to learn a censoring-
aware late-stage continuation; then, this teacher model is used to constrain a student
model oriented toward raw CDF observations. In this framework, reliable early- and
mid-stage raw observations retain their direct supervisory role, while late-stage sparse
or already censored intervals rely more on the soft targets provided by the teacher.
In other words, distillation in this thesis is not a model compression technique in the
general sense, but a bias correction mechanism that re-projects the censoring-aware
synthetic data back into the raw observation domain.
    Gaussian outputs also offer a practical advantage here: when both the teacher and
student output univariate Gaussian distributions, the Kullback–Leibler divergence
between them has an analytical closed-form solution [63], [70], so a distillation loss
built from it can be evaluated exactly, without Monte Carlo sampling or the extra
gradient variance sampling would introduce. The direction of that divergence, however,
is not a free choice: this thesis initially adopted the forward KL direction from teacher
to student for its algebraic consistency with the Stage 2 NLL objective, but found
that it opens a subtle optimisation loophole under the mean mismatch this censored
setting produces, letting the student discount uncorrected mean error by inflating its
own predicted variance rather than correcting the mean. Chapter 4 (Section 4.5.3)
derives this failure mode in closed form and details the resulting mean-squared-error-
based loss that removes the shortcut. Kang and Kang (2024) showed specifically
for regression tasks that teacher-generated soft targets substantially reduce student
errors in under-labelled regions [71], which provides direct empirical support for the
distillation design adopted here. Note that Tarvainen and Valpola’s mean-teacher
mechanism operates via an exponential moving average of the student’s own weights
[72], whereas this thesis uses a separately pretrained and frozen teacher model; the
shared principle is teacher-guided soft supervision, not the specific EMA update rule.
The broader knowledge distillation literature (Hinton et al. 2015 for classification
settings [73]) established that teacher-guided training can improve student performance
in data-sparse regimes; the present regression extension for censored trajectory labels
follows the same structural principle.
    The resulting learning basis binds right-censoring-aware data construction, het-
eroscedastic regression for input-dependent uncertainty, and distillation-style bias
correction based on a manually fitted and extrapolated teacher into a unified framework.

                                            28
    A related but distinct problem sits at the opposite end of the trajectory: near the
start of injection, the observed CDF penetration front is dominated by threshold and
registration noise rather than genuine spray growth, so a continuous mean/variance
head alone gives no direct, interpretable signal for when the trajectory should be treated
as having begun. The production Stage 3 student therefore adds a third, auxiliary
onset output alongside (𝜇, 𝜎): a binary-cross-entropy-supervised logit that ramps
from 0 to 1 over a short window after the nominal start of injection (Chapter 4). This
onset side-branch leaves the penetration mean/variance prediction itself unchanged; it
exists to give downstream consumers, such as the screening interface of Section 4.8, a
discrete onset-confidence signal that the continuous heads do not directly provide.

2.4.3   Physics-Informed Shape Constraints: Precedent and Applicability

Beyond right-censoring and uncertainty, the surrogate model must also satisfy fun-
damental penetration shape constraints. Purely data-driven smooth MLPs generally
yield reasonable interpolations in densely sampled regions; but in locally sparse or
lightly extrapolated areas of the operating space, the model may exhibit non-physical
oscillations, localized regressions, or sharp gradients. Such locally weakly supported
regions, for instance, specific combinations of nozzle hole diameters, injection pres-
sures, and durations, do exist in the test matrix of this thesis. Therefore, relying solely
on data losses is insufficient to guarantee that trajectory shapes remain physically
credible at all times.
    Encoding physical priors into neural network training objectives is a representative
paradigm in the Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) literature [74]. The
core mechanism of Raissi et al.’s work lies in using Automatic Differentiation to
compute partial derivatives of network outputs with respect to inputs, and adding
these derivative-related terms as soft penalties to the loss function. In this way, partial
differential equations or empirical physical laws can serve as constraints, guiding the
network to stay within a reasonable function space even in data-sparse regions.
    This thesis does not attempt to use neural networks to solve full Navier–Stokes
equations. Rather, it borrows the constraint concept of PINNs: first- and second-order
temporal derivatives of the penetration mean are obtained via automatic differentiation,
and corresponding soft penalties are added to the loss. The first-order derivative
constraint is used to suppress unphysical negative velocities, while the second-order
derivative constraint is applied as a gated soft penalty that encourages concavity
only after the initial acceleration-dominated transient, including the later injection
and post-injection deceleration phases, meaning the penetration growth rate decays
with time. This also explains why smooth activation functions like Swish/SiLU
mentioned earlier are necessary: only when the second-order derivatives can be stably
and smoothly propagated is a curvature-based physical shape constraint suitable for
gradient descent optimization.




                                            29
2.4.4   Evaluating Probabilistic Predictions: Proper Scoring Rules and Model
        Calibration

Having the network output both mean and variance does not automatically imply
that its uncertainty is reliable. The evaluation criteria for probabilistic predictions
differ from deterministic regression: Mean Squared Error or Mean Absolute Error
primarily evaluates point predictions, whereas the predictive distribution must be
tested for both accuracy and spread simultaneously. Therefore, evaluating probabilistic
models requires the use of Strictly Proper Scoring Rules. Gneiting and Raftery’s work
showed that a proper scoring rule attains its expected optimum only when the predicted
distribution aligns with the true conditional distribution [75]. The Continuous Ranked
Probability Score (CRPS) is a commonly used metric of this kind: it simultaneously
penalizes mean bias and overly broad predictive distributions, forcing the model to be
accurate while maintaining appropriate sharpness.
    Moreover, modern deep networks can exhibit overconfidence even if their point
prediction accuracy is high: the nominal probability of a prediction interval does
not necessarily equal the empirical frequency of true observations falling within that
interval. Guo et al. systematically diagnosed this phenomenon and emphasized the
necessity of post hoc probability calibration and empirical coverage checks [76]. In
the context of this thesis, a simply trained heteroscedastic network might yield overly
small variances under certain conditions, causing the actual observations to fall outside
the prediction intervals more frequently than the nominal level indicates. Therefore, a
surrogate model aimed at early design screening must verify uncertainty calibration
on a hold-out set; for out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization, empirical coverage
(e.g., 1𝜎 and 2𝜎 coverage) should also be checked via spatial cross-validation, such
as holding out complete hardware families. This constitutes the basis for adopting
CRPS, reliability diagrams, and Leave-One-Nozzle-Out (LONO) diagnostics in the
subsequent results evaluation.




                                           30
2.4.5   Differences Between Kernel Methods Baselines and Neural Surrogates
        Outside the Distribution

Choosing between kernel method baselines, such as Sparse Variational Gaussian
Process (SVGP), and neural surrogates is not merely a matter of capacity or imple-
mentation cost; the two types of models inherently carry different inductive biases,
and these differences only become apparent when test inputs deviate from the training
support. A Gaussian process employing an ARD Matérn kernel and inducing point
variational posteriors is essentially a Bayesian model in function space: the kernel
function explicitly encodes a smoothness prior, and the predictive mean will smoothly
revert to the prior mean as features move away from the training inducing points,
while the predictive variance increases in the same direction [39], [40]. Thus, the
out-of-distribution behavior is conservative by default—not because the model is
meticulously tuned for it, but because wherever the data cannot constrain it, the prior
naturally dominates. Neural surrogates have no equivalent fallback mechanism: a
trained network is a deterministic nonlinear extrapolator, whose values outside the
distribution are governed jointly by the learned function geometry and the shape or
anchor priors added during training. From the perspective of Bayesian Deep Learning,
Wilson and Izmailov point out that this is a general disparity rather than an architectural
quirk: kernel methods naturally achieve mild generalization via their priors, whereas
neural networks require explicit calibration to approximate similar behaviors [77].
Large-scale empirical studies by Ovadia et al. confirmed the practical consequences
under distribution shifts: deep models, including heteroscedastic forms, often exhibit
overconfidence or inherit the biases of the training distribution when the test distri-
bution shifts, whereas probabilistic baselines lose accuracy much more gracefully
[78]. For this thesis, these references serve merely as general background rather
than core arguments, as the contribution of this thesis lies in right-censoring-aware
learning protocols and screening interfaces, not in the choice of regressors itself. The
significance is methodological: the Leave-One-Nozzle-Out evaluation in Chapter 5
should not be read as an architectural showdown with only one winner. When the
held-out nozzle indeed falls outside the training support, the kernel method baseline
will be more conservative a priori, while the phased MLP will more aggressively fit
the structure of the training distribution and carry this structure to the hold-out folds.
Therefore, the empirical results in Chapter 5 are a consistent manifestation of this
"prior versus likelihood" separation, not an accident requiring post hoc explanation,
and the thesis presents them in this spirit.




                                            31
2.5 Spray Wall Impingement Mechanisms and Probabilistic Im-
    pingement Risk Screening
Penetration control of diesel pre-injection sprays is primarily an issue of combustion
organization, not just a problem of impingement avoidance. In dual-fuel diesel-gas
engines, the geometric distribution of the pre-injection spray—including penetration,
cone angle, and the extent of liquid-phase spread—directly determines the spatial
position and size of the ignition kernel, and thereby affects whether the flame can
efficiently propagate into the premixed main fuel gas charge [7], [8], [9]. If the
pre-injection penetration is too short, the ignition kernel position deviates from the
appropriate zone or its volume is insufficient, which may lead to inadequate main
fuel flame propagation, elevated unburned hydrocarbons (e.g., methane slip), and a
corresponding drop in overall combustion efficiency; If the pre-injection penetration is
too long, even if definitive impingement has not yet occurred, it may alter the relative
position of the ignition kernel to the gas charge, leading to incomplete combustion of
the main fuel and increasing the likelihood of liquid-phase wall impingement [8], [9].
In other words, pre-injection penetration is foremost a key control variable for main
fuel mixing, ignition kernel placement, and combustion efficiency; on top of this, it
is also the core geometric input for wall-impingement risk screening. Predictions of
pre-injection penetration and its uncertainty in this thesis thus primarily serve the
evaluation of pre-injection control and combustion organization, while concurrently
supporting wall risk ranking.
    In large-bore medium-speed dual-fuel engines such as the Wärtsilä W20DF (bore
200 mm), this combustion organisation challenge takes a specific form. At pilot
injection timing, the premixed methane–air charge is largely homogeneous throughout
the cylinder volume rather than stratified; the pilot spray therefore does not ignite
into a locally enriched mixture but must establish an ignition kernel that subsequently
propagates through a uniform lean charge. Penetration in this context primarily
controls where in the homogeneous charge the kernel forms: if penetration is too short,
the kernel may be confined close to the nozzle tip and fail to grow rapidly enough
to consume the bulk cylinder charge within the available crank-angle window. In
large-bore 4-stroke engines, methane slip is closely associated with the gas exchange
phase—unburned methane escapes through the exhaust valve during the valve-overlap
period rather than being retained in quench zones as in smaller automotive engines.
Karmann et al. demonstrated that pilot spray penetration and breakup characteristics
in a large-bore dual-fuel engine (MAN 35/44DF, medium-speed 4-stroke) differ
substantially from automotive-scale predictions, confirming that penetration models
calibrated on small-nozzle data cannot be directly applied at marine-engine scale [79].
Zhang et al. showed that a 2 ◦ CA advance in pilot injection timing measurably changes
combustion efficiency and methane slip for a marine natural gas/diesel dual-fuel engine,
illustrating how sensitive the combustion organisation is to pilot spray geometric
control [80].




                                          32
     When this spatial control fails, the contact between liquid spray and engine walls
becomes a consequence that must be addressed. If liquid fuel reaches the piston crown
or cylinder wall before evaporating or igniting, a fuel film may form on the wall,
leading to wall wetting, localized mixing deterioration, elevated unburned hydrocarbon
emissions, and lube oil dilution [10], [11], [12]. This issue cannot be understood purely
from the micro-physical perspective of single-droplet impacts. Moreira et al. note in
their review that while single droplet impact studies have indeed accumulated a wealth
of fundamental knowledge, directly extrapolating it to continuous spray impingement
is unsafe, because the collective behavior of a spray plume is further modulated by
adjacent droplet interactions, residual wall films, and secondary atomization [12].
Thus, moving from a single droplet to a plume is not a simple smooth interpolation,
but involves real-scale and mechanistic gaps. At the marine large-bore scale, the
problem becomes further complicated: spray development scales cannot be obtained
by simple proportional enlargement, and optical studies on large marine nozzles also
indicate that wall constraints significantly alter ignition delays and natural luminous
flame intensities [81].
     In engineering, the most direct screening rule is relatively clear: compare the
liquid tip penetration 𝑆tip (𝑡) with the distance from the nozzle to the wall 𝐿 wall ; when
𝑆tip (𝑡) ≥ 𝐿 wall , it is flagged as an impingement [15]. In Baumgarten’s systematic
treatise on mixture formation in internal combustion engines, penetration correlations
are treated as baseline tools for simplifying spray trajectories, and the comparison of
penetration with wall distance serves as the first-order impingement criterion [15]. It
is worth noting that subsequent research has not discarded this geometric core but has
appended more information to it. Targeting high-pressure gasoline direct injection,
Park et al. reconstructed the radial fuel mass distribution from spray images, and
combined it with injection timing to estimate the mass fraction of fuel impinging on the
piston and cylinder wall; their trend conclusion—that impingement quantity decreases
as injection pressure rises and atomization improves—is not surprising in itself;
what is more valuable is its methodological implication: image analysis can provide
quantitative impingement estimations without running full CFD [82]. Under the ECN
framework, Peraza et al. used a single-hole nozzle and a temperature-controlled flat
wall system to measure spray impingement, characterizing how the penetration-wall
distance relationship varies with ambient density and temperature [10]. These works
collectively demonstrate that: no matter how measurement details evolve, penetration
remains the most critical geometric input in impingement screening.




                                            33
     However, for short-pulse pre-injections, treating a deterministic threshold as the
primary screening tool is too fragile. Penetration itself carries uncertainties stemming
from needle valve transients, cycle-to-cycle momentum fluctuations, and residual
optical measurement noise; the SOI/EOI-dominated stages also make steady-state
correlations less reliable over large segments of the observation window. If a quantity
that should be treated as a distribution is compressed into a single value, and then
subjected to a binary pass/fail judgment, information useful for ranking candidate
operating conditions is concealed. The approach taken in this thesis represents
penetration as a conditional Gaussian distribution 𝑆tip ∼ N (𝜇(𝑥, 𝑡), 𝜎 2 (𝑥, 𝑡)), and
projects this distribution onto a 2D engine geometry plane with the nozzle center as the
origin. Under the premise that lateral expansion is controlled by the spray half-cone
angle and approximated by a normal distribution, the probability density of the spray
front can be written as a rotated 2D Gaussian. With this simplified geometry, the
wall-impingement risk score can be expressed as the integral of the probability mass
falling within the geometric mask regions of the wall and piston. This output should
be understood as a probabilistic screening proxy metric intended for early design
ranking, rather than a fully validated production-grade impingement prediction. The
detailed cone-width convention, piston-mask parameterization, and piston-kinematic
assumptions are therefore deferred to the screening interface in Section 4.8, rather
than expanded in this literature-review chapter.
     The intended engineering application context for this screening output encompasses
several complementary use cases:

    • Nozzle geometry pre-screening: ranking candidate nozzles (varying hole diam-
      eter, count, and umbrella angle) by impingement probability before committing
      to prototype manufacture;

    • Injection timing sweep: evaluating how the risk score varies with pilot start-of-
      injection timing for a given nozzle, without requiring a separate test campaign
      for each timing point;

    • Ambient condition sensitivity: estimating how changes in back pressure or
      charge density (e.g. from alternative fuel blends or different engine load points)
      shift the impingement risk margin for an existing nozzle design;

    • Operational margin quantification: expressing the safety margin between
      the 𝑃90 penetration envelope and the wall geometry to support pre-injection
      strategy decisions and fleet-level parameter monitoring.




                                          34
In each case the surrogate replaces or reduces the number of physical spray-chamber
experiments or full CFD runs required at the pre-screening stage, providing a fast
probabilistic ranking that directs resource-intensive methods toward the most promising
candidates.
     Table 2 summarizes how the aforementioned literature threads support the design
responses in this thesis. One structural consequence threads through every response
listed in the table. The top-view multi-plume configuration was chosen because it
preserves the plume-to-plume and shot-to-shot variation that data-driven modelling
requires; the same choice caps the observable trajectory below the nozzle-to-wall
distance of the target engine geometry (W20DF bore 200 mm, nozzle-to-wall radius
≈100 mm), producing a structural right-censoring mechanism quantified in Chapter 4
(Section 4.2.1: 39.6% of trajectories reach the cap). This single consequence is
why a rigid empirical penetration law is treated as a baseline rather than a target,
why censoring-aware statistics replace ordinary regression, and why the screening
output in Section 2.5 is a distribution rather than a deterministic pass/fail threshold.
The remaining chapters treat this chain—experimental configuration → structural
censoring → biased labels → risk-ranking distortion—as the unifying constraint
behind the data, training, and evaluation choices detailed there. The remaining gap
lies in the fact that these threads are scattered from one another: optical diagnostics,
transient penetration theories, multi-plume registration, uncertainty-aware learning,
shape constraints, and impingement screening each resolve a part of the problem,
yet fail to form an assembled workflow suitable for the current archived data. The
contribution of this thesis is not just touching upon these issues separately, but
treating them as design constraints for a unified workflow. This integrated scheme
is subsequently validated collectively by the specific workflows, model results, and
screening prototypes.
