                                               36
3 Experimental Data and Image Processing Work-
  flow
3.1    Workflow Overview
Figure 2 illustrates the overall data flow of the surrogate modeling workflow used
in this thesis, organized into four swimlanes. Raw inputs (high-speed Mie videos,
calibration configurations, and test metadata) first undergo CUDA-accelerated image
processing to generate 1D features and boundary points; based on this, binary-
thresholded (BW) and CDF-based penetration trajectories along with other geometric
metrics are obtained. These observables are expanded into synthetic trajectories via
non-parametric curve fitting and are used to drive a three-stage neural network training:
Stage 1 (MSE), Stage 2 (NLL), and Stage 3 (Raw + KD, knowledge distillation). The
ultimately trained model is deployed in a wall impingement screening application,
which outputs impingement probabilities and visualization animations in combination
with piston geometry and physical parameters. Table 3 summarizes these same stages
in textual form. This chapter focuses on the first two swimlanes of Figure 2: how
the Optical Spray Combustion Chamber (OSCC) video data undergoes calibration,
preprocessing, registration, and conversion into plume-resolved geometric observables.
The construction of trajectory targets, surrogate modeling training, and probabilistic
screening are deferred to the next chapter.
    Raw per-frame boundary extraction alone cannot supply the surrogate of Chapter 4
with what it needs: a fixed-cardinality, censoring-flagged, plume-resolved time series
that is comparable across nozzles with different hole counts and frame rates. This
chapter therefore treats image processing as an auditable methodological stage rather
than an ancillary preprocessing step; its five methodological elements (listed below)
are what convert a discrete, event-by-event observation table into that structured
target. Existing spray-image analyses often report penetration, cone angle, or boundary
descriptors for individual operating conditions. In contrast, the surrogate models
in this thesis require consistent, plume-resolved, and censoring-aware time-series
targets across many nozzle geometries and operating conditions. This chapter therefore
addresses the target-construction gap between raw optical spray recordings and
machine-learning-ready observables.




                                           37
    These tasks are performed based on data from the Wärtsilä OSCC. The raw
materials are terabytes of room-temperature, non-reacting, top-view Mie scattering
recordings, acquired across multiple operating conditions and nozzle geometries under
a fixed camera setup. The pipeline first manually calibrates the nozzle geometry,
then generates plume-resolved observables via CUDA-accelerated preprocessing,
polar registration, and plume strip extraction, and constructs penetration targets
through reduced-order fitting, which are subsequently passed to the three-stage MLP
surrogate described in Chapter 4. At the implementation level, this image-processing
stage is built as a four-lane overlapped pipeline governed by a resumable checkpoint
contract, in which disk prefetch, GPU compute, central processing unit (CPU) feature
extraction, and asynchronous output run concurrently, so that the multi-terabyte archive
is reprocessed end-to-end on a single workstation GPU rather than through serial
frame-by-frame processing (Section 3.4.9).
Contributions of this chapter. This chapter contributes five reusable methodological
elements to the image-to-observable pipeline:

   1. a log-ratio (homomorphic) foreground estimator that treats glare and non-
      uniform illumination as multiplicative effects and removes them as additive
      offsets in log space (Section 3.4.1);

   2. a rank-1 separable Sobel edge-enhancement stage kept entirely on the GPU as
      two one-dimensional passes (Section 3.4.2);

   3. an FFT harmonic-phase alignment that converts the known multi-hole count
      into an automatic, per-recording plume registration rule rather than a hand-set
      reference angle (Section 3.4.4);

   4. a cumulative-distribution (CDF) penetration front that is robust to fragmented
      leading-edge pixels while remaining consistent with standard diesel-spray
      penetration conventions (Section 3.4.7);

   5. a four-lane overlapped GPU pipeline with a resumable checkpoint contract that
      reprocesses the multi-terabyte archive end-to-end on a single workstation GPU
      (Section 3.4.9).




                                          38
Figure 2: Overview of the surrogate modeling data flow. The four swimlanes
correspond to raw inputs, image processing, data augmentation and deep learning, and
model deployment; arrows denote key data transfer and training connections (Median
Filtering, Warm Start, Warm Start + Distillation, Supervised Fine-Tuning).


3.2    Experimental Datasets
The input data originate from a pressurized OSCC operated at the Wärtsilä test facility.
A total of nine nozzles were tested: eight prototype nozzles (Nozzle1–Nozzle8)
recorded between October and November 2024, and one reference injector (Nozzle0)
recorded in June 2022. Table 4 summarizes the geometry of each nozzle and the range
of operating conditions covered, and Figure 3 visualizes the geometric design space
spanned by these nozzles.
    Across these nine nozzles, the injection pressure ranges from 1,400 to 2,200 bar,
the commanded injection duration from 340 to 1,200 µs, and the chamber pressure
from 4.42 to 35 bar. Each operating condition subfolder typically contains five repeated
recordings, and each recording is cropped to approximately 100 frames to limit GPU
memory footprint. At the camera frame rates of 25,000 and 34,000 frames per second
(FPS) used in this study, 100 frames define a physical time window of 4.0 ms and
2.94 ms respectively, which is sufficient to capture the pilot injection and its early
relaxation tail. The processed archive totals 266 test condition subfolders and 2,992
recording files (one CSV per .cine); the cropped working archive occupies tens of
GB, while the full uncropped recordings constitute terabytes of raw input.

3.3    Manual Calibration of Nozzle Geometry
Manual calibration fixes the nozzle center, calibration radius, and angular reference
used by the subsequent batch processing. The operator selects visible points on a
circular nozzle feature in a representative frame. Mathematically, these selected nozzle
edge points (𝑥𝑖 , 𝑦𝑖 ) are fitted with an algebraic circle model:

                              𝑥𝑖2 + 𝑦𝑖2 + 𝐷𝑥𝑖 + 𝐸 𝑦𝑖 + 𝐹 = 0,

which is solved via least squares using standard linear solvers (e.g., np.linalg.lstsq)
[42], [43]. The recovered circle parameters are:
                                                         √︃
                           𝐷                𝐸
                   𝑐𝑥 = − ,         𝑐𝑦 = − ,        𝑟 = 𝑐2𝑥 + 𝑐2𝑦 − 𝐹,
                            2               2
where (𝑐 𝑥 , 𝑐 𝑦 ) is the nozzle center and 𝑟 is the fitted radius. The same fit estimates
an average angular offset based on the deviation of the selected edge points from an
ideal equally spaced set. This ideal spacing is used as a nominal injector-geometry
reference for anchoring plume-wise coordinates; it does not assert that the observed
liquid-phase Mie envelopes remain symmetric or equally spaced after injection.

    The calibration values are saved to a configuration file, after which the batch pipeline
can process the entire folder without manual recentering. The minimal config.json
schema is {plumes, centre_x, centre_y, inner_radius, outer_radius}; the
two radii jointly define the annular processing mask, the local orifice origins, and the
downstream strip geometry.

           (a) Point selection                           (b) Calibration overlay
Figure 4: Nozzle geometry calibration tool. (a) The operator marks visible nozzle-
edge points on a representative frame for the algebraic circle fit; (b) the fitted center,
plume guide lines, and annular processing mask are overlaid on the recorded spray
pattern to verify the geometric anchor before batch processing.

    The optical path (lens focal length, working distance, and sensor alignment) was
configured by the experimental team following standard high-speed imaging practice for
the vessel geometry used in this campaign; no formal lens-distortion characterization
(e.g. a checkerboard intrinsic calibration) was performed or is available for this archive.
Because the axial measurement region of interest (< 90 mm) is small relative to the
camera-to-plume working distance, the resulting out-of-plane parallax from the 3D
cone expansion of the spray is expected to be a second-order effect relative to the fixed
pixel-to-millimeter scale calibrated above; quantitative validation of this assumption
is deferred to the downstream LONO consistency checks of Chapter 5.
    This design suits the current dataset because the camera and injector geometries
remain fixed within a single recording, and several artifacts concentric with the nozzle
in the background can serve as auxiliary references. Calibrating only once and
reusing the same center for all frames avoids inter-frame jitter during downstream
plume angle analysis. This one-time calibration
                                            42      is deliberately kept as a user-visible
operation rather than fully automated: because camera and injector geometry are
fixed for the duration of a recording campaign, a single manually verified calibration
Figure 5: Overview of the multi-hole top-view Mie scattering processing pipeline,
from raw .cine recordings, through calibration, CUDA-accelerated preprocessing,
plume registration, and penetration extraction, to CSV export.


3.4    Batch Video Processing and Plume Extraction
For each recording, the processing chain answers five practical measurement questions,
each taken up by a dedicated subsection below: where the nozzle center is (Section 3.3),
where each plume direction lies (Section 3.4.4), how each plume can be represented
in a common local coordinate system (Section 3.4.6 and the affine remapping that
precedes it), how far the liquid-phase front has penetrated (Section 3.4.7), and which
boundary descriptors can be extracted consistently (Section 3.4.8).
    For each .cine recording, the processing workflow loads the video into the
current array backend, reuses the annular mask for the entire folder, applies the
multi-hole preprocessing and postprocessing steps, extracts plume-wise penetration
curves, computes boundary-based geometric metrics, and exports synchronized CSV
and metadata files. The most computationally expensive parts of this chain are
kept strictly local to the backend: when CuPy is available, convolutions, angular
binning, remapping, thresholding, connected component cleaning, and morphological
operations are all executed along the CUDA path until the final tabular export.



                                          43
    This implementation choice stems from practical limitations in early internal
workflows. The legacy pipeline relied on frame-by-frame MATLAB processing and
lacked effective batch parallelism: processing ∼50 GB of videos typically took around
a day and was prone to crashing. Therefore, migrating heavy image operations to a
batch CUDA-oriented pipeline is considered a methodological design in this thesis
rather than merely a software optimization. All algorithmic stages described in the
following subsections run inside this batch pipeline; the concurrency design that lets
them execute end-to-end over the full archive on a single GPU, a four-lane overlapped
execution model governed by a resumable checkpoint, is detailed in Section 3.4.9,
after the individual operations it schedules have been introduced.

3.4.1   Background Normalization and Foreground Extraction

The first algorithmic contribution in the batch pipeline is a foreground construction
stage designed for noisy Mie videos, rather than schemes aimed at ideal binary
images. The goal is to estimate a stable relative foreground contrast under non-uniform
illumination before any geometric measurement is made. In the log-ratio foreground
extraction step, the raw frame 𝑉𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) is mapped to

        𝐿 𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) = log(𝑉𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) + 𝜀),     𝐿 bkg (𝑥, 𝑦) = median𝑡<𝑡SOI 𝐿 𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦),

followed by a log-ratio reconstruction:
                          (︁                        )︁     𝑉𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) − 𝐵(𝑥, 𝑦)
           𝑅𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) = exp 𝐿 𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) − 𝐿 bkg (𝑥, 𝑦) − 1 =                     .
                                                               𝐵(𝑥, 𝑦) + 𝜀

The next step multiplies 𝑅𝑡 by an intensity gate 𝑀𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) = 1[𝑉𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) > 𝛼𝐵(𝑥, 𝑦)],
subtracts the pre-injection baseline, and applies a soft threshold

                 𝐹𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) = max(𝑀𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦)𝑅𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) − 𝑅0 (𝑥, 𝑦) − 𝜏, 0) ,

where 𝜀 is a small constant to prevent log-zero, 𝑡SOI is the start of injection, 𝐵(𝑥, 𝑦) =
exp(𝐿 bkg (𝑥, 𝑦)) − 𝜀 is the linear-scale background, 𝛼 is the contrast multiplier for the
intensity gate, 𝑅0 (𝑥, 𝑦) is the residual pre-injection baseline, and 𝜏 is a soft threshold
used to suppress background noise. The result is then rescaled with robust quantiles
before entering the geometric processing. The logarithmic formulation acts as a
homomorphic ratio filter: spatial glare, vignetting, and background illumination are
treated as multiplicative image effects and are converted into additive terms in log
space. This sequence is critical for Mie videos, as the raw intensity is not a direct
measure of liquid volume fraction; the actual goal is to stably extract geometry under
non-uniform illumination, glare, and background textures, rather than performing a
rigorous optical inversion [16], [26], [45].

    Fixed Preprocessing Parameters. The intensity-gate, threshold, and rescaling
parameters introduced above are held fixed across the batch archive rather than tuned
per recording; Table 5 lists the values used in the batch processing.
    The necessity of this robust preprocessing is visually apparent, but the display
enhancement itself does not enter the quantitative chain. Figure 6 compares the
appearance of the same spray frame under two display gains, with other GUI parameters
held constant. At a lower gain, the plume core is relatively compact; at a higher gain,
a bright halo diffuses along the spray core, occupying a much larger image region.
This example does not dictate which display gain is physically more truthful, but
illustrates that if gamma-corrected, display-gain-adjusted, or locally contrast-enhanced
frames were used for boundary extraction, the boundaries would shift with the display
mapping [34]. Thus, these views are strictly for manual inspection. Quantitative
computations use a robust background estimation from pre-injection statistics, and
convert the representation into an edge-dominant Sobel feature space, ensuring the
geometric response is dictated by fixed operators and local boundary structures rather
than display brightness [16], [26], [45].

3.4.2   Gradient-Based Edge Enhancement

The second contribution is an explicit edge-enhancing high-pass stage acting directly
on the entire video cube. The physical purpose is to emphasize local liquid-phase
boundary contrast before plume-wise segmentation. The Sobel kernels 𝐾𝑥 and 𝐾 𝑦 are
generated exactly once and applied via 2D convolution:
                                  √︃
                                                 (︁       )︁ 2
                       𝑆𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) = (𝐾𝑥 ∗ 𝐹𝑡 ) 2 + 𝐾 𝑦 ∗ 𝐹𝑡 ,

Figure 6: The same spray frame displayed in the interactive calibration GUI with
gains of 1.0 and 7.0, with other display parameters held constant. The higher-gain view
accentuates the bright halo around the spray, demonstrating that display mappings
alter visual boundary perception; these gain views are for manual inspection only and
do not serve as inputs for quantitative boundary extraction.

where 𝐾𝑥 and 𝐾 𝑦 are the horizontal and vertical Sobel convolution kernels, ∗ denotes
the 2D convolution operator, 𝐹𝑡 is the preprocessed foreground image, and 𝑆𝑡 is the
resulting gradient magnitude.
    To ensure computational efficiency during batch processing, the 2D convolutions
with 𝐾𝑥 and 𝐾 𝑦 are factored into sequential 1D convolutions. Since the directional
Sobel kernels are rank-1 matrices, they can be represented as outer products of 1D
operators:
                          𝐾 𝑥 = g ⊗ d𝑔 ,     𝐾 𝑦 = d𝑔 ⊗ g,
where g is a discrete Gaussian envelope and d𝑔 is its derivative. Factoring each 2D
convolution into two sequential 1D convolutions reduces the per-pixel multiply-add
complexity for a 𝑘 × 𝑘 kernel from 𝑂 (𝑘 2 ) to 𝑂 (𝑘). In the GPU backend, these
operations are dispatched via CuPy (cupyx.scipy.ndimage) to execute the two 1D
passes efficiently on device arrays without custom tiling overhead.




                                           46
              Foreground                                     Sobel magnitude
        (a)                                            (b)




Figure 8: Representative frame visualizations of the edge-enhancing high-pass stage.
(a) Foreground after background subtraction; (b) Sobel gradient magnitude.


    This is followed by another robust rescaling and the application of an annular
geometric mask. This step is critical at the implementation level because it transforms
the low-contrast grayscale spray into an edge-dominant representation, while keeping
the entire operation on the CuPy backend. The contribution here lies not in the Sobel
operator itself, but in its integration into a batch video workflow that avoids repeated
host-device copies, preparing a consistent downstream geometric processing for each
plume [45], [46].

3.4.3    Plume Direction Estimation from Angular Intensity Profiles

After preprocessing, the pipeline abandons contour tracking in Cartesian coordinates
for finding plume directions. Instead, the processed image is reparameterized into
polar coordinates around the calibrated nozzle center. For each pixel,

                     𝜃 (𝑥, 𝑦) = atan2(𝑦 − 𝑐 𝑦 , 𝑥 − 𝑐 𝑥 ) mod 360◦ ,

and the angular signal in bin 𝑘 for each frame is accumulated as
                                 ∑︁                                𝐴𝑡 (𝑘)
                    𝐴𝑡 (𝑘) =              𝐼𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦),   𝐷 𝑡 (𝑘) =          ,
                                                                   #B𝑘
                               (𝑥,𝑦)∈B𝑘

where B𝑘 is the set of pixels falling into angle bin 𝑘, 𝐼𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) = 𝐹𝑡 (𝑥, 𝑦) is the
background-subtracted foreground intensity (defined in Section 3.4.1), 𝐴𝑡 (𝑘) is the
accumulated angular signal, 𝐷 𝑡 (𝑘) is the average angular density, and #B𝑘 is the
pixel count in that bin. In the CuPy implementation, both angular-bin assignment
and weighted summation are executed directly on GPU arrays. Conceptually, this
radially compresses the 2D image into a 1D angular occupancy signal; for multi-hole
alignment, it is significantly more stable than edge fragments in the raw frames [46].



                                                47
3.4.4   Fourier-Based Orientation Alignment of Multi-Hole Sprays

Rather than proceeding from fixed offsets, the plume guide angles are estimated from
the periodicity of the angular signal. Let
                                           ∑︁
                                   ¯
                                   𝐴(𝑘) =     𝐴𝑡 (𝑘)
                                             𝑡

be the time-summed angular profile, and let 𝑃 be the known number of plumes. To
align the plume orientation, the Fourier-based phase alignment step computes the
                                      ¯ reads the phase of the plume harmonic 𝑃, and
real fast Fourier transform (FFT) of 𝐴,
converts it to an angular offset:
                                        (︁         )︁
                                     arg F { 𝐴}[𝑃]
                                             ¯        180◦
                             𝜙ˆ0 = −                       .
                                           𝑃           𝜋
This is exactly what makes the alignment adaptive: the method uses the harmonic
phase observed in the current dataset, rather than manually assuming a reference angle
for each recording. The multi-hole processing workflow then constructs nominal
plume guide directions from the equally spaced hole layout,
                             360◦ 𝑝 ˆ
                      𝜙𝑝 =         − 𝜙0 ,        𝑝 = 0, . . . , 𝑃 − 1,
                               𝑃
where the FFT phase supplies the measured global angular offset. These guide angles
define plume-local strip coordinates, but they do not force the measured envelopes
to be symmetric: plume width, occupied angular support, penetration, and boundary
descriptors are still extracted from the image response after remapping. Observed
envelope asymmetry can arise from hole-to-hole hydraulic variation, cavitation and
breakup, droplet-size-dependent scattering, illumination or projection effects, and
plume interaction; Mie brightness is therefore not interpreted as a symmetric mass
distribution. The registration step also incorporates a triangle-threshold occupancy
mask and short-gap filling to exclude empty angular sectors. While FFT itself is
standard, the contribution here is transforming the angular density signal into an
automatic plume registration rule using the known multi-hole count of the injector
while preserving plume-to-plume asymmetry in the downstream observables [46],
[83]. This registration procedure is structurally robust to isolated plume anomalies
because the phase estimate is derived from a signal already averaged over the full
frame count and radial coordinate, giving a single malfunctioning hole limited leverage
over the aggregate harmonic phase. Persistent hydraulic anomalies such as orifice
blockage were not observed during this campaign; more importantly, the robust outlier
and quality-control filtering applied downstream in the fitting pipeline (Section 4.3) is
explicitly designed to flag and exclude trajectories exhibiting anomalous penetration
behaviour, which would intercept such a failure mode even if the alignment step itself
were mildly perturbed.

Figure 9: Polar angular occupancy analysis. (a) Frame-wise angular signal heatmap;

    The same occupied-angle mask also yields a simple proxy for the cone angle.
The approach treats the bin-wise mask as a periodic signal over [0, 360◦ ), merges
True segments crossing the 0◦ /360◦ boundary, and converts each contiguous segment
length from bins to degrees. The resulting angular width quantifies the angular support
span occupied by each plume throughout the recording. Both the total occupied angle
and the average width of contiguous segments are reported; the latter serves as a
rough cone angle proxy, summarizing the average angular spread of the plume without
independently fitting edges frame by frame.

3.4.5      Affine Remapping to Plume-Aligned Strip Coordinates

Once the target angles are determined, each plume is remapped to a common strip
coordinate system centered on the left side of the nozzle. The physical purpose is to
express all plume measurements along a shared local axis. In image-processing terms,
each plume angle 𝜙 𝑝 defines an affine rotation plus translation that places the nozzle
at the left edge and middle height of the output strip; the remapping is achieved using
the inverse relationship
        [︃ ]︃        (︃ [︃ ]︃   )︃         [︃                   ]︃
          𝑥 src    −1 𝑢                        cos 𝜙 𝑝 sin 𝜙 𝑝
                =A            −b ,    A=                           , b = t − Ac,
          𝑦 src           𝑣                   − sin 𝜙 𝑝 cos 𝜙 𝑝

                                                      49
Figure 10: Occupied-angle mask derived from the FFT-based plume registration,
shown in two coordinate systems. (a) Cartesian view: angular bins occupied by the
spray over the full video duration are rendered as white wedges, with the nozzle center
(red cross) and the field of view (FOV) boundary (red dashed circle) overlaid; the
annotation reports the total occupied angle, detected run count, and cone-angle proxy.
(b) Polar unrolled view: each column corresponds to one 0.5◦ angular bin, with white
indicating spray presence and the green horizontal line marking the FOV radius;
individual run widths (cone-angle estimates per plume) are annotated at the top.


where c = (𝑐 𝑥 , 𝑐 𝑦 ) ⊤ is the nozzle center and t is the strip origin translation. For each
plume, the inverse sampling coordinate maps are precomputed exactly once from the
forward affine transform, and the video cube is then remapped and resampled using
nearest-neighbor, bilinear, bicubic, or Lanczos kernels. These maps store source image
sampling coordinates rather than spray intensities: the X-map pixel values give the
𝑥-coordinates sampled in the source image for the output strip pixels, while the Y-map
stores the corresponding 𝑦-coordinates. Following remapping, the multi-hole pipeline
applies a fixed standardized strip size and a triangular plume mask. Because the inverse
map is precomputed once per plume angle, the per-frame overhead is rotation-agnostic;
all subsequent metrics are measured in this shared local strip coordinate system [46].

3.4.6   Plume Strip Binarization and Morphological Repair

The high-pass strips are no longer binarized using triangle thresholding, because that
rule is highly sensitive to near-zero peaks, long-tailed intensity distributions, and
the proportion of zeros introduced by masking. The current implementation first
applies percentile-based min-max normalization to each plume’s (𝑡, 𝑦, 𝑥) high-pass
strip volume, mapping the 𝑞 min = 5 and 𝑞 max = 99.9 percentiles to 0 and 1, and
clipping values outside the range. A single fixed threshold 𝑇BW = 0.01 is then applied
to the normalized strips 𝑆˜ 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑦, 𝑥):
                                           [︁                   ]︁
                          𝐵 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑦, 𝑥) = 1 𝑆˜ 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑦, 𝑥) > 𝑇BW .

Figure 12: Compact example from high-pass representation to binary spray support.

    When the binary support requires further patching, the temporal and frame-wise
binary support repair step processes the temporal volume first, followed by single
frames. For each plume’s (𝑡, 𝑦, 𝑥) binary volume, the repair step first performs closing
with a 3 × 3 × 3 structuring element, fills holes, and retains the 3D largest connected
component; subsequently, the 2D largest connected component is retained for each
frame. The former leverages temporal adjacency to repair transient breaks, while
the latter removes dangling fragments in individual frames. Figure 12(d) shows the
impact of this repair path on a single-frame support mask; it should be interpreted as a
robustification step for binary geometric metrics rather than a physical restoration of
the raw Mie intensity field.

3.4.7                  Penetration Measurement from Cumulative Axial Intensity

The penetration estimator is deliberately based on cumulative intensity support
rather than a single brightest pixel or the outermost isolated blob. For each plume
strip 𝐼 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑦, 𝑥), the cumulative axial-intensity penetration estimator first collapses
transversely,                                 ∑︁
                                 𝐻 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑥) =    𝐼 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑦, 𝑥),
                                                                                  𝑦




                                                                                 52
then subtracts the left-edge baseline for each frame, clips negative values, and generates
a support mask via automatic thresholding, largest connected component cleaning,
and binary opening. Within this support region, the axial cumulative-distribution front
calculation constructs the axial cumulative distribution
                                            ∑︁
                                               𝜉≤𝑥 𝐻 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝜉)
                                                     ˜
                              𝐶 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑥) = ∑︁                  ,
                                              𝜉 𝐻 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝜉) + 𝜀
                                                ˜

where 𝐻˜ 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝜉) is the baseline-subtracted and cleaned transverse intensity sum, 𝜀
prevents division by zero, and 𝐶 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑥) is the resulting axial cumulative distribution.
Subsequently, the penetration front 𝑥ˆ 𝑝 (𝑡) is defined as the minimum position satisfying

                             𝑥ˆ 𝑝 (𝑡) = min{𝑥 : 𝐶 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑥) ≥ 𝑞},

where the quantile threshold is fixed at 𝑞 = 0.995 across the entire batch archive in
this work. Intuitively, this treats the normalised axial intensity profile as a distribution
of where the liquid phase resides along the spray axis: 𝐶 𝑝 (𝑡, 𝑥) is its cumulative
distribution, and the front 𝑥ˆ 𝑝 (𝑡) is simply its 99.5% quantile: the axial distance behind
which 99.5% of the column-summed brightness has accumulated. A quantile is
governed by the bulk of the distribution rather than its extreme, so an isolated bright
speckle or a detached scattered-light halo ahead of the front contributes negligibly
to the cumulative sum and cannot displace the recovered tip, unlike a brightest- or
outermost-pixel rule that a single outlier can capture. Because the column-summed
intensity CDF rises steeply at the leading edge, the recovered front is insensitive
to 𝑞 within a neighbourhood of this value; 𝑞 = 0.995 is chosen as a conservative
leading-edge definition that excludes the faint scattered-light halo beyond the liquid
core, rather than as a tuned hyperparameter. The inner radius is then subtracted,
expressing the distance relative to the nozzle annulus, followed by explicit trajectory
cleaning. This cleaning step does not elevate all receding values via a cumulative
maximum, but directly truncates the tail upon detecting the first non-physical backward
jump:
            Δ𝑥ˆ 𝑝 ( 𝑗) = 𝑥ˆ 𝑝 ( 𝑗 + 1) − 𝑥ˆ 𝑝 ( 𝑗),  𝑗 ★𝑝 = min{ 𝑗 : Δ𝑥ˆ 𝑝 ( 𝑗) < 0},
                              𝑥ˆ 𝑝 (𝑡) ← NaN for 𝑡 ≥ 𝑗 ★𝑝.




                                             53
Figure 13: Time-distance (TD) intensity maps illustrating the CDF penetration
extraction on a representative single plume (single-hole recording; the same processing
applies to all nozzles). Top: the TD map compresses the plume strip transversely,
yielding a 2D view of axial spray development over time; hydraulic delay (white
dashed) and nozzle closing (black dashed) are annotated. Bottom: the CDF penetration
front 𝑥ˆ 𝑝 (𝑡) (pink curve) overlaid on the gain-corrected TD map, extracted at quantile
𝑞 = 0.995.

In this processing rule, truncation starts around the first negative difference, potentially
sacrificing weaker reappearing signals later; this is a conservative choice made to
avoid mistaking isolated noise spikes or boundary reflections for genuine penetration
retractions. Following tail truncation, a valid-frame mask is constructed and isolated
short valid segments are removed. This CDF front is one of the pipeline’s primary
practical contributions: it is far less sensitive to fractured leading-edge pixels than a
naive maximum-index definition, while still yielding a penetration quantity consistent
with standard diesel spray conventions [13], [14], [32].
    The practical payoff of this cumulative-support definition becomes clearest when
the CDF front is placed beside the two binary-projection alternatives that the same
pipeline also exports: a maximum-index axial penetration (bw_x) and an outermost-
boundary polar penetration (bw_polar),54both defined in Section 3.4.8. Figure 14
overlays all ten plumes of two representative top-view recordings under the three
definitions and exposes the two raw-output behaviours that the subsequent cleaning
Figure 14: Raw per-recording CV output for the three penetration defini-

                                              56
Figure 15: Near-nozzle timing signal used to detect the hydraulic delay and nozzle
closing event. The robustly normalized patch intensity (blue) is formed by summing,
frame by frame, all pixels within the calibrated near-nozzle patch. It rises above the
high threshold (red dashed) at the hydraulic delay (solid red vertical line) and drops
below the low threshold (orange dashed) at nozzle closing (black vertical line); the
detected active injection interval is highlighted in green. Single-hole recording shown
for clarity; the same detection algorithm is applied to all nozzles.


Finally, the opening and closing times near the nozzle are inferred from a plume-local
detection window in the binarized strip. In the production multi-hole pipeline, this
window is 20 px high and 30 px wide, centered vertically on the plume strip and
starting at inner_radius downstream of the orifice origin. The detection logic sums
the binary support signal inside this window, applies a median-absolute-deviation-
scaled two-threshold hysteresis rule, and marks the resulting active injection interval;
Figure 15 shows a representative timing signal with the recovered hydraulic delay and
nozzle closing events.
    These metrics are important because they compress each aligned strip into a
compact geometric state vector; however, the downstream modeling in this work
remains centered on penetration, as it is the most well-supported target in existing
analyses [16], [31]. Taken together, these stages (background normalization, edge
enhancement, polar registration, strip remapping, binarization, penetration extraction,
and boundary descriptors) must be evaluated for every one of the 2,992 recordings in
the archive; Section 3.4.9 returns to how the pipeline is structured so that this remains
tractable on a single workstation GPU.




                                           57
3.4.9   Computational Processing of Large Optical Video Archives

Processing the full archive end-to-end on a single GPU requires keeping that GPU
continuously busy rather than stalling on disk reads or output writes; the design
described here reduces full-archive processing from the day-scale, crash-prone legacy
script to ≈72 minutes (≈42 recordings per minute; Table 6). The pipeline runs four
lanes in parallel: disk prefetch, GPU compute, CPU feature extraction, and output
writing (Figure 16). While the GPU processes one recording, the disk worker loads the
next. Output files are written in the background after the GPU finishes. If the process is
interrupted, a checkpoint file allows it to resume from where it stopped. Video loading
employs depth-1 producer-consumer prefetching: while recording 𝑁 is processed on
the GPU, a background worker reads recording 𝑁 + 1 from disk, overlapping GPU
computation with disk I/O rather than executing serially. Host-to-device transfers
and CuPy array construction are deliberately kept in the main thread because the
CuPy CUDA context is thread-bound; this allows kernel scheduling, synchronization,
and memory pool states to be centrally managed by the same thread without locking
overhead. Output writing is handled by a long-lived three-worker I/O pool: metrics
CSVs, metadata JSONs, boundary archives, and optional preview video files are
submitted as futures immediately after the GPU stage finishes, overlapping the GPU
processing of the next recording with the disk writes of the previous one. A fourth,
CPU-bound lane runs concurrently with the GPU stage. Once a recording’s binarized
plume volumes are copied back to the host, per-plume boundary and geometric features
are extracted across a thread pool of up to ⌊𝑛cores /2⌋ workers. Because this feature
extraction is numpy/scipy-bound and releases the global interpreter lock (GIL),
threads, rather than processes, provide concurrency without the Windows spawn cost
of duplicating large plume arrays across process boundaries.




                                           58

Figure 16: Four-lane overlapped execution model of the batch image-processing
pipeline. Time flows left to right; each column is one processing cycle. While
the GPU processes recording 𝑁 (Lane 2: host-to-device transfer and float16 cast,
preprocessing, then post-processing and CDF extraction), a single prefetch thread
reads recording 𝑁+1 from disk (Lane 1), and the host outputs of the previous recording
are handed to a CPU thread pool for per-plume boundary metrics (Lane 3) and to a
three-worker pool for asynchronous CSV, metadata, boundary, and preview writes
(Lane 4). In steady state all four lanes are busy simultaneously, so disk I/O and output
writing are hidden behind GPU compute. A checkpoint with an fsync after every
recording makes an interrupted multi-hour batch resumable without duplicated work;
the measured per-stage costs are given in Table 6.

    All lanes share a single backend abstraction (xp/cp): when a CUDA runtime
is available the heavy operations dispatch to CuPy on one GPU, and the identical
code path falls back to NumPy otherwise. On load, each video is additionally cast to
float16 and normalized on the device, halving its working-set footprint and memory
bandwidth relative to float32.
    Memory management is also tailored to the archive access pattern. After each
recording is processed, temporary arrays are released, and the CuPy arrays are returned
to the default memory pool via garbage collection; however, block-level pool freeing is
intentionally deferred to subfolder boundaries. Because consecutive recordings within
the same subfolder typically share the same frame dimensions, retaining the pool
blocks allows the next file to directly reuse allocated GPU buffers, avoiding repeated
round-trips to the CUDA driver; enforcing a pool block free at subfolder boundaries
limits fragmentation during long-running archive jobs.
    Table 6 quantifies the effect of this design across the full archive. The GPU
postprocessing-and-CDF stage is the only hotspot (∼81% of per-recording time), CPU
feature extraction accounts for ∼12%, and the CSV, metadata, and boundary writes
contribute essentially nothing to the main-thread critical path (<0.1%), confirming that
the asynchronous I/O lane successfully hides disk latency behind GPU compute. The
benefit of pool reuse is visible in the load stage: the first recording in each subfolder
incurs a cold load of ∼0.022 s, whereas subsequent
                                            59        identically-shaped recordings load
in ∼0.010 s once their device buffers are reused from the pool.
         Stage                         Mean (s)   % of total   Lane

         Disk load                       0.011       0.8       prefetch → main
         Preprocessing                   0.043       3.0       GPU
         Postprocessing + CDF            1.167      81.3       GPU
         BW feature metrics              0.167      11.6       CPU thread pool
         CSV + metadata submit          <0.001       0.0       async I/O
         Boundary NPZ submit            <0.001       0.0       async I/O
         Preview build (optional)        0.043       3.0       main; async write

         Per recording (total)           1.435      100.0      —

Table 6: Measured per-recording wall-clock breakdown, averaged over the full archive
of 2,992 recordings processed on a single GPU. GPU stages dominate at ∼84%;
the asynchronous CSV, metadata, and boundary writes contribute <0.1% to the
main-thread critical path because they are submitted as futures to the I/O lane and
overlap the next recording’s GPU work. The per-recording times sum to ≈72 minutes
(≈42 recordings/min) for the full cropped archive.


     The pipeline also maintains a resumable output contract. Every run writes a
checkpoint and explicitly flushes the filesystem after each recording. The completion
condition requires both the image-derived feature CSV and the experimental-parameter
metadata JSON to exist; when boundary export is enabled, the corresponding boundary
archive must also exist. Any partial results will trigger reprocessing rather than being
silently accepted. This contract allows a failed, hours-long batch job to resume from
the last successful file without duplicating work.
     The same checkpoint logic is used to unify metadata from heterogeneous testing
years. The frame rate is preferentially read from the Phantom CINE header field
FrameRate, with FrameRate16 and fPbRate as sequential fallbacks; the injection
duration is queried from a test-matrix JSON keyed by the CINE filename, further
falling back to group constants; injection pressure, plume count, and umbrella angle
are sourced from the nozzle or test-matrix JSON files, falling back to conservative
in-script defaults only when missing. This metadata hierarchy is particularly critical
for the mixed Nozzle0 (2022) and Nozzle1–8 (2024) archives, as their recording header
conventions differ.

3.5    Boundary Extraction Without Pixel-Level Ground Truth
Boundary extraction in this thesis relies on gradient enhancement, morphological
cleaning, and physical consistency checks. In Mie scattering images, droplet scattering
creates strong local contrast between the spray and the background, which suits this
approach well.




                                          60
Figure 17: Visual inspection of spray boundary extraction results. The grayscale
foreground is obtained from the raw Mie image via logarithmic background division,
Sobel gradient enhancement, and nearest-neighbor rotation remapping; the red contour
is the BW boundary derived from the binary support image. This overlay demonstrates
that the boundary proxy predominantly tracks the effective liquid-phase envelope
formed by local contrast changes, rather than relying on a single foremost bright point.


    No pixel-level ground-truth masks were annotated. Defining such masks for
high-speed Mie time series requires extensive manual labelling, and the resulting
labels would reflect subjective boundary judgments rather than a physical ground truth.
The spray boundary shifts with atomization and breakup, so no unique pixel-level
ground truth exists. Metrics like IoU or F1 are therefore not applicable here.
    Instead, boundary reliability is assessed through overlay inspection, temporal
continuity, and agreement across multiple geometric proxies. This aligns with the
goal of this work: extracting stable penetration, cone angle, and boundary envelope
observables rather than training a semantic segmentation model. Quantitative down-
stream evidence for these CDF-based observables, in particular leave-one-nozzle-out
(LONO) transfer diagnostics and censoring-controlled prediction behaviour under the
FOV right-censoring defined above, is presented in Chapter 5. A systematic sensitivity
sweep over the fixed thresholding and percentile-normalization parameters (Table 5)
was judged to be outside the scope of the present work; any residual segmentation drift
would instead be expected to surface as systematic bias or degraded transfer in the
downstream LONO diagnostics of Chapter 5, rather than being characterized directly.

                                                                62
4 Censoring-Aware Penetration Surrogate Modeling
  and Probabilistic Screening
This chapter begins with the plume-resolved observations generated by the image
processing workflow, transforming these discrete, partially right-censored measure-
ments into a trainable target, an uncertainty-aware surrogate model, and a simplified
geometric screening proxy. The separation between "video-to-observation" and
"observation-to-predictor" is intentional, allowing the assumptions and limitations of
each layer to be examined independently.
    Short pilot injections exacerbate the tension between measured trajectories and
trainable targets. Classical empirical penetration laws remain physically valuable,
but their applicability narrows when the observation window is dominated by the
start-of-injection (SOI) and end-of-injection (EOI) transients. The right-censoring
problem identified in Chapter 2 is treated here as a target-construction constraint that
propagates through the fitting, heteroscedastic uncertainty, and distillation stages.

Terminological Conventions.          Four operational labels recur throughout this chapter:
SGQR. The three-parameter sigmoid-gated quarter-root single-branch fit defined in
Section 4.3.
𝐴. The single amplitude divisor used for target scaling and physical reconstruction,
                   ˆ and 𝜎𝑆 = 𝐴𝜎;
𝑆ˆ = 𝑆/𝐴, 𝜇 𝑆 = 𝐴 𝜇,               ˆ its exponents are justified in Section 4.4.
Datasets #1/#2/#3. Respectively, the raw cleaned CDF observations, the censor-
truncated evaluation points and P50/SGQR oracle, and the post-fit-shielded SGQR
trajectory archive; their distinct roles are specified in Section 4.2.2.
LONO. Leave-one-nozzle-family-out retraining and evaluation; unlike the group-aware
in-domain split, it probes generalization to a nozzle family absent from training.
    Spatial right-censoring refers specifically to a trajectory reaching its nozzle-specific
FOV cap. Density-drop signal loss is a distinct loss of observable Mie support before
the cap, although either trigger can truncate Dataset #2. Post-fit shielding denotes
the SGQR plausibility and robust-outlier gate; “filtering” remains a generic verb
that should always be qualified, while “screening” is reserved for the downstream
geometric proxy. The Stage 2 teacher is the frozen heteroscedastic MLP used to
supervise Stage 3; SGQR curves are fitting targets, not themselves a teacher model.

4.1    Problem Reframing and Observation Conventions
The modeling task in this chapter is intentionally reframed as a low-dimensional
probabilistic regression problem rather than a raw image prediction problem. Chapter
3 has already converted the top-view Mie records into plume-resolved temporal
observations. Therefore, the surrogate model receives operating conditions, geometric
descriptors, and time, and predicts the conditional penetration distribution,

          𝑥 = {𝑡, 𝜃 tilt , 𝑁plume , 𝑡inj , 𝑃cb , 𝑃inj , 𝑃ch , 𝑑𝑛 , 𝜌𝑎 } ↦−→ {𝜇 𝑆 (𝑡), 𝜎𝑆 (𝑡)}.



                                                  63
The scalar target 𝑆(𝑡) is measured in millimeters, and 𝜎𝑆 (𝑡) is interpreted as a
heteroscedastic conditional spread rather than a single global residual band. 𝜎𝑆 (𝑡) does
not include epistemic uncertainty. Multi-seed retraining probes only the sensitivity of
aggregate metrics to initialization and optimization stochasticity; it does not quantify
pointwise epistemic uncertainty arising from limited data coverage.
     Among the penetration descriptors exported by the image processing workflow,
CDF penetration is used as the primary training and evaluation observation. Binary
axial penetration 𝑆BW,x and polar/boundary penetration surrogate 𝑅BW remain im-
portant consistency checks and geometric diagnostics, but they inherit thresholding,
morphological, and boundary interpretation choices from the binary support images.
The CDF front, in contrast, tracks the axial cumulative support of the cleaned plume
strips, making it less sensitive to isolated leading pixels and aligning more directly
with the time-series target required by the surrogate. Therefore, unless explicitly
marked otherwise, all training and evaluation protocols below use the CDF penetration
convention.
     This reframing also clarifies the role of uncertainty. The objective is not merely to
fit a smooth mean curve through every measured point; the downstream screening task
requires a distribution over plausible penetrations at each time point. Consequently,
this chapter processes the archive in three complementary ways: as a collection of
raw time-indexed measurements, as a statistically truncated point-level evaluation set,
and as an archive of smoothed fitted trajectories that can serve as a smooth teacher.
The remainder of this chapter will define these data populations, construct the fitting
targets, and then compare neural network-based and kernel-based method backbones
on the same scaled regression problem.

4.2 Right-Censoring in Penetration Targets and Three Derived
    Data Populations
4.2.1   Quantifying How Many Trajectories Are Truncated by the Field of View

Before constructing fitting targets, we quantify the severity of the CDF penetration
right-censoring problem directly from the image processing output. This audit defines
censoring spatially, rather than based on the subsequent 5 ms surrogate model horizon.
For each nozzle family 𝑛, all positive raw CDF penetration values derived from the
multi-hole Mie workflow are pooled, and a field-of-view (FOV) cap 𝐶𝑛 is estimated
as the 𝑞 = 0.999 quantile. A plume trajectory’s raw or cleaned CDF trace is flagged
as spatially right-censored if it reaches 𝐶𝑛 − 𝜖 𝑛 , where 𝜖 𝑛 = max(1 mm, 0.01𝐶𝑛 ).
This audit is implemented in the spatial right-censoring audit script, which compares
the raw Mie output in the image processing directory with the fitting targets in the
synthetic data export directory (codebase identifiers are listed in Appendix A).
    The resulting scalar diagnostic is the spatial censoring rate,

where 𝑛(𝑖) is the nozzle family of trajectory 𝑖.




                                                65
    The cleaned split contains 24 316 CDF penetration trajectories, of which 9630
satisfy this spatial right-censoring criterion, yielding a spatial censoring rate of
39.6%. The median nozzle-specific cap is 92.52 mm. Among the censored cleaned
trajectories, the median time to first hit the cap is 1.76 ms, equivalent to 44.4% of the
raw positive observation span. If a naive target construction method simply held the
final cleaned CDF penetration value forward, its median gap relative to the accepted
SGQR continuation at 5 ms would be approximately 46 mm; if the cap itself were
treated as a terminal saturation value, the corresponding median gap would still be
43.96 mm. These continuation gaps are not viewed as errors against a wide-field
ground truth. They quantify the magnitude of target bias that would be introduced by
reading cap-hitting trajectories as physical saturation observations.
    This audit clearly points out the modeling consequences. The raw CDF penetration
trajectories handed over by Chapter 3 are not merely short or noisy; a substantial portion
reach nozzle-specific spatial limits long before the time horizon used by the surrogate
model target. Therefore, reduced-order continuations, heteroscedastic teachers, and
the subsequent raw/knowledge distillation (KD) regime split are all responses to the
quantified observational problem, not optional modeling embellishments.

4.2.2   Three Populations Derived from the Cleaned CDF Archive

The cleaned CDF archive is not used as a single homogeneous dataset. Downstream
code reads it through three structurally distinct populations, each with its own selection
biases and role in the surrogate protocol.
     Dataset #1: Raw Cleaned CDF Observations. This population consists of the
per-frame (𝑡, 𝑆) observations that passed Chapter 3’s CDF extraction, hydraulic delay
alignment, jump truncation, and short-segment filtering. It is stored in long and
wide formats in the cleaned CDF sequence exports. The cleaned archive contains
approximately 24,316 post-fit-shielded trajectories and 703,592 individual frame
observations, drawn from a raw inventory of 30,341 plume trajectories; the per-gate
attrition counts on this common raw denominator are reported in Table 9. Its advantage
is point-wise fidelity: these rows are the closest objects in the pipeline to the measured
penetration records, and they preserve the time-varying noise and inter-plume spread.
Its limitation is the censoring quantified above: late-time bins are depleted by FOV
hits and signal decay, so raw supervision is only trustworthy when coverage remains
high. Thus, Stage 3 uses this population directly only in the reliable raw CDF regime.

Table 8: Three derived populations read from the cleaned CDF penetration archive.
Dataset #1 is the closest point-wise measurement record, Dataset #2 is a lower-bias
evaluation yardstick, and Dataset #3 is the smoothed fitting target archive used by
Stage 1 and Stage 2.


    Dataset #2: Statistically Truncated Points and P50/SGQR Oracle. This popu-
lation is derived by aggregating the observations in Dataset #1 per operating condition.
A point-level table bins time across 0–5 ms at Δ𝑡 = 0.1 ms intervals, then truncates
each condition at the first time bin satisfying either a density-drop signal-loss criterion
or an FOV-cap criterion: the smoothed trajectory count per bin drops below a fixed
fraction of its intra-condition peak, or the per-bin 𝑝 95 penetration reaches 95% of
the estimated FOV cap. The resulting uncensored point set contains about 506,634
points spanning 592 operating conditions. An accompanying P50/SGQR oracle is
constructed by taking the per-bin median penetration for each surviving condition
and fitting a SGQR to this median trace; it covers 495 conditions and provides a
condition-level reference curve. This dataset is not used for training. It serves as
a lower-bias evaluation yardstick for uncensored CDF accuracy, P50 observation
behavior, SGQR extrapolation diagnostics, leave-one-nozzle-out (LONO) evaluation,
and the comparison between MLP and Sparse Variational Gaussian Process (SVGP).




                                              67
    Dataset #3: Per-Trajectory SGQR Fitted Archive. For every accepted plume
trajectory, the reduced-order SGQR model described in Section 4.3 stores the fitted
parameters and associated fit diagnostics. This population provides smooth, differen-
tiable, and extrapolatable targets over the surrogate model horizon. Stage 1 selects a
representative fitted trajectory from this population for each operating condition group,
and Stage 2 trains on all SGQR trajectories that pass post-fit shielding. Dataset #3 is
therefore the training source for the clean-plume Stage 2 teacher, but it also carries an
explicit selection bias: it favors optically clean, monotonic trajectories that are well
represented by SGQR.
    The distinction between Stage 1 representative trajectories and the P50/SGQR
oracle is particularly important. A Stage 1 row is a real SGQR fitted trajectory extracted
from Dataset #3: within each operating condition group, the fitted curves are evaluated
at 5 ms, the group median is computed, and the fitted curve whose 5 ms evaluation is
closest to the group median is retained. The P50/SGQR oracle is a synthetic trajectory
constructed from Dataset #2 by taking the per-time-bin median and then fitting a
SGQR to that median trace. The former is a training target and inherits Dataset #3’s
post-fit shielding; the latter is an evaluation reference and never enters training. The
shared 5 ms horizon is a deliberate design redundancy. Raw Dataset #1 observations
are effectively exhausted by approximately 2 ms in most operating conditions after
spatial censoring; extending the SGQR reconstruction out to 5 ms gives Stages 1 and 2
uniformly dense SGQR targets over the full prediction window. Stage 3 receives this
continuation only indirectly through the frozen Stage 2 teacher, while using Dataset #1
directly where raw coverage remains reliable.

4.3     Censoring-Aware SGQR Target Construction
4.3.1   Trajectory Cleaning and Time Alignment

The prerequisite for fitting and learning is that all penetration curves are aligned with
the start of injection (SOI) as time zero: whether it is a polynomial empirical model
or a neural network, its fitted parameters are highly sensitive to the time origin. If
the start times of individual plumes are scattered across different frames, families of
curves under the same operating conditions will exhibit systematic offsets, preventing
the model from learning the true penetration dynamics.
    The learning model is not directly trained on the raw exported trajectories. Instead,
the cleaning and fitting routines first clean and align them. For each plume, the routine
finds the earliest valid penetration segment, takes the first two positive samples after
onset, fits a line to these early non-zero points, and uses the 𝑥-axis intercept of this line
as the sub-frame onset delay estimate if the fitted slope is positive. If an area-based
onset marker exists (projected area derived by summing foreground pixels of the
binarized spray image per frame and converting via pixel-to-physical scale), this frame
anchors the delay sweep; otherwise, it falls back to the first positive penetration frame.
The resulting raw delay is then clipped to a small, median-centered window (across
plumes) so that a single anomalous plume does not excessively shift the entire group.



                                             68
    This delay estimate is used in two coupled ways. The penetration array itself is
shifted left by the nearest integer number of frames, while the aligned time vector
retains the fractional residual through an exact offset subtraction. This pipeline
performs plume onset registration, preserving sub-frame temporal information prior
to the subsequent reduced-order fitting. After alignment, sudden unphysical jumps are
removed, and low-quality fits are flagged via a set of rule-based masks. The residual
offset between plumes post-alignment typically does not exceed three frames; hydraulic
delay (the time difference between the electrical signal and spray appearance) is not
formally statistically analyzed across operating conditions, but the temporal magnitude
corresponding to this frame-level spread at the current frame rate is much smaller than
the injection duration.

4.3.2   Two-Stage Candidate Model

Hiroyasu and Arai described diesel spray penetration as two regimes: pre-breakup
growth proportional to 𝑡, and post-breakup quasi-steady growth proportional to 𝑡 1/2 ,
with the breakup time 𝑡 𝑏 marking the transition [13]. In pilot diesel injection conditions,
however, 𝑡 𝑏 falls very early in the observable window (empirically only a few camera
frames), so the pre-breakup linear regime occupies too little of the signal to fit
reliably. Zhou et al. further established that the SOI acceleration phase follows an
uncertain exponent that is difficult to characterise from short, noisy Mie trajectories
[20]; in practice, the fitting window begins effectively in √        the post-𝑡 𝑏 regime. From
this starting point, two functional primitives are chosen: 𝑡 to span the early post-
breakup quasi-steady regime familiar from the H–A framework, and 𝑡 1/4 to capture the
empirically observed late-stage deceleration associated with post-injection dynamics
[21]. Rather than imposing an explicit delayed clock (𝑡 − 𝑡𝑖 ), which would require
reliably identifying the end-of-injection moment from noisy per-plume records, a
sigmoid gating weight 𝑤(𝑡) interpolates continuously between the two branches,
making the transition differentiable and independent of any external physical feature.
    Each accepted trajectory is then fitted with a smooth two-stage model,
                                      √                                          (︂ 𝑡 − 𝑡 )︂
                                                                                          0
         𝑆(𝑡) = (1 − 𝑤(𝑡)) 𝑘 sqrt 𝑡 + 𝑤(𝑡)𝑘 quarter 𝑡 1/4 ,         𝑤(𝑡) = 𝜎                  ,
                                                                                        𝑠
where 𝑘 sqrt , 𝑘 quarter , 𝑡0 , and 𝑠 are optimized in log-space. Specifically, the fitting
problem is framed as a robust nonlinear least squares estimation,
             ∑︁                                  [︁                                          ]︁
        min       𝜌 𝛿 (𝑆(𝑡𝑖 ; 𝜃) − 𝑦𝑖 ) ,    𝜃 = log 𝑘 sqrt , log 𝑘 quarter , log 𝑡 0 , log 𝑠 ,
         𝜃
              𝑖
using the Huber penalty function
                                      {︄ 1 2
                                         2 𝑟(︂ ,          |𝑟 | ≤ 𝛿,
                            𝜌 𝛿 (𝑟) =                )︂
                                        𝛿 |𝑟 | − 12 𝛿 ,   |𝑟 | > 𝛿.
where 𝑟 = 𝑆(𝑡𝑖 ; 𝜃) − 𝑦𝑖 is the fit residual, and 𝛿 is the threshold where the penalty
transitions from squared error to absolute error. This objective is solved via trust-region
nonlinear least squares with 𝛿 = 1, while the logarithmic parameterization preserves
the positivity of the fit gains and transition scale [84], [85].

                                              69
    Therefore, this model should be read as a temporal parameterization rather than an
explicit physical law. Its purposes are twofold: to provide credible extrapolation far
from the EOI (downstream surrogate models require stable targets) and acceptable
interpolation near the SOI (where relative errors are large and early apparent physics
are hard to constrain from the image trajectory itself). The sigmoid gating is important
for this role. Rather than imposing a hard breakpoint between two empirical branches,
it creates a differentiable transition, which maintains the smoothness of the fitted
curve and makes it more compatible with subsequent neural network stages. In other
words, early transient physics are not forced into a strict closed-form law here; they
are merely approximated well enough to allow the subsequent network to learn the
remaining condition-dependent behavior from the full operating point inputs. When
compared to the extended EOI expressions in the spray literature, this interpretation
becomes clearer. The post-EOI model of Zhou et al. distinguishes between a global
injection clock 𝑡 and a delayed clock (𝑡 − 𝑡𝑖 ), because the late-stage penetration branch
is activated only after the end-of-injection dynamics propagate downstream [21].
The present pipeline handles this delay in two layers. First, the preprocessing stage
estimates a plume-specific onset delay based on the early linear extrapolation described
above, shifting the observed sequence to a common 𝑡 ≈ 0 reference. Second, the
reduced-order fit itself still does not incorporate an explicit delayed clock within the
parameter branch. Instead, it uses
                                               (︂ 𝑡 − 𝑡 )︂
                                                       0
                                     𝑤(𝑡) = 𝜎
                                                    𝑠
to suppress the quarter-root branch prior to the learned transition time 𝑡0 , allowing the
𝑡 1/4 term to dominate only after this transition. Algebraically, this is not equivalent to
replacing 𝑡 1/4 with (𝑡 − 𝑡𝑖 ) 1/4 . Rather, it is an engineering reparameterization: over a
finite observation window, a delayed quarter-root branch can typically be adequately
represented by a gated, unshifted quarter-root branch with adjusted amplitude and
transition placement, especially when the early portion of that branch is strongly
attenuated by 1 − 𝑤(𝑡) or a near-zero 𝑤(𝑡). In this sense, the physical clock of the delay
is absorbed into the learned switching parameters (𝑡0 , 𝑠) and the branch amplitude,
rather than being imposed as a fixed, known constant.




                                            70
    In summary, the engineering treatment above is reasonable for the goals of this
thesis, but its applicability has clear boundaries. From an engineering perspective,
compressing the cleaned discrete observations into a few parameters via curve fitting
not only smooths out measurement noise but also reconstructs 𝑦 = 𝑓 (𝑡) at any arbitrary
time bin density, permitting extrapolation within reasonable limits, something that
cannot be done directly with discrete observations. Its justification lies in three points:
the fit acts here as a smooth compressor for noisy trajectories, not as a final mechanistic
spray law; it preserves the differentiability of the curve, facilitating subsequent learning;
and it delegates the operating condition dependencies to the neural network modeling,
eliminating the need to hard-code every physical effect into a closed-form analytical
expression. However, this treatment should be understood as a reduced-order surrogate
modeling strategy inspired by empirical penetration theory, rather than a proof that
shifted-time physical effects have vanished. The ablation experiment below involving
a quarter-root-only branch explicitly contrasts this.

4.3.3   Quarter-Root Dominance and Selection of the SGQR Production Model

The four-parameter candidate is useful mainly as a diagnostic model. In accepted
fits, its square-root branch nearly always collapses, so retaining 𝑘 sqrt adds an unstable
degree of freedom without adding useful trajectory shape. At the fit transition time 𝑡0 ,
the branch-weight ratio is measured as

                                           𝑘 sqrt · 𝑡 01/4
                                      𝑟=                     .
                                               𝑘 1/4
Across 14,563 accepted plume fits in the polar binary penetration archive (the diagnostic
archive on which the four-parameter candidate was characterised before the production
target was switched to CDF penetration), the median ratio is 𝑟 med = 2.9 × 10−9 , with
an interquartile range of [9.0 × 10−10 , 1.1 × 10−8 ]. The collapse is structural rather
than archive-specific: because the breakup time 𝑡 𝑏 falls within the first few observable
frames (as argued above), the square-root branch is starved of support irrespective of
the penetration definition, which is what motivates adopting the single-branch SGQR
form for the CDF production fits below. In 99.8% of accepted fits, 𝑟 < 0.5; in 100%,
𝑟 < 1. Expressed in absolute magnitude, the median quarter-root branch contributes
69.2 mm at 𝑡0 , while the median square-root branch contributes only 2.0 × 10−7 mm.
Successful four-parameter fits therefore degenerate into quarter-root dominated curves.
    A simplified single-branch model, dubbed SGQR (for quarter-root, 1-branch),
eliminates this risk by replacing the two-stage mixture with a single sigmoid-gated
quarter-root branch,                      (︂ 𝑡 − 𝑡 )︂
                                                  0
                            𝑆SGQR (𝑡) = 𝜎             𝑘 1/4 𝑡 1/4 ,
                                               𝑠
reducing the number of parameters from four to three: [log 𝑘 1/4 , log 𝑡0 , log 𝑠]. An
equivalent post-fit shielding pipeline was built in parallel for SGQR, using the same
far-time plausibility window, parameter bounds, grouped robust outlier scores, and
RMSE < 3 mm success criterion. The two model forms are therefore compared under
symmetric post-fit shielding rules.

                                             71
    Out of 23,495 CDF penetration sequences in the nine-nozzle campaign, the four-
parameter model achieved a 61.3% success rate, while SGQR achieved 90.0%, an
absolute improvement of 28.6 percentage points, without a single nozzle regressing.
On sequences accepted by both models, the median RMSE was essentially unchanged
(1.23 mm for the four-parameter model and 1.27 mm for SGQR). On the previously
flagged subset, the median RMSE dropped from 8.12 mm to 1.08 mm, a reduction
of roughly 7.5×. Removing 𝑘 sqrt therefore preserves fit quality on well-behaved
trajectories while preventing unstable far-time behavior on difficult ones.
    Based on this ablation, SGQR was promoted to the production fitting target. Its
curves serve as direct targets for Stages 1 and 2; Stage 3 receives their influence
through the frozen Stage 2 teacher while also using reliable raw observations from
Dataset #1. The four-parameter results are retained merely as a historical baseline for
structural diagnostics and to motivate the model reduction step. Consequently, the
practical purpose of this fit is not to claim a universal penetration law, but rather to
compress noisy plume trajectories into smooth, differentiable, and trainable targets,
while preserving the heteroscedastic variations within the dataset for the subsequent
learning stages.

4.3.4   SGQR Diagnostics: Scaling, Residuals, and Identifiability

Empirical Scaling Check. After fitting each trajectory, three parameter values
(𝑘 1/4 , 𝑡 0 , 𝑠) are obtained. Because the fit constraints force the gating parameters (𝑡 0 , 𝑠)
to make the sigmoid product converge to near 1 within a finite time window, the end
of the observable time period and the extrapolation segment satisfy 𝑆(𝑡) ≈ 𝑘 1/4 · 𝑡 1/4 ;
therefore, 𝑘 1/4 is not an abstract fit amplitude, but the asymptotic linear scaling
coefficient of the 𝑡 1/4 penetration law, which can be directly compared with the
proportionality coefficients of classical empirical formulas. Performing an ex-post
log-log regression on it allows testing whether the fitting results follow the power-law
expected by classical penetration theory with respect to the operating conditions.
Regressing across 24,316 accepted fits via Ordinary Least Squares:

                  log 𝑘 1/4 = 𝑎 log Δ𝑃 + 𝑏 log 𝜌𝑎 + 𝑐 log 𝑑𝑛 + constant

yields exponents 𝑎 ≈ 0.52, 𝑏 ≈ −0.23, 𝑐 ≈ 0.26, and 𝑅 2 ≈ 0.27. These pooled
regressions are descriptive diagnostics over the full accepted archive; they motivate, but
do not themselves fit, the production amplitude exponent. The value used in 𝐴 is fixed
at 0.5, matching the Bernoulli exponent and exceeding the Hiroyasu–Arai exponent of
0.25. The single-seed five-fold Stage 1 LONO feature ablation in Section 4.4.2 provides
out-of-sample support for this choice, but does not by itself rule out model-selection
over-fitting.




                                               72
Figure 20: Empirical scaling of the SGQR parameters (𝑘 1/4 , 𝑡 0 , 𝑠) against Δ𝑃 =
𝑃inj − 𝑃ch , coloured by chamber pressure 𝑃ch . Panel titles report the exponents and 𝑅 2
from log 𝑦 = 𝑎 log Δ𝑃 + 𝑏 log 𝜌𝑎 + 𝑐 log 𝑑𝑛 + constant. The fitted amplitude coefficient
follows the expected ambient-density trend, whereas the diameter and gating-parameter
scalings are weaker.


    Comparison against classical Hiroyasu–Arai theory reveals varying degrees of
agreement. The density exponent 𝑏 ≈ −0.23 almost perfectly matches the −1/4 in the
H–A post-breakup form 𝑆 ∝ Δ𝑃1/4 𝜌𝑎−1/4 (𝑑𝑛 𝑡) 1/2 , serving as a positive signal for the
physical plausibility of the fitted parameter. The injection pressure exponent 𝑎 ≈ 0.52,
however, is markedly steeper than the H–A predicted 1/4; the exact reason for this
deviation is uncertain: the condition coverage of the dataset, the SGQR functional
form, and the possibility that the 𝑡 1/4 branch absorbed contributions beyond the free-jet
phase are all compatible with this result. The diameter exponent 𝑐 ≈ 0.26 is much
lower than the classical 1/2, most likely reflecting collinearity between nozzle diameter
and other operating variables in the experimental matrix (independently confirmed
in Section 4.4.1). The regression 𝑅 2 for the gating parameters 𝑡 0 and 𝑠 are only
0.17 and 0.14, respectively, which is expected: the sigmoid switching parameters are
not directly predicted by classical theory; they mainly absorb residual onset delay
variations after preprocessing alignment.




                                           73
     As a cross-check, direct log-log regressions on the raw discrete observations
within fixed 0.1 ms time bins yielded an 𝑅 2 of roughly 0.47–0.51 in the mid-trajectory
window (0.35–0.75 ms), confirming that the three physical variables indeed explain
a meaningful portion of the raw penetration variance in the well-sampled region.
The lower 𝑅 2 ≈ 0.27 obtained from the compressed 𝑘 1/4 parameter reflects the
integral effect over noisy onset and post-injection regions rather than a model failure;
the qualitative trends of the Δ𝑃 exponent (0.44–0.65, spanning values around the
Bernoulli value 0.5 while remaining above the H–A value 0.25) and the chamber
pressure exponent (≈ −0.25) remain consistent across both analyses.
     Residual Structure and Heteroscedasticity. Diagnostics of the temporal distri-
bution of the fit residuals reveal the systematic behavior of the SGQR model across
different trajectory stages, providing the physical rationale for the three-stage training
and mixed supervision design. Reconstructing the residual 𝑆(𝑡)     ˆ − 𝑦(𝑡) at each ob-
servation frame for every cleaned trajectory and binning by Δ𝑡 = 0.1 ms yields the
empirical envelope in Figure 21, revealing two distinct regions of differing nature.
In the central window 𝑡 ∈ [0.3, 1.0] ms, the median residual remains below 0.4 mm,
and the robust standard deviation 𝜎robust (𝑡) = 1.4826 MAD(𝑡) reaches a minimum of
∼ 0.60 mm around 𝑡 = 1.0 ms, indicating that SGQR is essentially unbiased and tight
in the well-sampled mid-trajectory region.
     Systematic biases exist at both boundary ends: an early positive bias of ∼ 1.7 mm
at 𝑡 = 0.05 ms with 𝜎robust ≈ 1.8 mm, stemming from imprecise onset alignment and
the steep transition of the sigmoid gate near 𝑡 0 ; and a late negative bias of ∼ −1.6 mm
at 𝑡 = 1.55 ms with 𝜎robust ≈ 0.7 mm, arising from the right-censoring of the surviving
plume population, where the frame count per bin drops drastically from ∼ 65,000 at
𝑡 = 0.15 ms to fewer than 200 frames by 𝑡 = 1.55 ms. Thus, the envelope is U-shaped
rather than monotonically growing. This dual-end bias is the core motivation for
introducing mixed supervision in Stage 3: the SGQR fitting target exhibits structural
errors in both the early pre-injection segment and the late observable tail, which
ultimately need to be corrected via direct supervision signals rather than relying solely
on the extension of the fitted curve. Moreover, the significant temporal variation of
the residual scale indicates that a fixed-variance loss is inappropriate, necessitating
the use of input-dependent 𝜎 in Stage 2’s negative log-likelihood (NLL) loss.
     Parameter Identifiability. The third diagnostic examines whether the three SGQR
parameters can be independently determined by the data. If strong mutual compensation
exists among parameters, the individual parameter values themselves will be hard
to interpret, and regressions against operating conditions will be weak. To this end,
using the trust-region Jacobian 𝐽 returned by SciPy, the asymptotic covariance Σ ≈
(𝐽 𝑇 𝐽) −1 𝜎
           ˆ 2 is computed, yielding pairwise parameter correlations. The median results
across all cleaned fits are: corr(log 𝑘 1/4 , log 𝑠) ≈ 0.76, corr(log 𝑘 1/4 , log 𝑡 0 ) ≈ 0.56,
corr(log 𝑡0 , log 𝑠) ≈ 0.21.




                                              74
Figure 21: Temporal residual structure of the SGQR production fit over all 24,316
cleaned trajectories. The top panel shows the median residual with ±𝜎robust ; the bottom
shows 𝜎robust (𝑡) = 1.4826 MAD(𝑡). The larger spread near onset and in the censored
late tail motivates the input-dependent variance used in Stage 2.


    These correlations suggest that the three SGQR parameters are not independent
physical knobs, but rather a coupled 3D smooth surface with considerable soft
redundancy. This is the price paid for introducing the differentiable sigmoid gate (in
contrast to the rigid stage breakpoints of Hiroyasu–Arai), and it also explains why
the gating parameters showed weak scaling relationships against operating conditions
in the earlier regression: the data constrains the surface as a whole, rather than
constraining each axis individually. Regarding downstream design, the surrogate
                                                                        ˆ rather than
model (Section 4.5) is trained to reproduce the entire evaluation curve 𝑆(·)
the parameter triplet itself, so strong parameter coupling does not propagate into the
input-output mapping of the network.




                                          75
Figure 22: Per-fit identifiability diagnostics of the SGQR production model. Left:

derived from (𝐽 𝑇 𝐽) −1 ; off-diagonal elements of ∼ 0.56 and ∼ 0.76 document the soft

4.3.5   Post-Fit Shielding and the Selection Bias It Introduces

A stricter post-fit shielding stage is also an important component of the engineering
contribution. The robust outlier mask first evaluates the fitted curve at 𝑡far = 5 ms to
form a far-time penetration surrogate. The row is retained only if this far-time value
remains within a broad physical plausibility window, and if the fit parameters pass
basic sanity checks on 𝑡 0 , 𝑠, point count, and fit cost. Robust grouped outlier scores
are then computed using the median absolute deviation,

                          𝑥 − median(𝑥)
  𝑧 robust (𝑥) = 0.6745                 ,     MAD(𝑥) = median(|𝑥 − median(𝑥)|) ,
                           MAD(𝑥) + 𝜖
applied to 𝑡0 , log(1 + RMSE), and log(1 + cost_per_point) within each file group.
Rows with |𝑧robust | > 3 on any of these diagnostics are flagged as outliers and excluded
from the cleaned archive.
    This post-fit shielding process introduces intentional engineering trade-offs, and its
primary mechanism shifted alongside improvements in the upstream computer vision
(CV) pipeline. In early processing, uneven illumination or inter-plume occlusion
sometimes led the CV pipeline to mistake background noise for spray tips, generating
physically impossible jumps that were subsequently truncated into severely shortened
sequences by explicit upward-difference thresholds; the 𝑛 ≥ 10 frame lower limit
and 𝑡0 < 0.8 ms sanity gate were primarily designed to intercept these degenerate
trajectories. Following CV enhancements, the vast majority of candidate sequences
now pass the full post-fit shielding pipeline (per-nozzle survival rates are reported in
Figure 23). Flagged sequences are typically longer trajectories whose 𝑡0 , RMSE, or
cost_per_point exceed the three-sigma robust threshold within their group, rather than
short noise-truncated sequences stemming from CV misidentification. As a result, the
shielding logic now serves more as a fit-quality gate than a CV artifact cleaner.


                                            76
Figure 23: CDF penetration SGQR post-fit-shielding survival rates regenerated from
the recovered PC archive. The plot reports total candidate sequence counts, cleaned
sequence counts, and passing ratios across all nine nozzle families, including nozzle 0.


     Therefore, post-fit shielding introduces selection bias along two dimensions. The
first is optical quality bias: the surviving dataset favors well-illuminated, unoccluded,
high-contrast plumes and does not represent true geometric asymmetry or late-time
signal decay. The second is injection profile regularity bias: SGQR is structurally
limited to monotonically increasing smooth penetration curves, while trajectories with
non-monotonic behaviors (arising from bi-modal injection or spray re-acceleration)
carry higher intra-group RMSE and are flagged by the robust z-score outlier gate. The
per-nozzle flag rate quantifies the resulting coverage gap: on the CDF source, the flag
rate for nozzle 3 is 11.9%, roughly double that of nozzles 4 and 6 (both 5.9%), which
is consistent with the spray re-acceleration events observed for nozzle 3 in the Stage
3 diagnostics. Thus, Stage 1 and Stage 2 jointly inherit both biases: their training
sets lean toward optically ideal plumes and trajectories whose injection profiles are
sufficiently regular to be adequately represented by the monotonic SGQR form.
