inventory.


     Figure 23 illustrates the SGQR post-fit-shielding survival rates for CDF penetration
per nozzle, summarizing the total sequence counts, cleaned sequence counts, and
passing ratios. The purpose of this plot is not to argue that the gate is unbiased, but to
clarify the footprint of that bias. Upon regenerating the report against the recovered
PC archive, the nine-nozzle campaign spans a CDF penetration survival band of
88.1%–94.1%. The recovered nozzle 0 archive contributed 15 475 candidate CDF
penetration sequences, of which 14 274 passed post-fit shielding (92.2%), making it
part of the reported coverage. Because no nozzle comes close to being completely
rejected or completely retained, Stage 2 can be interpreted as a clean-plume teacher
over the recovered archive, while still inheriting the explicit spatial and late-time
selection biases documented here.
     This trade-off is deliberate but bounded. By excluding ambiguous data, the pipeline
provides Stage 2 with a clean teacher target that is less susceptible to optical vanishing
effects, background reflections, and incorrect hydraulic delay alignment. That target is
still conditioned on well-illuminated, unoccluded plumes. When Stage 3 relies on it,
the output should therefore be interpreted as a clean-plume continuation rather than a
debiased ensemble across all nozzle geometries.
     In the population terminology of Section 4.2.2, the SGQR fits define Dataset
#3. Stage 3 subsequently reintroduces Dataset #1 during reliable coverage intervals
to recover raw early-time behavior without treating the right-censored tail as fully
observed values.




                                             78
Figure 24: Support topology of the training matrix. The left panel counts injection-
pressure levels over the (𝑃ch , 𝑃cb ) plane; the middle and right panels count nozzle-
diameter levels over (𝑃ch , 𝑃inj ) at 𝑃cb = 1 bar and 4 bar. Most cells contain at most
one diameter level, and all six levels occur only for 𝑃inj = 2000 bar, 𝑃cb = 4 bar, and
𝑃ch ∈ {5, 10, 15} bar.


4.4    Physics-Guided Feature Engineering
Before the engineered feature architecture was finalized, an early nine-feature MLP
trained using direct pressure and diameter inputs revealed a structural problem. Nozzle
diameter exists at only six levels in the dataset, and these are not uniformly available
across all pressure combinations: all six diameter levels appear exclusively within the
family (𝑃inj = 2000 bar, 𝑃cb = 4, 𝑃ch ∈ {5, 10, 15} bar), whereas injection pressure
has only four levels. Figure 24 makes this discretely sampled grid explicit: most
cells in the (chamber pressure, injection pressure, diameter) grid contain zero or one
observation, and full six-diameter coverage exists only along a single pressure family.
    Because a smooth MLP treats these as continuous inputs, it generates derivative
structures along the diameter and pressure axes that the data does not constrain.
Figure 25 shows the result of dense diameter sweeps at 𝑡 = 0.85 ms: between adjacent
observation levels, the predicted mean curve undergoes five to six gradient sign
reversals per slice, and autodiff gradients peak at ∼ 4 × 104 over a support span of
about 0.05 mm, equating to a curvature norm on the order of 107 . The same diagnostic
on injection pressure showed non-monotonic, unsupported extrapolation over sparsely
supported slices. The root issue is structural. A smooth MLP is asked to learn
continuous laws from what is effectively a discrete grid, so adding regularization
can only suppress the instability at the cost of useful capacity. Therefore, the revised
architecture addresses the problem at the source.
    The revised surrogate model first replaces the weakly supported pressure and
diameter inputs with a single scaling group. The Hiroyasu–Arai post-breakup branch
[13] provides the dimensional starting point:
                                        (︃        )︃ 1/4
                                       Δ𝑃
                            𝑆(𝑡) ∝ 𝐾 𝑝                     𝑑𝑛1/2 𝑡 1/2 ,
                                       𝜌𝑎




                                             79
Figure 25: Dense diameter interpolation by the direct-feature MLP at 𝑡 = 0.85 ms for
three operating conditions. The top row compares the predicted mean with the secant
through the six observed diameter levels; the bottom compares the corresponding
autodiff gradient with the secant slope. The unsupported sign reversals and gradient
spikes show that continuous diameter dependence is not identified by the sparse
support.


where the H–A exponent assigns Δ𝑃1/4 to the pressure ratio group. The SGQR scaling
check above and the Dataset #2 verification below both show that the short-pilot training
window is better collapsed by a stronger pressure exponent (validated out-of-sample
by the LONO ablation in Section 4.4.2), so the operational amplitude divisor is

                                 𝐴 = Δ𝑃0.5 · 𝜌𝑎−0.25 · 𝑑𝑛0.5 ,

computed from the canonical feature table exported alongside the test matrix JSON
file. This form should be read as an empirical short-window scaling choice, not a
rejection of the Hiroyasu–Arai late-time asymptote. For nozzles 1 through 8, the
chamber density is taken directly from the logged ambient gas density; for nozzle 0,
where only ambient chamber pressure was logged, 𝜌𝑎 is reconstructed using a linear
law 𝜌𝑎 = 5 kg/m3 × 𝑃ch /4.42 bar calibrated against the nozzle 1-8 data.
     In the first design iteration, all four sparse inputs were fully merged into 𝐴, yielding
a five-feature pure-scaling baseline

                           [ 𝑡norm , 𝜃 tilt,𝑧 , 𝑛plumes,𝑧 , 𝑡𝑖,𝑧 , 𝑃cb,𝑧 ],




                                                 80
where the subscript 𝑧 indicates that a feature has been standardized by subtracting
the training-set mean and dividing by the training-set standard deviation (z-score
normalization), 𝑡 norm denotes time rescaled by a fixed min-max mapping from the
[0, 5 ms] window to [0, 1], and both sets of scaler parameters are estimated from
the training split only and held fixed for validation and test to prevent leakage. 𝐴
was used solely as a target divisor and did not enter the network. The training target
was reparameterized as amplitude-scaled penetration 𝑆(𝑡)       ˆ    = 𝑆(𝑡)/𝐴; the network
predicted 𝜇ˆ and log 𝜎   2
                       ˆ , and physical predictions were recovered during inference via
𝜇 𝑆 = 𝐴 𝜇ˆ and 𝜎𝑆 = 𝐴 𝜎.  ˆ This baseline achieved a Stage 1 test MAE of 8.05 mm and
a Stage 2 test MAE of 9.28 mm.
    However, fully absorbing 𝑃inj and 𝑃ch into 𝐴 eliminated their independent ex-
planatory power beyond what is captured by the fixed Δ𝑃0.5 exponent. Regressions
also estimated non-zero chamber pressure coefficients at fixed Δ𝑃 (roughly −0.21 to
−0.27 in the mid-injection window), meaning 𝑃inj and 𝑃ch carry independent structure
that cannot fully collapse into Δ𝑃. The global Δ𝑃0.5 exponent is itself a mean-field
approximation; when 𝑃inj and 𝑃ch are omitted, any local residual non-linearities in the
condition space are left out of the network’s reach. Nozzle diameter 𝑑𝑛 , however, is a
different case: it exists at only six discrete levels in the dataset and suffers from severe
confounding in the condition matrix (Figure 24), making it an unreliable continuous
input even if reintroduced. It was therefore retained inside 𝐴, letting the H–A prior
carry its effect.
    Building on this, the final design adopted a seven-feature scaling-plus-residual-
pressure formulation

               [ 𝑡norm , 𝜃 tilt,𝑧 , 𝑛plumes,𝑧 , 𝑡𝑖,𝑧 , 𝑃cb,𝑧 , log 𝑃inj,𝑧 , log 𝑃ch,𝑧 ],

where 𝐴 continues to absorb the dominant diameter and dimensional pressure de-
pendencies, while log 𝑃inj and log 𝑃ch are fed in as separate inputs so the network
can learn residual pressure structure beyond the fixed H–A exponents. Compared to
the five-feature baseline, Stage 1 test MAE dropped from 8.05 mm to 2.91 mm, and
Stage 2 test MAE dropped from 9.28 mm to 5.63 mm, confirming that the residual
pressure features provided information obscured by the fixed H–A exponents. Figure 26
summarizes the resulting network and the role of 𝐴 in the physical reconstruction.
    Before any optimization, a scaling validation routine tests the scaling hypothesis
on the actual dataset: each trajectory is divided by its own 𝐴, and the linear regression
𝑅 2 of 𝑆(𝑡)
       ˆ against the sparse features is calculated. In the seven-feature production
run, the sparse feature 𝑅 2 drops from 0.32 for the physical target 𝑆 to 0.016 for the
               ˆ so the check passes. If the check fails, training aborts by default,
scaled target 𝑆,
making the physical prior falsifiable rather than merely asserted. The 0.016 reflects
variance in 𝑆ˆ not explained by the sparse non-time features alone; it does not indicate
that the full model has weak signal, since time itself is the dominant predictor of
penetration magnitude.

Figure 26: Seven-feature amplitude-scaled MLP architecture.          A three-layer

    Stage 1 and Stage 2 also share a group-aware data split: each operating-condition
group, defined jointly by nozzle identity, injection pressure, chamber pressure, and
injection duration, is assigned as a whole to the training, validation, or test set to prevent
leakage between curves that share the same operating point. Without this constraint, a
random trajectory-level split would let the network interpolate between nearly identical
samples drawn from the same operating condition seen during training; the holdout
error would then reflect within-condition smoothness rather than generalization across
the condition space. Therefore, group assignment is not a mere implementation detail
but a structural constraint that ensures the reported test metrics measure interpolation
across different operating conditions, not memorization within a condition. Stage
2 warm-starts from Stage 1’s engineering run directory and reuses its scaler states,
ensuring that variant selection and 𝐴 scaling remain consistent across the two stages.




                                             82
4.4.1   Validating the Physics Scaling Formula Against Uncensored Observa-
        tions

The construction of Dataset #2 starts by unrolling the individual (𝑡, 𝑆) discrete
observation points from the cleaned CDF wide-format sequence table. Even after
excluding points from trajectories that reached the spatial FOV cap, the remaining
discrete points exhibit condition-dependent late-time observability loss: although a
spray plume can remain within the field of view, the Mie scattering signal may fall
below the detection threshold because of droplet breakup, spatial dilution, and local
drops in droplet number density. Some conditions enter this signal-loss regime at
𝑡 < 0.5 ms, while others survive until significantly later. To address this, a point
table generation tool was constructed (codebase identifiers listed in Appendix A) to
determine condition-level truncation thresholds for each of the 592 operating condition
groups using two statistical/optical metrics: (i) the smoothed trajectory count per
bin drops below a fixed fraction of its intra-condition peak (density-drop signal-loss
criterion), or (ii) the per-bin 𝑝 95 penetration reaches 95% of the estimated FOV cap
(FOV-cap criterion). Figure 27 illustrates one representative example of each. The
first bin satisfying either criterion, along with all subsequent observation points, is
discarded, leaving 𝑁unc = 506 634 censor-truncated points for downstream log-log
regression use.




Figure 27: Representative per-condition truncation triggers. Top panels show
trajectory counts; bottom panels show observations, CDF quantiles, the FOV threshold
and cap, and the selected onset. Left: for Nozzle 1, 𝑝 95 reaches the approximately
81 mm FOV threshold near 𝑡 = 1.0 ms. Right: for Nozzle 2, density-drop signal loss
occurs while the maximum remains about 7 mm below that threshold. Post-onset
quantiles become noisy, supporting their exclusion from direct supervision.

comparison. The 𝑐 𝑑𝑛 column is unreliable because of the confounded diameter support
described in the text.


    Per-Condition Temporal Balance. For any regression on time-indexed penetration
observations, a direct concern is that trajectories surviving into late time bins represent
a progressively self-selected subset of the population, potentially biasing the sample
toward slow or wall-restricted plumes. Per-condition truncation mitigates this concern.
Before truncation, the median coefficient of variation for the per-bin trajectory count
across time is CVraw = 0.52, reflecting attrition as trajectories either hit the FOV cap or
lose detectable Mie support. After truncating each condition at its selected threshold,
the median intra-condition CV drops to 0.052 (interquartile range [0.033, 0.082]),
and the median ratio of minimum to average bin count within a condition is 0.92.
Within each surviving window, point counts are thus approximately uniform in time:
the depleted tails are removed rather than reweighted, ensuring each retained time bin
contributes statistically comparable evidence to the downstream regression.
    Log-Log Scaling Regression. Ordinary least squares regression

                  log 𝑆 = 𝑎 log Δ𝑃 + 𝑏 log 𝑃ch + 𝑐 log 𝑑𝑛 + constant

is fitted independently at each 0.1 ms bin, where Δ𝑃 = 𝑃inj − 𝑃ch , and 𝑃ch serves as a
proportional proxy for ambient density 𝜌𝑎 (valid under ideal gas conditions at constant
temperature). Table 10 reports results within the highly informative mid-trajectory
window 𝑡 ∈ [0.30, 0.70] ms, where both point counts and signal strength are high.
     The injection pressure exponent 𝑎 stabilizes around 0.47–0.52 in the 0.40–0.60 ms
window, close to the Bernoulli value of 0.5 and the prior 𝑘 1/4 regression (𝑎 ≈ 0.52).
The chamber pressure exponent 𝑏 stabilizes around −0.23 to −0.25, highly consistent
with the Hiroyasu–Arai prediction of −0.25 and the 𝑘 1/4 estimate (𝑏 ≈ −0.23). Their
agreement across the compressed 𝑘 1/4 parameters and the raw point observations
provides corroborating evidence that the Δ𝑃0.5 · 𝜌𝑎−0.25 component of 𝐴 is not solely
induced by the SGQR fit. Figure 28 displays the exponent evolution over the full time
span.

    Family-Collapse Check. Beyond the regression evidence above, the practical
payoff of choosing Δ𝑃0.5 over the classical Δ𝑃0.25 can be seen directly in how well the
corresponding 𝐴-scaled target collapses the population onto a single curve. Figure 29
re-scales the representative SGQR trajectories used in Stage 1 training (Section 4.5)
by 𝐴 under each exponent choice: the spread across operating conditions, measured
by the median post-0.5 ms ratio of scaled to physical standard deviation, drops from
0.378 under Δ𝑃0.25 to 0.057 under the adopted Δ𝑃0.50 , a roughly 6.6× tighter collapse,
and a direct visual counterpart to the exponent evidence in Table 10 and Figure 28.
    Diameter Exponent and H–A Prior. The 𝑐 𝑑𝑛 column in Table 10 is extremely
large, ranging from 1.7 to 6.0; these values lack physical meaning. Two structural
reasons prevent the diameter exponent from being identified from this dataset. First,
the range for the six nozzle hole diameters is only 0.333–0.384 mm (a ratio of 1.15,
yielding a log-leverage of 0.14), which is roughly three times smaller than the log-
leverage available across the injection pressure range (0.45). Second, pressure and
diameter do not vary independently in the design matrix: the full six-diameter coverage
occurs only within a single injection pressure family (𝑃inj = 2000 bar, 𝑃cb = 4 bar),
                                          85 not only in hole diameter but also in
and within that family, the nozzle sets differ

4.4.2   Feature-Representation Ablation for Cross-Nozzle Generalization

The single-split improvements reported earlier in this section (Stage 1 test MAE
dropping from 8.05 mm to 2.91 mm) confirm that within the same group-aware split,
the seven-feature design outperforms the pure-scaling baseline. To justify this feature
design before moving to multi-stage training, a dedicated Stage 1 leave-one-nozzle-out
(LONO) ablation evaluated all nine variants of the engineered feature design in a
five-fold split. Each fold held out one nozzle family (nozzle 0, nozzle 1, nozzle 2, nozzle
4, or nozzle 5) from the training pool, evaluating holdout generalization under varying
feature configurations. Every cell of the ablation used the same deterministic Stage
1 MSE objective (described in Section 4.5) under a single fixed seed (42) to isolate
feature effects. The final multi-stage model evaluations and baseline comparisons are
reserved for Chapter 5.
    The nine variants span three orthogonal design axes: the amplitude exponent
(𝛼 = 0.25 vs. 𝛼 = 0.5 in 𝐴 = Δ𝑃 𝛼 𝜌𝑎−0.25 𝑑𝑛0.5 ), the presence of log-pressure residuals
(log 𝑃inj,𝑧 , log 𝑃ch,𝑧 ), and the presence of 𝑑𝑛 as an independent input. A legacy
nine-feature reference configuration (where raw log 𝑃inj , log 𝑃ch , log Δ𝑃, and 𝑑𝑛 were
fed directly to the network, targeting 𝑆 in millimeters) predating the amplitude scaling
was included as a baseline. Table 11 reports the mean physical MAE across the five
folds, and Table 12 breaks down the results per fold (the row labels correspond to
variant identifiers in the codebase to facilitate archival reproduction).
    Across the nine variants, three design conclusions are stable. First, 𝛼 = 0.5 has
lower mean LONO MAE than 𝛼 = 0.25 in every matched feature pairing, although the
smallest difference (14.26 mm versus 14.48 mm) is treated as unresolved rather than a
robust ordering. Second, the log-pressure residuals provide a modest additional gain.
Third, re-exposing 𝑑𝑛 as an independent input is associated with a 3.6 mm to 6.8 mm
increase in mean MAE across the matched amplitude-scaled variants. Because 𝑑𝑛 is
already encoded in 𝐴 and has sparse, confounded support, this result is consistent with
a redundant nozzle-correlated channel encouraging family-specific fitting; it does not
directly demonstrate memorization. Tables 11 and 12 retain the exact aggregate and
per-fold values.
    The production configuration (𝛼 = 0.5, with log-pressure residuals and no
independent diameter) was selected because it achieved the lowest unweighted five-
fold mean LONO MAE, 7.46 mm, not because it dominated every fold or minimized
cross-fold dispersion. Its cross-fold standard deviation is 6.52 mm, compared with
6.08 mm for the pure-scaling 𝛼 = 0.5 variant. Nozzle 0 is the largest-error fold;
although its recorded diameter, plume count, and umbrella angle match Nozzle 1,
Chapter 5 identifies it as a factory-fresh reference injector outside the prototype design
lineage, so the fold combines operating-condition extrapolation with an injector-
population shift. Its larger group count affects the precision and composition of that
fold estimate but does not give it additional weight in the equal-fold arithmetic mean.
Every ablation cell uses seed 42, so sub-0.3 mm differences are treated as unresolved
using the nearest available multi-seed variability estimate, not as a formal significance
test.

residual-pressure configuration has the lowest five-fold mean, while the per-fold values
show that this aggregate result does not imply dominance on every individual nozzle
family.

    The single-split improvements reported earlier in this section and the LONO
ablation reported here are mutually consistent: amplitude scaling is the load-bearing
element, log-pressure residuals provide modest further gains, and diameter is best
left absorbed within 𝐴 rather than re-exposed. The numerical values differ because
the LONO protocol holds out an entire nozzle family rather than a stratified random
subset; the LONO mean MAE (7.46 mm) is by design higher than the in-domain test
MAE (2.91 mm) since it measures generalization to nozzle geometries never seen
during training, rather than interpolation within a supported domain. The full-chapter
reporting protocol introduced in Section 4.7.1 reuses these same LONO splits for
the cross-model comparisons in Chapter 5. The production feature set is therefore
supported by multi-family LONO evidence rather than only by the single-split accuracy
quoted earlier, while the single-seed design and confounded nozzle matrix still limit
causal interpretation of individual features.

Figure 30: The three-stage training curriculum. Stage 1 uses representative SGQR
trajectories, Stage 2 uses the full post-fit-shielded SGQR archive, and Stage 3 uses raw
CDF points partitioned by coverage. Warm starts propagate weights and scaler states;
the frozen Stage 2 teacher supplies distillation targets where direct raw supervision is
unreliable.


4.5     Three-Stage Neural Surrogate Training Curriculum
The ordering of the three stages is deliberate, not incidental, and follows a curriculum-
learning strategy [64]. Each stage addresses a specific limitation established by
the censoring audit: trajectory-level noise, inter-plume variability, and late-time
observability loss. Stage 1 gives Stage 2 a well-conditioned starting point so that the
heteroscedastic fit is not asked to learn the mean trend and inter-plume spread from
scratch simultaneously.
    The MLP surrogate model is trained as a curriculum across three data populations
rather than a single monolithic regression. Stage 1 learns a stable condition-level
mean from the representative SGQR trajectories in Dataset #3. Stage 2 expands to
the full post-fit-shielded SGQR archive and learns the heteroscedastic inter-plume
spread, yielding the Stage 2 teacher. Stage 3 then reintroduces the Dataset #1 raw CDF
observations where coverage is reliable, falling back to that teacher where FOV-cap
hits and density-drop signal loss render direct raw supervision unsafe. Figure 30
summarizes the curriculum and the data population feeding each stage.

4.5.1   Stage 1: Deterministic Baseline on Amplitude-Scaled Targets

Stage 1 trains a deterministic mean model on a representative trajectory subset drawn
from Dataset #3. For each file-level operating condition group, the fitted curves are
evaluated at 𝑡far = 5 ms, the group median penetration is calculated, and the nearest
actual SGQR fitted trajectory is retained as the Stage 1 representative. This is not the
P50/SGQR oracle from Dataset #2. It yields 599 representative trajectories, divided
by group into 421/89/89 training/validation/test trajectories.


                                           88
   The loss applied to the amplitude-scaled target 𝑆ˆ is
                          (︂      )︂        [︃ (︂                )︂ 2 ]︃
                                                         2
         Lstage1 = MSE 𝜇,     ˆ 𝑆 + 𝜆 var E log 𝜎
                                ˆ                       ˆ − 𝑐0
                             [︄      (︃        )︃ 2 ]︄        [︄         (︃ 2 )︃ 2 ]︄
                                        𝜕𝜇 𝑆                               𝜕 𝜇𝑆
                    + 𝜆 1 E ReLU −                     + 𝜆 2 E 𝑔(𝑡) ReLU              ,
                                         𝜕𝑡                                 𝜕𝑡 2

where 𝑐 0 = −2 is the log-variance prior center: since Stage 1 trains on a single
representative trajectory per condition, there is no inter-plume spread to learn, so
log 𝜎ˆ 2 is regularised toward the constant 𝑒 𝑐0 ≈ 0.135 to provide a numerically stable
initialisation for Stage 2 warm-starting rather than a data-estimated uncertainty. The
one-sided derivative penalties encode two physical constraints on spray penetration:
(i) ReLU(−𝜕𝜇 𝑆 /𝜕𝑡) 2 penalises only negative slopes: the negative sign selects the
violation direction (a decrease in penetration is unphysical), and the square scales the
penalty with the severity of the violation while leaving correct non-negative slopes
with zero gradient; (ii) ReLU(𝜕 2 𝜇 𝑆 /𝜕𝑡 2 ) 2 penalises only positive second derivatives:
positive curvature means the spray is still accelerating, which is unphysical once
the initial momentum-driven transient has passed. The gate 𝑔(𝑡) = 𝜎((𝑡 − 𝑡 𝑐 )/𝜏𝑐 )
suppresses the concavity penalty during the early transient where acceleration is
expected, activating it progressively only beyond 𝑡 𝑐 . Both penalties are evaluated on the
physical reconstruction 𝜇 𝑆 = 𝐴 𝜇ˆ so that the constraints act on the observable millimetre
scale rather than on the scaled surrogate output. In the discrete implementation on
the time grid, these derivatives are approximated by finite differences: a first-order
forward difference for the slope and the standard three-point (central) stencil for the
curvature. The network is a three-layer MLP of dimensions [512, 512, 128] with
Swish gated activations, a random unit masking rate of 0.3, and a decoupled weight
decay AdamW optimizer with gradient clipping and early stopping based on validation
loss. The Stage 1 run achieved a test physical MAE of 2.91 mm; the holdout metrics
are summarized alongside the other two stages in Table 19, and Figure 31 shows the
corresponding training curves.
    This test MAE is not directly comparable to the Stage 3 results reported in Chapter
5: it is measured on a single curated representative trajectory per condition under
the amplitude-scaled, shape-constrained objective, not the common fixed-table CDF
protocol used downstream. Stage 1’s contribution to the curriculum is accordingly
the well-behaved warm-start checkpoint from which Stage 2 learns the conditional
spread, not a stand-alone accuracy claim.




                                               89
Figure 31: Stage 1 training curves: total epoch loss, physical-space MAE, and the
two shape-penalty terms (𝑑1 monotonicity and 𝑑2 gated concavity) for the training,
validation, and test splits. The shape penalties grow while the mean fit tightens during
the first ∼40 epochs and then decay as the network reconciles accuracy with the
physical constraints.


4.5.2   Stage 2: Learning the Full Plume Population and Its Conditional Spread

Stage 2 warm-starts from the Stage 1 checkpoint and expands the training set from
the representative subset to the post-fit-shielded SGQR trajectories in Dataset #3,
replacing the MSE data-fitting term with a heteroscedastic Gaussian NLL over the
scaled target [70]:

The first term is the heteroscedastic NLL on the scaled target 𝑆.
remain on the physical space reconstruction 𝜇 𝑆 = 𝐴 𝜇.     ˆ The row table expands to
71,700 post-fit-shielded trajectories, retaining rows that pass the fit-level mask and
the far-time robust gate for inter-group spread. The split is performed at the grouped
case level rather than the individual plume trajectory level, so trajectories from the
same physical case do not leak across training, validation, and testing partitions. Each
trajectory has 512 time samples over 0 ms to 5 ms, and the Stage 2 training set contains
36,710,400 point-wise samples: training on 49,908 trajectories, validating on 10,961,
and testing on 10,831.
    The retained trajectories span the operating range covered by the production
training set: control back pressure from 1 to 4 bar, injection pressure from 1,400
to 2.200 bar, injection duration from 340 to 1.200 µs, and fitted far-time penetration
from 26.2 mm to 217.3 mm. Thus, Stage 2 sees more than a single median curve
per condition; it observes the residual intra-condition plume and repeat variability
that survives post-fit shielding. The heteroscedastic output separates the conditional
mean trajectory from its conditional spread, while retaining the known mean–variance
coupling limitations of variance-network training [65], [66].

     epoch                                        -0.317
     Test, restored best  151    5,545,472        -0.240 /      5.63 mm    7.61 mm
     checkpoint                                   -0.240

Table 13: Summary of Stage 2 losses. The best validation NLL is achieved at Epoch
116; early stopping terminates training at Epoch 151 after failing to find a better
validation loss. The test row is evaluated after early stopping, but the model weights
are restored to the best validation checkpoint prior to test evaluation.


    Training reuses Stage 1’s feature mappings and scaler states. The input vector
comprises normalized time coordinate, 𝜃 𝑧 , 𝑁plume,𝑧 , Δ𝑡 inj,𝑧 , 𝑃cb,𝑧 , log 𝑃inj,𝑧 , and
log 𝑃ch,𝑧 (seven features in total); the architecture remains a three-layer MLP of
dimensions [512, 512, 128], warm-started from the Stage 1 checkpoint. The Stage 2
run uses the AdamW optimizer with a learning rate of 8 × 10−4 , decoupled weight
decay coefficient of 10−4 , batch size of 96, gradient clipping norm of 1.0, log-variance
bounds of [−10, 6], and early stopping with a patience of 35. Therefore, the only
intentional modification in the data fitting term is the replacement of deterministic
MSE with a Gaussian NLL and its learned variance output.
    Table 13 shows a holdout test MAE of 5.63 mm, close to the validation MAE. The
Stage 2 test MAE is numerically higher than Stage 1’s 2.91 mm, but the two numbers
are not directly comparable: Stage 1 trained on 599 representative trajectories (one
per operating condition group), while Stage 2 trains on 49,908 trajectories spanning
intra-group inter-plume variability. A broader, more diverse training population is an
expected input, not a relaxed accuracy standard. The mean predicted standard deviation
of 7.61 mm remains larger than the point error scale. This is the desired behavior
for this stage: the model does not force the mean function to explain all residual
inter-plume variability, but instead allocates non-zero conditional uncertainty that
the subsequent collision and distillation stages can exploit. The resulting checkpoint
serves as the teacher model for the distillation stage.




                                             91
Figure 32: Stage 2 NLL training curves showing Gaussian NLL for training and
validation per epoch. The best validation checkpoint is reached at Epoch 116; early
stopping halts training at Epoch 151.




Figure 33: Toy inference of the Stage 2 NLL model for a representative 2000 bar,
5 bar case. Predictions are generally smooth and well-behaved, but the earliest portion
of the trajectory is still shaped predominantly by the fitted target prior, motivating
subsequent distillation refinement.




                                          92
4.5.3   Stage 3: Blending Direct Observations with Teacher Guidance

The NLL stage produces heteroscedastic predictions that closely track the fitted training
targets, but the fitted curve is not equally faithful across the entire time axis. Near
the start of injection, absolute penetration is small, so any timing offset, fit delay
ambiguity, or smoothness prior yields a relatively large fractional error; the earliest
portion of each predicted curve is shaped primarily by the fitted target prior rather than
directly observed raw plume dynamics. Figure 33 illustrates this in a representative
toy inference sweep.
    To refine early-time dynamics using raw CDF penetration observations while
preventing collapse in late-time sparse regions, a knowledge distillation stage is
introduced atop the Stage 2 teacher [71], [73]. In population terms, Stage 3 blends the
Dataset #1 raw observations with the Stage 2 teacher learned from Dataset #3. The
student network inherits the teacher weights and undergoes further training under the
joint guidance of raw CDF penetration and the teacher.
    CDF Penetration Coverage Intervals. Because the raw CDF penetration se-
quences are right-censored (by 𝑡 ≈ 3 ms, many operating conditions have lost over
70% of their measurements), direct raw supervision cannot be applied uniformly
across time. For each operating condition group, raw observations are binned over
[0, 5] ms at Δ𝑡 = 0.1 ms intervals. The smoothed sample count per bin dictates three
mutually exclusive intervals: high-confidence raw interval (smoothed coverage ≥ 70%
of peak), uncertain raw interval (coverage between 20% and 70%), and teacher-only
extrapolation interval (coverage below 20% or fewer than 4 samples per bin). Inter-
val transitions require at least two consecutive bins to remain in the same interval,
preventing isolated dips from triggering premature interval changes.
    The 70%/20% coverage boundaries are conservative engineering rules rather
than optimal values drawn from a full grid search. Archived single-seed five-fold
LONO ablations provide only directional support for disabling the Stage 3 anchor and
retaining mild KD guidance in the reliable interval; the differences are small relative
to cross-fold dispersion (Appendix C, Table 36). A systematic sensitivity analysis of
the coverage boundaries and 𝜎ref remains outside the current scope.
    Figure 35 demonstrates why this interval division motivates distillation over a
single-source target. In the representative 2000 bar, 5 bar case, the surviving CDF
penetration points in the uncertain interval still carry useful local information, but
they occupy a thinned and biased subset of the teacher’s wider predictive distribution.
Consequently, the teacher Gaussian acts as a stable continuation of the raw trend rather
than an arbitrary replacement for measurement data.
    Supervision Weights. Each grid point receives two independent weight channels:

           Interval                               Raw Weight 𝑤 raw   KD Weight 𝑤 kd
           High-confidence raw interval                 1.0               0.25
           Uncertain raw interval                       0.0                1.0
           Teacher-only extrapolation interval          0.0               0.75

    Both channels are further modulated by a global time bin rebalancing weight
(inverse frequency, clipped to [0.5, 2.0]).

                                                 93
Figure 34: Generated raw CDF coverage heatmap used to audit the 70%/20% interval
boundaries. Contours mark the thresholds distinguishing reliable from uncertain, and
uncertain from teacher-only.


    In the high-confidence raw interval bins, the raw NLL is the primary signal,
accompanied by mild KD regularization. In the uncertain raw interval bins, raw
supervision is dropped, and the teacher KD MSE dominates. In the teacher-only
extrapolation interval bins, the KD weight is slightly reduced to acknowledge teacher
extrapolation uncertainty.

                                         94
Figure 35: Uncertain-interval CDF observations and the Stage 2 teacher distribution
for 𝑃inj = 2000 bar and 𝑃ch = 5 bar. Black points are surviving raw observations,
orange points are teacher samples, and the blue curve and band show the teacher mean
and ±1𝜎. The thinned raw support motivates teacher guidance as coverage declines.


                                        95
    Two single-run checks support this interval filter: substituting the default cleaned
wide-format CDF sequence table with a pre-truncated raw source yielded negligible
RMSE change (ΔRMSE ≈ −0.003 mm), whereas disabling the low-confidence raw
weight filter degraded RMSE by roughly 0.065 mm. The latter effect (≈ 0.065 mm)
exceeds this model’s seed-to-seed RMSE variation (≈ 0.040 mm across five seeds)
and is comparable in magnitude to the MLP–SVGP in-domain point-accuracy margin
(0.072 mm); the single run is therefore read as directionally consistent and numerically
non-negligible relative to these margins, though not statistically resolved on its own. It
corroborates (but does not on its own prove) the first-principles argument that treating
right-censored late frames as ordinary raw supervision is worse than relying on the
teacher in these bins.
    Composite Loss. The training objective is
        L = Lraw + LKD + 𝜆 onset Lonset + 𝜆 anchor Lanchor + 𝜆 𝑑1 𝑃𝑑1 + 𝜆 𝑑2 𝑃𝑑2 ,
The KD component is

This form was not the first candidate. Because both teacher and student output

form expression,
               (︂                              )︂         𝜎2 𝜎12 + (𝜇1 − 𝜇2 ) 2 1

and the forward direction 𝐷 KL ( 𝑝 teacher ∥ 𝑝 student ) (teacher ≡ 1, student ≡ 2) was the

minimising Gaussian NLL is itself equivalent to minimising 𝐷 KL ( 𝑝 data ∥ 𝑝 model ), so
the same forward direction in Stage 3 preserves this training-objective consistency.
This direction, however, opens a subtle optimisation loophole. The teacher–student
mean mismatch enters only through (𝜇1 − 𝜇2 ) 2 /(2𝜎22 ), divided by the student’s own
predicted variance. Holding the means fixed, minimising over 𝜎2 yields the stationary
point (𝜎2★) 2 = 𝜎12 + (𝜇1 − 𝜇2 ) 2 : forward KL can reward the student for absorbing
uncorrected mean error into inflated variance rather than reducing the error itself.
Near the field-of-view boundary, where surviving raw observations pull the student
mean downward, this uncertainty-hedging behaviour makes it cheaper to widen 𝜎2
than to lift 𝜇2 back toward the teacher mean, drifting toward an over-broad student
with a systematically under-predicted late-stage mean, a pattern confirmed empirically
by the Stage 3 distillation diagnostics in Chapter 5. The production loss above removes
this shortcut: separating the KL term into a direct mean MSE and a log-variance
MSE means the mean penalty no longer divides by 𝜎22 , so inflating the variance can
no longer discount it, while the log-variance term pins 𝜎2 directly to the teacher 𝜎1
instead of leaving it as a free slack variable.

     Training Diagnostics. The student is optimized using AdamW with decoupled
weight decay, lr = 3 × 10−3 , a gradient clipping norm of 1.0, and early stopping

mixed objective rather than model-comparison metrics.
     These Stage 3 loss values cannot be directly compared to the Stage 2 scaled
NLL. Stage 2 optimizes SGQR targets over the post-fit-shielded trajectory table,
whereas Stage 3 blends raw CDF penetration likelihood, teacher-student KD MSE,
onset supervision, and shape penalties over distinct raw/KD interval populations.
Accordingly, the reproducibility pipeline exports the metrics for each stage with
explicit target population labels, rather than collapsing all NLL-like figures onto a
single leaderboard.
     Scope of the Distillation Refinement. Stage 3 should be read as a censoring-aware
refinement of the observation window (its raw/teacher blending operates over the same
training population already used by Stages 1 and 2) rather than as a remedy for missing
design-family coverage. No new nozzle-family information enters the pipeline at this
stage: the feature design that carries whatever cross-family generalization the surrogate
has was fixed earlier, motivated by the scaling regressions and licensed by the LONO
feature audit of Section 4.4.2. Accordingly, the Nozzle0 cross-design-family collapse
that dominates the LONO evaluation (Chapter 5, Section 5.5) reflects that earlier
feature-and-population design, not a shortcoming this refinement stage is positioned
to fix.




                                           98
    The three-stage surrogate model constructed above will be compared against
physical baselines and kernel-based alternative backbones in Chapter 5. The following
subsections distinguish their roles: the Sparse Gaussian Process is treated as an
alternative probabilistic regressor for the same low-dimensional scaling task, while
the Hiroyasu–Arai and Naber–Siebers forms remain empirical physical baselines.

4.6 Sparse Variational Gaussian Process as an Alternative Sur-
    rogate Backbone
The Sparse Variational Gaussian Process (SVGP) model should be interpreted as
a strong alternative surrogate backbone rather than a weak baseline. It solves the
identical low-dimensional regression problem as the MLP: targeting the amplitude-
scaled penetration 𝑆ˆ = 𝑆/𝐴, transforming predictions back to millimeters via 𝐴 𝜇ˆ and
  ˆ and evaluating on the same CDF observations and fixed tables. It is the inductive
𝐴𝜎,
bias of the Gaussian Process that differs, not the physical objective.
    The implemented Gaussian Process employs an "a-only" five-feature set,
                           [ 𝑡norm , 𝜃 tilt,𝑧 , 𝑛plumes,𝑧 , 𝑡𝑖,𝑧 , 𝑃cb,𝑧 ],
with the primary pressure, density, and diameter dependencies absorbed into 𝐴. It
is sparse and heteroscedastic: one SVGP learns the conditional mean, and a second
learns the log-residual variance. Both use a Matérn 5/2 kernel with Automatic
Relevance Determination lengthscales, while inducing-point variational inference
renders the million-point-scale Stage 3 tables computationally feasible [39], [40], [86].
Both heads instantiate 𝑀 = 256 inducing points initialized by 𝑘-means clustering
on 50,000 subsampled training inputs, with learn_inducing_locations=True
allowing further refinement during optimization; the variational posterior uses a
Cholesky parameterization that retains the full 𝑀 × 𝑀 covariance rather than a mean-
field approximation. Training uses Adam with learning rate 10−2 , mini-batch size
1024, and 80 epochs on each head.
    This architecture is well suited to the low-dimensional, smooth regression task. In
the refreshed in-domain and LONO comparisons reported in Chapter 5, the SVGP is
the stronger point predictor. Outside densely sampled regions, its kernel formulation
supplies an explicit support-dependent prior, but this does not turn a held-out nozzle
family into an interpolation problem.
    Three implementation choices define the SVGP inductive bias: ARD Matérn-5/2
kernels, a fixed 𝑀 = 256 inducing-point budget, and 𝑘-means initialization on the
training inputs. Together they produce a smooth, support-dependent sparse-kernel
predictor and are plausible contributors to the LONO behavior reported in Chapter 5.
However, the present experiments do not ablate these mechanisms separately, and
𝑘-means initialization does not guarantee that a held-out cross-family point lies near
multiple inducing supports. The evidence therefore supports an architecture-level
empirical comparison with the MLP, not a causal attribution of the transfer gain to
ARD, variational capacity, or inducing-point placement individually. The SVGP was
also not evaluated with the full seven-feature set, so the effect of that restriction remains
untested.

                                                 99
    The MLP is retained as the primary protocol instantiation for three reasons. First,
the Stage 3 student carries an onset side-branch used by the screening interface; the
current SVGP replaces the penetration (𝜇, 𝜎) regressor but lacks this side channel.
Second, the MLP directly implements the regime-aware raw/KD curriculum. Third,
the MLP supports the feature, anchor, and distillation ablation matrices within the
available workstation budget. The Chapter 5 comparison therefore separates point-
accuracy performance from these deployment and diagnostic capabilities rather than
treating either architecture as universally preferable.

4.7    Physical Baselines and Evaluation Protocol
The Hiroyasu–Arai and Naber–Siebers baselines reported in the results chapter are
evaluated on the same canonical clean tables as the learned surrogates, rather than fitted
directly to the raw image pixels. Therefore, the comparison tests different penetration
function families under identical observation definitions and evaluation tables; it does
not conflate differing image-processing methods.
    Upstream, the canonical tables are generated by the common workflow described
in Chapter 3: nozzle center calibration, polar plume strip extraction, background
and gradient enhancement, axial intensity projection per plume, CDF front quantile
extraction, anomalous jump truncation, short-segment removal, pixel-to-millimeter
conversion, and SOI/hydraulic delay time alignment. The fixed-table evaluator then
supplies the baseline models with point-wise penetration 𝑆(𝑡), the corresponding
normalized operating-condition features, and canonical physical quantities derived
from the test-matrix JSON file, such as 𝑑𝑛 , Δ𝑃, 𝜌𝑎 , and 𝐴.
    The Hiroyasu–Arai baseline uses the two-stage fittable function
                   ⎧    (︃       )︃ 1/2
                   ⎪
                   ⎪       2Δ𝑃
                   ⎨ 𝑘 𝑣 𝜌𝑙             𝑡,            𝑡 < 𝑡𝑏 ,
                   ⎪
                   ⎪
                   ⎪                                                           𝜌𝑙 𝑑𝑛
         𝑆HA (𝑡) =      (︃    )︃ 1/4                             𝑡𝑏 = 𝑘 𝑏               ,

where 𝑑𝑛 is the nozzle hole diameter, Δ𝑃 = 𝑃inj − 𝑃ch , and 𝜌𝑙 , 𝜌𝑎 are the fuel and
ambient gas densities [13]. The "calibrated" row in the results table does not simply
recycle literature constants; it fits the logarithmic parameters (𝑘 𝑣 , 𝑘 𝑝 , 𝑘 𝑏 ) on the
training split using Huber robust least squares, and subsequently evaluates the fixed
parameters on the validation and test splits. The logarithmic parameterization ensures
that the gains and breakup timescale remain positive, while the Huber loss mitigates
the impact of minor image-derived outliers on the global empirical law.
    The √︁Naber–Siebers baseline employs the delayed square-root form 𝑆NS (𝑡) =

scaling group, distinct from the production amplitude divisor 𝐴 of Section 4.4. The
reported row fits log 𝑘 NS and 𝜏, with the delay constrained to [−0.1, 0.8] ms; angle
correction exists only as an optional implementation path, and the final comparison
uses neither the training-set cone angle nor the oracle cone angle.

    To make the physical baselines usable in probabilistic evaluations, their uncertainty
is not assigned as an arbitrary constant. Post-fitting, the training/validation residuals are
time-binned at 0.1 ms intervals, and the robust spread is estimated using 1.4826 MAD,
with a floor set at 0.5 mm to prevent unrealistically narrow bands in sparse early
intervals. This 𝜎(𝑡) allows the empirical laws to report 1𝜎 and 2𝜎 coverage and
CRPS, while preserving their traditional penetration-related mean functions. The
SVGP described in Section 4.6 is evaluated on the exact same tables, but is construed
as an alternative probabilistic surrogate backbone rather than a traditional physical
baseline.

4.7.1   Evaluation Protocol and Metrics

All models are scored on common fixed tables loaded from explicit cleaned dataset
roots. The evaluation pipeline does not rerun image processing or create new training,
validation, and test partitions; it applies frozen checkpoints and pre-fitted baseline
parameter sets to canonical point tables generated upstream. The refreshed Chapter 5
results are therefore common-table comparisons, not newly isolated untouched-test
evaluations. A separate archived Stage 3 diagnostic sweep compared KD formulations
and 𝜆 𝜎 values on a censoring-inclusive fixed table; because that table informed
the design choice, those diagnostics remain design-selection evidence rather than
independent confirmation.
    Four evaluation perspectives are kept separate because they address different
questions. The conservative uncensored CDF protocol removes intervals beyond
the density-drop signal-loss or FOV-cap thresholds from Dataset #2 and serves as
the primary physical point-level metric for censor-controlled comparisons. The
full-clean CDF protocol measures overall point accuracy on the common cleaned
CDF set including right-censored regions, and is reported as a censoring-inclusive
supplementary check. The P50 observation protocol collapses each condition to the
median observed penetration points, testing the robustness of the mean head against
inter-plume spread. The SGQR extrapolation protocol evaluates predictions over the
companion SGQR grid fitted to the P50 trajectories; it is an extrapolation diagnostic,
not an independent ground truth.


                                            101
    Point Error Metrics. Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) and Mean Absolute Error
(MAE) measure the accuracy of the predicted conditional mean 𝜇(𝑡) against the
point-wise penetration observations in each fixed evaluation table. Both are computed
across the full time horizon represented by those trajectories. Because errors within a
single trajectory are temporally correlated, the effective sample size is much smaller
than the raw point count; the reported values describe the average per-point error
across a heterogeneous population of operating conditions and time points, rather than
statistically independent samples.
    Empirical Coverage. A heteroscedastic model outputs a conditional mean 𝜇(𝑡) and
conditional standard deviation 𝜎(𝑡). The primary calibration question is whether the
predicted uncertainty intervals are empirically credible: under a Gaussian predictive
distribution, 68.3% of observations should fall within the [𝜇 − 𝜎, 𝜇 + 𝜎] interval,
and 95.4% within the [𝜇 − 2𝜎, 𝜇 + 2𝜎] interval. The empirical 1𝜎 coverage on each
fixed evaluation table is the principal calibration metric for each probabilistic model:
values above 68.3% indicate conservative (over-dispersed) intervals, while lower
values signify overconfident (under-dispersed) predictions. For physical baselines,
𝜎(𝑡) is the time-binned robust residual spread estimated during baseline fitting; for
MLP stages, 𝜎(𝑡) is the direct network output.
    Continuous Ranked Probability Score (CRPS). The CRPS evaluates the pre-
dicted cumulative distribution function 𝐹 against a scalar observation 𝑦 [75], defined
as                                    ∫ ∞
                      CRPS(𝐹, 𝑦) =          (𝐹 (𝑧) − 1[𝑧 ≥ 𝑦]) 2 d𝑧.
                                       −∞
Under a Gaussian predictive distribution with mean 𝜇 and standard deviation 𝜎, this
simplifies to a closed-form expression that rewards both accuracy (small |𝜇 − 𝑦|) and
calibration (a 𝜎 that is neither too wide nor too narrow). Lower CRPS is better; a
perfectly sharp and accurate point prediction yields CRPS = 0.
    Reliability, ECE, and PIT. For each prediction, the observation can be transformed
into a predictive percentile,             (︃          )︃
                                             𝑦 𝑖 − 𝜇𝑖
                                   𝑢𝑖 = Φ                .
                                                 𝜎𝑖
If the Gaussian predictive distribution is calibrated, the 𝑢𝑖 values should be uniformly
distributed over [0, 1]. Reliability diagrams compare nominal lower-tail probabilities
against the empirical fraction of observations below each percentile; the expected
calibration error (ECE) here summarizes the absolute deviations over the chosen
probability grid, adapting the calibration-error construction used in neural-network
calibration [76]. Probability Integral Transform (PIT) histograms portray the same
information: U-shaped histograms indicate under-dispersion, hump-shaped histograms
indicate over-dispersion, and skewness indicates mean bias. Because the evaluation
table contains hundreds of thousands of time-correlated points, the KS p-value is
treated as a sensitivity indicator rather than a binary pass/fail label; the histogram
shape, ECE, and coverage curves carry the engineering interpretation.




                                            102
    Leave-One-Nozzle-Family-Out Evaluation. The standard group-aware split
retains all nozzle families in the training and validation partitions and evaluates
interpolation across the supported domain of pressure and duration combinations. To
probe out-of-distribution robustness in a physically meaningful direction, a secondary
LONO evaluation is performed: all operating conditions belonging to one nozzle
family are removed from the training and validation splits, the model is retrained on
the remaining families, and performance is evaluated solely on that family. LONO
uses five representative families (nozzles 0, 1, 2, 4, and 5, matching the Stage 1 feature
ablation in Section 4.4.2) rather than all nine, bounding retraining compute while
including Nozzle 0 as the sole cross-design-lineage fold. The protocol therefore probes
a deliberately difficult cross-family transfer and extrapolation scenario. Results for
both evaluation protocols are reported in Chapter 5.

4.8    Probabilistic Screening Interface
The screening outputs below assume in-domain deployment; cross-family calibration
degradation is addressed in Chapter 5. The discussion of spray-wall impingement
mechanisms in the literature review concluded that the appropriate downstream
application for an uncertainty-aware penetration surrogate is a probabilistic screening
proxy rather than a binary pass/fail rule, and that penetration should be projected
onto the engine geometry plane as a rotating 2D Gaussian before integrating against
the cylinder wall and piston crown masks. The GUI prototype realizes this proxy by
coupling a Stage 3 student checkpoint with a parameterized piston crown, cylinder
wall, and slider-crank displacement model, outputting spatial snapshots, probabilistic
time histories, cumulative exposure, and animations. The MLP is the most complete
plug-and-play model for this interface: the onset-bearing production configuration
(Section 4.5, Table 14) provides both penetration (𝜇, 𝜎) and the onset side-branch
usable in the diagnostic plots, whereas the later residual-adapter lineage (Chapter 5)
drops the onset side-branch and exposes penetration only. The SVGP from Section 4.6
could replace the penetration mean and variance in the same geometric integration, but
it has no side model under any configuration and so cannot provide the onset branch.
The default configuration uses the Design A piston, RPM = 250, crank phase 0◦ , a
time window of 0–5 ms, and a 60-frame time grid.
       Spray Probability Density. Let each plume’s spray axis be rotated by tilt angle
𝜃 tilt from radial, and let 𝛼 denote the half-cone angle. In the plume-aligned coordinate
system, the student prediction supplies the axis-parallel standard deviation 𝜎∥ (𝑡), while
the lateral spread is represented by a second Gaussian. The current implementation
does not set the lateral standard deviation equal to the geometric half-width; it interprets
the fixed half-cone angle as an approximate three-standard-deviation envelope and
adds a grid-size floor:
                          (︁             )︁
                       max 𝜇+ (𝑡), 𝜎∥ (𝑡) tan(𝛼/2)
                    {︃                                   }︃
     𝜎⊥ (𝑡) = max                                  , 2Δ𝑥 ,       𝜇+ (𝑡) = max(𝜇(𝑡), 0).
                                    3



                                           103
Figure 37: Generated sensitivity sweep for the lateral Gaussian cone-width factor.
The selected 3𝜎 interpretation is marked alongside adjacent 𝛼 values to display the
dependence of screening probabilities on this modeling convention.


Here Δ𝑥 is the grid spacing, defaulting to 0.25 mm/px. The lower bound prevents
the lateral PDF from non-physically collapsing near the start of injection when 𝜇(𝑡)
is small, and the 1/3 factor treats the geometric cone angle as an approximate 3𝜎
envelope, an engineering convention for probabilistic collision screening rather than a
value calibrated against measured spray widths. This fixed-𝛼 assumption serves only
for early screening and ranking, not as a validated time-varying spray envelope model.
    This same 𝜇+ (𝑡) construction also resolves a broader consistency concern: be-
cause the underlying per-time Gaussian prediction (𝜇, 𝜎) has unbounded support, it
nominally assigns nonzero density to non-physical negative-penetration positions.
This does not leak probability mass in the deployed screening interface, however,
since the density is evaluated on a canvas grid anchored at the physical nozzle exit
and centered using 𝜇+ (𝑡) rather than 𝜇(𝑡); density nominally corresponding to 𝑆 < 0
therefore falls outside the sampled grid and mask domain and is never included in the
discretized wall-hit integral below.

     Piston Geometry Scheduler. The prototype exposes three interchangeable crown
profiles. The default Design A is a piecewise circular arc structure, assembling a
bowl-shaped crown from five arc radii (outer bowl, inner bowl, crown, lip, and bottom)
with rigid closure constraints that maintain geometric consistency whenever any radius
is altered. Design B is a dual-arc bowl profile with tangential constraints. The Hermite
variant replaces the arc structure with a quintic Hermite spline, defined by a small set
of control points, each carrying position, tangent vector, and second derivative vector
in the (radial, depth) cross-sectional plane. Matching position, slope, and curvature
at each node provides 𝐶 2 continuity across segment boundaries, meaning the crown
surface lacks curvature jumps, a property relevant to the collision integration grids
used here, where sudden curvature changes can alias onto rasterized masks.




                                          105
    This parameterization also simplifies systematic parameter sweeps: altering a
single scalar (e.g., bowl depth) continuously deforms the entire bowl profile, while the
rest of the control point structure adapts via splines without requiring the re-solving
of constraint equations. Figure 38 illustrates the baseline four-point crown with its
tangent arrows (panel a) alongside a seven-level depth sweep (panel b), showcasing
continuous, smooth deformation induced by changing one parameter. All three designs
return a binary piston mask on the identical canvas grid as the spray density; recent
default GUI artifacts use Design A, so the black crown shown in default screening
images is not the Hermite swept profile.
    Piston Kinematics. The crown axial position follows the simplified slider-crank
form [87]
                    ℎcrown (𝑡) = ℎ0 − 2𝑠 cos(𝜔𝑡 + 𝜑),            RPM
                                                         𝜔 = 2𝜋 60   ,
implemented with 𝑡 passed in seconds to the angular velocity term, while the GUI
displays the time axis in milliseconds. This expression is the slider-crank limit as
𝑟/𝑙 → 0, omitting higher-order connecting rod corrections. The phase 𝜑 situates
the reference event at 𝑡 = 0. Default prototype settings (RPM = 250, 𝑠/2 = 55 mm,
ℎ0 = 75 mm, 𝜑 = 0) correspond to a short injection window near top dead center,
during which the crown moves less than 1 mm; within this regime, connecting rod
corrections are negligible.
    Per-Frame Collision Integration. The instantaneous collision probability and
cumulative exposure over the injection window are
                     ∬                                        ∫ 𝑇
          𝑃hit (𝑡) =    𝑝(𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑡) 1Ωpiston (𝑡) (𝑥, 𝑦) d𝐴, 𝐸=     𝑃hit (𝑡) d𝑡,
                                                                 0

where 1Ωpiston (𝑡) is the binary indicator function for the instantaneous piston mask;
wall probability 𝑃wall (𝑡) is computed by integrating the same PDF over the wall mask
𝑥 ≥ 120 mm. Spatial integration executes over the canvas grid, while cumulative
exposure is computed via trapezoidal quadrature over the time grid. When the loaded
checkpoint carries the onset side-branch, its probability output is evaluated on the
identical time grid and plotted alongside 𝑃hit (𝑡) and 𝑃wall (𝑡), allowing near-SOI onset
confidence and geometric interception probability to be read separately; checkpoints
without the onset side-branch omit this overlay.
    Default Input Conventions. Recent GUI artifacts use 𝑑𝑛 = 0.34 mm, injection
pressure 2000 bar, chamber pressure 15 bar, control back pressure 4 bar, 10 plumes,
injection duration 800 µs, fixed cone angle 20◦ , tilt angle 20◦ , and a 0–5 ms window.
These settings define the archived demonstration; the resulting probability ranges,
peak interception score, cumulative exposure, and geometry sensitivity are reported in
Chapter 5, Section 5.7.
    Claimed Scope. The rotating Gaussian envelope, analytical cone angle spreading,
and slider-crank crown model are engineering simplifications, not resolved droplet-
scale physics. This prototype is the probabilistic screening proxy argued for in
the literature review; it is not a collision predictor validated against experimental
high-speed cinematography or CFD impingement fields.

                                                           107
    The modeling workflow presented in this chapter (from cleaned reduced-order
targets to staged heteroscedastic training, and finally to probabilistic collision scoring)
constitutes the surrogate modeling contribution of this thesis. This chapter establishes
the design decisions, their motivations, and the ablation evidence supporting them;
Chapter 5 reports the head-to-head quantitative verdict (coverage, CRPS, and leave-
one-nozzle-out statistics) among Stage 1, Stage 2, Stage 3, the physical baselines, and
the SVGP alternative backbone.




                                           108
5     Results
Chapter 4 defined the penetration surrogate as a censoring-aware probabilistic regres-
sion problem embedded in an optical-archive-to-screening workflow. This chapter
systematically builds the evaluation evidence and gives the quantitative verdict: the
evidence does not point to a single leaderboard winner, but instead separates into three
claims – fixed-table in-domain accuracy, transfer to unseen nozzles, and usability as a
conservative screening surrogate.
    The fixed-table numbers in this chapter are taken from a refreshed evaluation
bundle. The bundle spans two cleaned dataset roots – the second-level cleaned root
and the third-level quality-gated root – and regenerates four fixed-table evaluation
protocols on top of them: the uncensored CDF protocol, the censoring-inclusive
full-clean CDF protocol, the observed P50 protocol, and the SGQR continuation grid.
Uncensored and full-clean are not two separate datasets but two protocol views of the
same held-out population: the former excludes right-censored regions as the primary
evaluation lens, while the latter retains them as a censoring-inclusive supplementary
check. All four protocols are primarily reported against the third-level quality-gated
root; the second-level root is used only as a dataset-sensitivity check, not the main
verdict. In this thesis, “QC” means quality control that applies numerical constraints
to each trajectory through a pre-training plume gate; its concrete rules are given in
Section 5.5.2.
    The boundary of evidential scope is therefore explicit: the refreshed bundle supports
the fixed-table in-domain comparison after the canonical protocols; the transfer claim
– together with the curriculum-ablation evidence that supports it – and the screening
claim continue to use their archived thesis artifacts. The refreshed run also exports
cluster-bootstrap 95% confidence intervals for the fixed-table point metrics, so headline
gaps can be checked against trajectory-level resampling uncertainty rather than seed
spread alone; seed standard deviations are still reported where multiple training seeds
define the relevant summary statistic.
    Table 17 defines the metric vocabulary used in the chapter. RMSE and MAE
measure point accuracy, bias measures signed systematic error, and tail metrics such
as P95 absolute error identify rare but large misses. Coverage, PIT reliability, ECE,
CRPS, NLL, and sharpness describe the predicted distribution rather than only the
predicted mean.

5.1    Evaluation Map, Protocols, and Artifact Contract
The chapter uses a layered evidence contract. Layer 1 is numeric evaluation: given a
dataset root and checkpoint roster, the pipeline exports canonical point tables, grouped
CSV summaries, reliability tables, PIT histograms, coverage curves, and a wide metric
table. Layer 2 is plotting: figures read only the Layer 1 artifacts and do not reload
checkpoints. This separation is important for the thesis because it allows figure styling
to change without changing the metric definitions.




                                          109
Short name       Full name                      Meaning in this chapter

                                                larger mistakes.

                                                tail-sensitive than RMSE.
Bias             Mean signed error              Positive values mean over-prediction; negative
                                                values mean under-prediction.

Table 17: Metric vocabulary used in this chapter. Point metrics and distributional
metrics answer different questions and are therefore not collapsed into one ranking.


    Table 18 defines the protocols used below. The primary fixed-table results use
the third-level quality-gated dataset root because it is the most conservative cleaned
production set available in the refreshed evaluation bundle. The second-level cleaned
root remains a dataset-sensitivity check rather than the main verdict. The censoring-
inclusive full-clean CDF protocol is regenerated on the same refreshed roots and
reported as a supplementary check: it shows how the comparison behaves when
right-censored regions are included, while the main fixed-table verdict deliberately
remains the uncensored protocol.
    The metrics are also separated by evidential role. RMSE, MAE, bias, and tail
absolute error judge point accuracy. Coverage, reliability curves, PIT histograms,
CRPS, NLL, and interval scores judge probabilistic behavior. Condition-level and
time-bin CSVs diagnose where errors concentrate. This prevents one model from
being declared globally superior when it only wins one axis.

5.2    Primary In-Domain Verdict
The primary fixed-table comparison is the QC-gated uncensored CDF protocol
(540,367 held-out CDF points per seed). Table 20 deliberately keeps the mainline
verdict separate from the post-hoc residual arms. Within the deployed-model and
baseline comparison, the single-output thesis SVGP is the strongest point predictor,
with an RMSE of 4.087 mm and an MAE of 2.857 mm. The five-seed production MLP
mean is close behind at 4.185 mm RMSE and 2.978 mm MAE, while both physical
curve-fitting baselines are much less accurate.

    While the single-output SVGP delivers higher point accuracy, the production MLP
offers more conservative coverage. Specifically, the five-seed mean 1𝜎/2𝜎 coverage
is 0.888/0.990 for the MLP, compared with 0.717/0.946 for the SVGP. The mainline
comparison therefore indicates an accuracy–coverage trade-off: the SVGP backbone
improves point prediction, whereas the production MLP serves as a conservative and
operationally convenient screening surrogate. The residual-SVGP and residual-MLP
extensions are analyzed in Section 5.6.3 as post-hoc adaptation evidence rather than
as part of the deployed-surrogate verdict.
    A similar pattern appears in the empirical coverage comparison (Figure 40). The
production MLP produces wider, over-dispersed intervals: its empirical coverage lies
above nominal Gaussian levels, reducing the risk, under this evaluation protocol,
of underestimating penetration uncertainty during screening. In contrast, the SVGP
variants are sharper and more accurate but have lower empirical coverage, representing
a different operating point in the accuracy–conservatism trade-off.

                                        115
5.3     Secondary Protocol Checks
The primary CDF table does not by itself explain whether a model is accurate inside the
observed window or merely stable under dense point-wise averaging. The observed P50
and SGQR continuation-grid protocols provide that separation. Table 21 summarizes
the refreshed QC-gated mainline comparison.
    On the observed P50 protocol, the single-output thesis SVGP yields lower point
error than the production MLP mean (2.516 mm versus 2.831 mm RMSE). On
the SGQR continuation grid, the same relative ordering holds (9.680 mm versus
10.047 mm), while the production MLP maintains higher 1𝜎 coverage. Because all
models exhibit positive bias in the late-time continuation region, the SGQR grid serves
as a stress test of reduced-order continuation under extrapolative conditions, not as a
direct frame-by-frame measurement score. The residual-SVGP results are analyzed
with the other post-hoc models in Section 5.6.3.

5.3.1   Censoring-Inclusive Full-Clean Check

The full-clean CDF protocol is the censoring-inclusive counterpart of the primary
table: it evaluates the same held-out population without excluding right-censored
regions. In the refreshed bundle this protocol is regenerated on the same QC-gated
root as the primary verdict, so the two tables differ only in whether right-censored
regions are included. It remains a supplementary check rather than the headline verdict
because the primary comparison deliberately excludes regions where the target itself
is shaped by field-of-view censoring.
    On the QC-gated root, the refreshed full-clean table contains 689,162 held-out
CDF prediction points, of which 148,795 (21.6%) lie in right-censored regions. In the
earlier archived bundle, the same censoring pressure was quantified at the trajectory
level: 9,630 of 24,316 cleaned trajectories (39.6%) were right-censored. This is the
spatial censoring ratio audited in Chapter 4 and cited in the data chapters. Both the
point-level and trajectory-level views show that treating the last visible measurement
as a physical terminal penetration would constitute a material protocol error rather
than a bookkeeping detail. On the refreshed censoring-inclusive view, the five-seed
production MLP reaches 4.584 mm RMSE, 3.211 mm MAE, 0.829 mm bias, and
conservative 1𝜎/2𝜎 coverage of 0.869/0.986. The Stage 3 SVGP is again the stronger
point predictor (RMSE 4.440 mm with 95% interval [4.373, 4.499], MAE 3.109 mm)
but with a sharper predictive distribution and less conservative coverage (0.706/0.942),
and both physical baselines remain far behind (11.187 mm and 10.442 mm RMSE,
corresponding to MLP reductions of 59.0% and 56.1%). The censoring-inclusive
ordering is therefore the same as the primary uncensored verdict.
