                                        120
5.4    Calibration and Distributional Behavior
Coverage at a few sigma levels is only a coarse summary, so the refreshed pipeline
also exports reliability curves, PIT histograms, CRPS, NLL, interval scores, and
sigma-bin calibration tables for the probabilistic fixed-table sets. These diagnostics
reinforce the same trade-off. The production MLP is conservative: its mean predicted
standard deviation is about 5.96 mm, and its 1𝜎 coverage is about 0.888. The mainline
single-output SVGP is sharper, with mean predicted standard deviation about 3.73 mm,
lower CRPS (2.063 mm), and lower RMSE, but less conservative 1𝜎 coverage. The
post-hoc QC-retrained residual-SVGP pushes CRPS lower again (1.988 mm) while
still remaining less conservative than the production MLP.
     This distinction is important for the downstream screening interface. A model
with a more accurate mean prediction is not automatically a safer rank-oriented
screening surrogate if its predictive distribution is under-dispersed for edge conditions.
Conversely, the MLP’s conservative empirical coverage reflects a deliberately cautious
predictive distribution under the measured protocol; it does not constitute a certified
bound on false-negative rates.




                                          121
                            (a) Reliability overlay.




                          (b) CRPS–sharpness plane.

Figure 44: Distributional diagnostics on the primary QC-gated uncensored CDF
table. The SVGP variants occupy the sharper and lower-CRPS region, while the
production MLP trades point sharpness for conservative coverage.



                                     122
Figure 45: PIT histograms for the refreshed primary comparison. These histograms
complement the reliability overlay by exposing model-wise deviations from a uniform
predictive distribution.




                                       123
5.5     Cross-Nozzle Transfer and Failure Boundaries
The fixed-table results above test in-domain performance on held-out points from the
cleaned dataset root. They do not answer whether the surrogate can generalize to
an entirely unseen injector. That question is handled by the Leave-One-Nozzle-Out
protocol retained from the archived thesis artifacts.
    Transfer performance is evaluated using a five-fold LONO protocol that holds out
Nozzle0, Nozzle1, Nozzle2, Nozzle4, and Nozzle5 in turn. Across all five folds, the
Stage 3 SVGP achieves a lower average RMSE than the MLP (10.16 mm ± 9.57 mm
versus 12.55 mm ± 12.46 mm). The same ordering remains after the out-of-design-
family Nozzle0 fold is excluded: the SVGP gives 5.95 mm ± 1.98 mm, compared with
7.00 mm ± 1.18 mm for the MLP.
    The empirical coverage results are mixed. After Nozzle0 is excluded, the 1𝜎
coverage values are nearly identical (0.697 for the SVGP and 0.696 for the MLP),
whereas the MLP retains higher 2𝜎 coverage (0.923 versus 0.890). The ± terms
denote cross-fold standard deviations from the archived single-seed LONO run, not
multi-seed training variability.
    The LONO results therefore show a clearer point-transfer difference than the
in-domain comparison: the SVGP is the more accurate point-transfer backbone.
The production MLP remains the retained model in the active workflow because it
provides conservative in-domain uncertainty estimates, exposes the auxiliary onset
output required by the screening GUI, and supports affordable ablation of the training
curriculum.
    The Nozzle0 collapse should not be hidden by averaging: Figure 47 gives direct
visual evidence. It is better interpreted as a cross-design-family extrapolation failure
than as an ordinary fold. This boundary is scientifically useful: it states where the
learned surrogate requires either additional family coverage, trunk-level adaptation, or
retraining.

5.5.1   Physical Failure-Mode Check: Nozzle 3

The LONO averages identify transfer boundaries, but they do not show the raw spray
morphology behind difficult cases. An archived Nozzle 3 condition provides a useful
failure anatomy by pairing Phantom high-speed Mie images with the corresponding
plume-by-plume penetration curves (Figure 48). In the early window from 0.6 ms to
about 1.0 ms, several bottom-right plumes remain short and discontinuous while other
holes have already formed continuous jets. The penetration curves show the same
event as a flat-top or step-like trajectory: a small leading liquid mass appears first,
decelerates, and is later overtaken by a faster main jet.




                                          124
                                  (a) Per-fold RMSE.




                               (b) Per-fold 1𝜎 coverage.

Figure 46: Archived LONO diagnostics for the Stage 3 MLP and SVGP. Nozzle0
dominates the high standard deviation and marks the out-of-design-family boundary
for both architectures.


    This image evidence is consistent with wake-driven catch-up or transient orifice-
throttling interpretations, but it is not a causal diagnosis. The experiment does not
contain simultaneous gas velocity, internal-flow visualization, or liquid-mass identity
tracking. The direct modeling conclusion is narrower and more useful: a smooth
monotone continuation target cannot represent abrupt step-like curvature changes
without distorting the target or the predictive uncertainty. These cases explain why
some within-family conditions remain difficult even after the global LONO and
fixed-table metrics look acceptable.




                                         125
Figure 47: Why Nozzle0 is out-of-design-family (archived residual-family-head
study). Nozzle1 through Nozzle8 share a single refurbished injector; Nozzle0 is a
new, factory-fresh reference injector, and its early-time penetration envelope is not
represented by the modified-injector training population. Left: normalized early-time
penetration response of Nozzle0 (≈2.5× the modified nozzles and never crossing
below it). Right: the full scatter separates Nozzle0’s early-time branch, which the
frozen trunk never represented.


5.5.2   Heuristic Data Quality Control: Plume-Level QC Gate

The third-level quality-gated dataset is materialized from the second-level cleaned
export by auditing CDF wide-series traces before any surrogate is trained. The gate
operates on whole condition–plume rows: it removes a plume under an operating
condition when the measured trace shows repeated shape pathologies or systematically
low penetration relative to the other holes under the same condition. The decision
rests on two heuristic rule families:

    • Shape pathology: each trace is checked for negative steps, spike-like steps or
      first-/second-derivative jumps, and “plateau-then-jump” behavior (a run of small
      steps followed by a sudden large jump); when the fraction of traces exhibiting
      these pathologies within a condition–plume group exceeds a threshold, the
      group is flagged as a recurrent shape pathology (thresholds in Table 23).


                                         126
 (a) 𝑡 ≈ 0.6 ms–0.8 ms    (b) 𝑡 ≈ 0.8 ms         (c) 𝑡 ≈ 1.0 ms      (d) 𝑡 > 1.0 ms




Figure 48: Visual evidence and corresponding penetration curves for an extreme
transient spray in Nozzle 3. The image sequence shows initially short, discontinuous
bottom-right plumes followed by a later main jet, while the lower curves show the
associated flat-top and step-like penetration behavior. The figure supports a modeling
failure-mode interpretation, not a uniquely identified fluid-mechanical cause.




                                           127
Parameter                 Meaning                                                Default
Negative step             A drop in penetration between adjacent samples         −2.0 mm
                          beyond this value flags a negative step
Step-spike magnitude      A single-step jump reaching this value flags a         14.0 mm
                          spike-like step
First-derivative spike    A rate of change between adjacent samples reaching     450 mm/ms
                          this value flags a spike
Second-derivative spike   A second-difference magnitude reaching this value      10 000 mm/ms2
                          flags a spike
Plateau step ceiling      Upper bound on consecutive small steps counted as a    1.3 mm
                          “plateau”
Plateau-then-jump         A jump following a plateau reaching this value flags   8.0 mm
                          “plateau-then-jump”
Strict-mode criterion     Group trace count and pathology fraction               ≥ 3 traces /
                          combination that flags recurrent shape pathology       ≥ 40%
Loose-mode criterion      Same, for groups with more traces and a lower          ≥ 5 traces /
                          fraction threshold                                     ≥ 20%

Table 23: Heuristic thresholds for the shape-pathology criteria (default configuration;
script-level mapping in Appendix A).


     • Systematic low penetration: at several early time grid points, each plume’s
       median penetration is compared to the condition-level median across the other
       holes under the same condition, giving a ratio and a MAD-based robust 𝑧-score;
       if the ratio falls below threshold at enough time points, the plume is flagged
       as systematically (or severely) low. If the same plume_idx on the same nozzle
       repeatedly earns this flag across enough conditions, it additionally earns repeat
       support across conditions (thresholds in Table 24).

    The hard-drop rule is an OR over three conditions: an optional manual image-
evidence flag; systematic low penetration with either repeat support or the severe
threshold; and recurrent shape pathology. The drop decision is then applied to the
CDF, Cartesian-width, and polar-width export tables, as well as the CDF censoring-
point table, for that condition–plume pair. The criterion is independent of any
trained surrogate – it rests only on measured trace shape, same-condition cross-hole
comparison, and cross-condition repeat support (plus optional manual image evidence)
– so QC here is a pre-training data-quality filter, not a post-hoc selection based on
model performance.

5.6     Why the MLP Workflow Was Retained
The preceding sections make the model trade-off explicit. The SVGP family is stronger
for point prediction and LONO transfer. The MLP workflow is retained for a different
reason: it makes the staged curriculum, uncertainty behavior, onset auxiliary output,
and screening deployment auditable within a Master’s thesis compute budget. The
ablation evidence explains that choice.

5.6.1   Curriculum Ablations

The Stage 2 anchor ablation tested whether an early-time mean anchor and an early-time
sigma anchor improve the teacher used by Stage 3. The 𝜇-anchor-only variant is the
most defensible teacher setting: it lowers the five-fold LONO RMSE from 14.54 mm
to 12.73 mm, improves MAE, and avoids the overconfident behavior introduced by
the double 𝜇 + 𝜎 anchor. The improvement is concentrated in a difficult fold, which is
precisely where a physically motivated anchor should matter; the full archived tables,
including the per-fold breakdown and the bias and coverage columns, are collected in
Appendix C.
    The Stage 3 supervision-mix ablation then tests how raw observations and teacher
guidance should be blended. The uncertain-regime mixed variant obtains the lowest
average LONO RMSE and MAE, but the margin between Stage 3 variants is only
about 0.21 mm, far below the cross-fold standard deviation. This should be read as a
mild selection preference, not as a sweeping architectural claim.




                                             129
Ablation result                  RMSE    MAE    Main interpretation
Stage 2 no anchor                14.54 10.80 Leaves early-time slack that can collapse on difficult folds.
Stage 2 𝜇-anchor only            12.73  9.86 Best teacher setting; improves bias and 2-sigma coverage.
Stage 2 𝜇 + 𝜎 anchor             13.05 10.06 Over-tightens early uncertainty without compensating accuracy gain.
Stage 3 baseline regime          12.68   9.97 Competitive but not best.
Stage 3 uncertain-regime mixed   12.55   9.80 Best LONO point score, with small margins relative to fold variance.
Stage 3 anchor-off               12.73   9.86 Highest archived 2-sigma LONO coverage among these variants.


Table 25: Condensed curriculum ablation verdict. The numbers are archived LONO
means. The purpose of this table is not to create another leaderboard, but to show
which design decisions are actually supported by the ablation evidence.


5.6.2      KD Loss Formulation and Near-Cap Bias

A separate Stage 3 diagnostic exposed a systematic under-prediction near the field-
of-view cap. The Stage 2 teacher was nearly unbiased near this cap, but the earlier
forward-KL student introduced approximately −2.70 mm of additional near-cap bias.
Replacing the KL term with a mixed mean-MSE plus log-variance-MSE distillation
loss removes the variance-inflation escape route: the selected 𝜆 𝜎 = 5.0 setting pulls the
near-cap bias back to about −0.57 mm, close to the teacher’s −0.59 mm. Across five
seeds, the aggregate near-cap RMSE is about 4.881 mm with a seed standard deviation
of 0.024 mm, and the near-cap bias standard deviation is 0.079 mm. The archived
slice-level evidence (the loss formulation, the supervision-regime and censoring-slice
table, and the calibration-drift diagnostics) is preserved in Appendix C.

5.6.3      Post-Hoc Adaptation: Residual, FiLM, and SVGP Extensions

The residual/FiLM/SVGP post-hoc adaptation study tests a specific physical hypothesis.
As documented in Appendix C, Nozzle 1–8 share a single refurbished injector while
Nozzle 0 is a new, factory-fresh reference unit; this hardware-state difference survives
the 𝑡 1/4 (𝐴-scaling) normalization, and no recorded operating feature encodes it. The
binary injector-family identifier (fresh reference versus the pooled refurbished families,
the family ID below) is therefore the only channel that carries this signal. On top of the
Stage 1–3 trunk of Section 4.5, this subsection tests three mechanisms of increasing
adaptation depth for injecting that channel into the MLP (Figure 51):

      • Direct family heads: replace the single mean head after the shared trunk with
        one head per family ( 𝜇ˆ = 𝑤 ⊤𝜇, 𝑓 ℎ(𝑥)); the family ID only selects the output head
        and never enters the feature vector.

      • Residual family heads: freeze the trunk and the shared mean head, then route
        the family ID to a zero-initialized additive correction ( 𝜇ˆ = 𝑤 ⊤𝜇 ℎ(𝑥) + Δ 𝑓 (ℎ(𝑥))).

      • Residual-FiLM: stack an identity-initialized last-block affine modulation ( ℎ˜ =
        𝛾 𝑓 ⊙ ℎ + 𝛽 𝑓 [88]) on the residual family head while retaining the Δ 𝑓 branch.




                                                   130
Figure 49: Archived Stage 2 anchor ablation. The 𝜇-anchor-only teacher is retained
because it improves the difficult transfer cases without forcing early uncertainty to
become overconfident.


For an unseen family, the residual and FiLM variants fall back to the shared predictor
(Δ 𝑓 = 0; 𝛾 = 𝐼, 𝛽 = 0), whereas a direct family head must hard-route to an existing
ID. The figure also identifies which components are frozen or updated and gives each
level’s warm-start source. The shared log-variance head remains shared and trainable
in all three mechanisms. This is the lightweight-adapter (PEFT) deployment pattern
referenced in the Chapter 2 workflow mapping (Table 1) [60]; the SVGP residual
variants test the same routed-residual idea on a kernel backbone (below).




                                         131
                                (a) Bias–truth curves.




                                (b) Five-seed stability.

Figure 50: Archived KD-form diagnostics. The mixed distillation loss repairs the
near-cap bias while preserving stable five-seed behavior.


    The remainder of this subsection follows three evidence chains: the controlled
MLP adaptation chain on the fixed table, the residual-SVGP in-domain point-error
frontier, and the residual-MLP within-family LONO follow-up.
    The evidence in this subsection comes from two training lineages that must be read
separately. The earlier adaptation study (archived 2026-06-08) established the family-
head → residual → FiLM ladder on an independent single-seed lineage that differs
from the five-seed production MLP in feature variant, Stage 2 teacher form, onset
auxiliary head, and trunk-freezing policy (Appendix Table 38); its numbers therefore
support within-lineage comparisons only, and direct deltas against the production
mean would misread lineage differences as architecture effects.




                                         132
Figure 51: Three injector-family conditioning mechanisms in increasing adaptation
depth (left to right): direct family heads, residual family heads, and residual-FiLM.


     Controlled retraining lineage. To isolate architectural effects from training-
lineage differences, a single-head pooled baseline without family routing (the “Stage 3
pooled baseline”; code label P0) was warm-started from the production Stage 2 winner.
It was retrained on the QC-gated data with the raw-CDF/KD mixed supervision of
Chapter 4 Stage 3, omitting only the Stage 3 onset auxiliary head. This supplies a
common-protocol starting point for the subsequent architectural changes rather than
defining another training stage. The controlled chain then proceeds as pooled baseline
→ direct family head → residual → FiLM.
     Unlike the pooled baseline, which is initialized from the Stage 2 teacher, each
subsequent level is initialized from its predecessor in a function-preserving way. The
maximum output difference remains below 10−6 after bridging, and the FiLM identity
initialization preserves the function exactly. Each level introduces one new mechanism
and distills from its predecessor. The chain is single-seed and is evaluated on the same
540,367-point QC-gated uncensored CDF fixed table as the primary comparison; for
this controlled retraining lineage, the table includes training trajectories. Table 26
is therefore a controlled architecture comparison on a common physical scale, not
an independent generalization test. The controlled pooled baseline (4.192 mm) is
consistent with the five-seed production mean (4.185 mm), indicating that the chain
starts from a representative operating point.
     The controlled chain settles three points.
     First, direct family heads bring no fixed-table benefit.

the chain is single-seed, and each level is function-preservingly initialized from its
predecessor. The evaluation table includes training trajectories, so this is a controlled

     • Point error: relative to the pooled baseline, the paired trajectory-cluster bootstrap
       gives ΔRMSE = +0.028 [+0.022, +0.034], with a confidence interval excluding
       zero.
     • Probabilistic scores: ΔCRPS = +0.052 [+0.049, +0.054], ΔNLL = +0.035,
       and ΔECE = +0.003 likewise show no improvement.

       2.362 to 2.231, yet the physical-scale metrics degrade; an improvement of the
       composite distillation loss does not imply an improvement on the fixed-table
       evaluation.
    Second, residual conversion is a bias-repair channel.
     • Overall bias: it drops from +0.508 to +0.050, with paired Δ = −0.458 [−0.467, −0.450].
     • P50 observed-window bias: it vanishes monotonically along the chain:
       +0.935 → +0.786 → +0.379 → +0.004.
     • Injector-family slice: the repair happens almost entirely on the refurbished
       family. The pooled baseline is nearly unbiased on the fresh reference family
       (57.6% of fixed-table rows; bias −0.019), but leaves +1.223 systematic over-
       prediction to the refurbished family; the residual head cuts the latter to +0.253,
       with within-family paired ΔRMSE = −0.072, significant.
     • Point-error trade-off: the reference family degrades slightly but significantly
       (+0.063), so the overall RMSE gain is only −0.011 [−0.020, −0.000]. The
       families’ biases differ by about 1.24 mm, which quantifies the family-conditional
       bias under pooled training and shows why only a family-routing channel can
       repair it.

                                (a) Empirical coverage.




                              (b) CRPS–sharpness plane.

Figure 52: Controlled post-hoc probabilistic diagnostics on the QC-gated uncensored

residual head, FiLM, and QC-retrained residual-SVGP; it is therefore evidence for the
controlled adaptation chain in Table 26, not a replacement for the primary production-
MLP versus single-output-SVGP comparison.


   Third, FiLM is a calibration channel rather than a point-error channel.

    • Probabilistic quality: relative to the pooled baseline, CRPS improves from
      2.283 to 2.192, NLL from 2.852 to 2.760, and ECE from 0.074 to 0.052; all
      paired confidence intervals exclude zero. This ordering also occurs in the earlier
      study lineage and is the only result that replicates stably across both lineages.



                                         135
    • Sharpness: the mean predictive standard deviation narrows from 5.79 mm to
      4.98 mm.

    • Point error and bias: FiLM’s RMSE difference against the pooled baseline
      is not significant (+0.005 [−0.009, +0.019], the only null paired delta in the
      chain). Its mean correction is nearly identical on both families (−0.293/−0.290),
      closer to a global shift than to family discrimination, and overshoots the overall
      bias to −0.292. The FiLM-versus-residual RMSE ordering also flips between
      the two lineages, so RMSE differences at this level carry no information.

    • Extrapolation boundary: no level of the chain reaches it; all four models have
      RMSE 10.66–10.74 on the extrapolated SGQR grid, and central-90 coverage
      remains conservative (0.963–0.977).

    The residual multi-task SVGP is an additive routed-residual model. In scaled
penetration space its mean has the form 𝑦ˆ (𝑥, 𝑓 ) = 𝑔shared (𝑥) + Δ𝑔 𝑓 (𝑥), where 𝑔shared
is a frozen shared Stage 3 SVGP and Δ𝑔 𝑓 is a zero-mean residual SVGP trained only
for injector family 𝑓 . The family identifier is deliberately not part of the kernel input
vector; it only routes each sample to the corresponding residual process. If a future
family has no trained residual process, the prediction falls back to the shared SVGP
with Δ𝑔 𝑓 = 0. The implementation also trains a final shared log-variance GP on the
residual errors, so the mean correction and uncertainty model remain separate.
    Among the reported fixed-table artifacts, the residual multi-task SVGP has the
lowest point-error values: RMSE 3.921, reaching 3.848 mm after QC retraining
(Appendix Table 40). However, these results come from a different training lineage
and are not differenced against the controlled MLP chain, so they do not isolate a causal
kernel-backbone effect. The residual-SVGP was not LONO-tested in the same way
as the main Stage 3 MLP and SVGP comparison, so transfer claims continue to rest
on the MLP arms. The refreshed design-comparison, climb, lineage-disclosure, and
family-slice tables, together with the archived adaptation-study figures, are collected
in Appendix C.
    The residual-MLP LONO follow-up uses the earlier study-lineage protocol (Ap-
pendix Table 42). Within the modified injector family, the residual conversion repairs
much of the bias of the direct family head and reduces RMSE across the in-family folds
from about 7.44 mm to about 5.16 mm. This result supports within-family adaptation
but does not establish zero-shot generalization to a genuinely new injector family.
    The broader conclusion is that injector identity is a useful conditioning variable,
but its value is channel-specific. In the controlled MLP chain, family identity does not
materially improve fixed-table point error: all four levels lie between 4.18 and 4.22
with overlapping confidence intervals. Instead, the residual head repairs family-linked
systematic bias, while FiLM improves uncertainty quality. These variants therefore
act as lightweight post-training adapters around a shared base surrogate.




                                          136
Figure 53: Archived residual-head LONO follow-up on modified-family nozzles. The
residual adapter improves in-family folds but exposes its boundary on design-shifted
cases.


    The residual multi-task SVGP provides a parallel routed-family adaptation on
a kernel backbone. Its reported fixed-table values define the in-domain point-error
frontier, but the lineage difference prevents attributing that gap to the kernel backbone
alone. The deployed pooled production MLP needs no identity input for point accuracy,
whereas deployments targeting bias or calibration may consider residual/FiLM-style
adaptation. The residual-SVGP has not been evaluated under a matching LONO
protocol and therefore cannot yet be treated as the preferred transfer architecture.
Because these adapters rely on known family identifiers, this adaptation is distinct
from zero-shot transfer. The Nozzle0 and Nozzle6 stress cases illustrate the boundary:
an injector identifier or output residual can shift a learned representation, but it
cannot synthesize trunk-level features for an unseen design family that the shared
surrogate never represented; the extrapolated SGQR region is likewise unaffected by
any conditioning mechanism.

5.7    Screening Demonstration and Evidence Boundary
The final purpose of the surrogate is not just to minimize penetration error. It is to
feed a probabilistic geometric screening interface. The current GUI prototype uses the
MLP because the deployed MLP workflow also provides an injection-onset auxiliary
output for temporal screening displays. An SVGP could replace the 𝜇, 𝜎 penetration
channels, but it does not directly provide this auxiliary onset output.




                                          137
Figure 54: Archived temporal summary of the wall-proximity screening prototype
for Design A. The output is a geometric screening score driven by the penetration
distribution, not a measured liquid-wall-wetting probability.


    Under the default toy inputs used in the archived demonstration, the screening
prototype maps the predicted penetration distribution into a time-resolved geometric
interception score. For Design A, the rank-oriented instantaneous piston score peaks
at 𝑃hit = 77.66%, and the trapezoidal cumulative exposure reaches 𝐸 = 2.475 ms by
5 ms. Changing only the geometry and kinematics changes the verdict: a wider bowl
at the same 250 rpm increases 𝐸 to 3.364 ms, while a faster Hermite-profile piston at
2250 rpm reduces the cumulative exposure to 1.446 ms despite a high instantaneous
opening.
    Consequently, these interception scores are rank-oriented geometric screening
metrics rather than validated probabilities of harmful liquid-state impingement in an
operating engine. The validated contribution remains the censoring-aware penetration
surrogate over its measured domain, together with a reproducible coupling from the
predictive distribution to the simplified screening interface.




                                        138
        (a) Design B, wider bowl.                  (b) Hermite-profile piston.

Figure 55: Archived geometry and kinematics sensitivity of the screening layer. The
same spray-side prediction can produce different rank-oriented screening scores after
projection onto different piston motion profiles.

5.8      Chapter Summary
Table 27 summarizes the chapter without collapsing all protocols into a single ranking.
Within the mainline deployed comparison, the single-output SVGP is the better point
predictor and the production MLP is the more conservative screening surrogate.
LONO transfer also favors the SVGP for point accuracy and exposes Nozzle0 as a
cross-design-family failure for both architectures. The raw-image failure check shows
why some within-family plume trajectories remain difficult for smooth continuation
targets. Residual adaptation is promising, but it remains a post-hoc extension. The
screening demonstration closes the workflow while keeping the wall-impact claim
at the correct evidential level. Detailed per-fold, per-regime, and residual-adaptation
diagnostics, together with the archived claims-to-evidence map of the earlier chapter
organization, are collected in Appendix C.
    The next discussion chapter should therefore not re-run every metric. Its task is to
interpret the trade-off: the thesis establishes a reproducible censoring-aware surrogate
workflow with conservative uncertainty and a working screening projection, while
also showing that matching LONO tests for the residual-SVGP, multi-seed replication
of the controlled adaptation chain, and trunk-level capacity extensions beyond output
heads are the likely next steps for point-accuracy improvements.




                                                          140
6    Discussion
6.1 Positioning of the Stage-3 Surrogate as a Screening Model
The synthetic dataset is generated by the reduced-order SGQR fitting in Section 4.3:
raw CDF trajectories are first subjected to cleansing and temporal alignment, then
fitted to the SGQR form, providing smooth extrapolation targets within the specified
time window.
     A natural objection at this point is why the workflow does not stop here and simply
promote the SGQR fit itself (or an improved empirical correlation built from its scaling
exponents) to a stand-alone predictive model, rather than proceeding through three
additional training stages to outperform Hiroyasu–Arai directly. Chapter 4’s own
diagnostics of the SGQR fit answer this. High per-trajectory fit accuracy (median RMSE
1.27 mm on accepted fits) only certifies that the three-parameter sigmoid-gated quarter-
root form can retrace an already-observed trajectory; it does not certify that (𝑘 1/4 , 𝑡0 , 𝑠)
are transferable, physically meaningful functions of operating condition. The 90.0%
post-fit-shielding pass rate is, by construction, a fit-quality and shape-plausibility
gate (a bounded far-time plausibility window, parameter sanity checks, and robust
per-file z-scores on RMSE and cost-per-point), not a test of physical generalization: a
trajectory passes because the monotonic sigmoid-gated shape happens to trace it well,
independent of whether the underlying breakup and evaporation physics is correctly
represented. Most fundamentally, the identifiability analysis (Figure 22) shows that
𝑘 1/4 , 𝑡0 , and 𝑠 are not independent physical knobs: median pairwise correlations of
∼ 0.76 and ∼ 0.56 indicate a softly redundant, coupled three-dimensional surface
rather than a clean decomposition into amplitude, onset time, and gating width,
and the pooled scaling regression built on top of this coupled surface is itself weak
(𝑅 2 ≈ 0.27) and confounded by nozzle-diameter/pressure collinearity in the design
matrix (Section 4.4.1). Taken together, these findings mean SGQR is fit for its
intended purpose: compressing noisy plume trajectories into smooth, differentiable,
per-trajectory targets, but its fitted parameters carry no validated claim to physical
generalization, so the fit cannot itself be published as a replacement empirical law
for Hiroyasu–Arai. Predictive credibility is instead earned downstream, by training a
surrogate on the full population of these fits and validating it end-to-end against raw
CDF observations, which is precisely the role assigned to Stages 1–3 below.
     Stage 1 extracts stable representative mean trajectories condition by condition
from the complete synthetic dataset, validating the 𝐴-scaling hypothesis. However,
because only one representative trajectory is retained per operating condition, using
Stage 1 in isolation neglects plume-to-plume and shot-to-shot variability, rendering it
unsuitable as a definitive screening model.
     Stage 2 extends the supervision to the entire synthetic dataset, concurrently
learning the conditional mean and conditional dispersion within the extrapolation
window via two output heads. This also explains its slightly higher MAE compared to
Stage 1: Stage 2 faces a harder supervision problem that more closely matches the
final screening requirement.



                                             141
    Stage 3 is designed around selecting different supervision signals based on the
temporal period. In early periods, where measured data are relatively dense, the
model learns directly from observed CDF data, preserving the details of the true
distribution. In late periods, where right-censoring makes observations sparse, the
model instead aligns with the conditional mean and variance from the Stage 2 teacher
model, following the rationale established in Chapter 2. The two supervision signals
are combined through a mixed KD loss that consists of a mean MSE term and a
log-variance MSE term (see Section 5.6.2). Because both the teacher and student
output unimodal Gaussian distributions, this loss has a closed-form solution, so Monte
Carlo sampling is not needed and no additional sampling noise is introduced. This
hybrid supervision explains why Stage 3 shows higher fidelity in early periods and
more conservative behavior in late periods.

6.2 Synthesis of Validated Results and Boundaries of Applica-
    bility
The validation evidence from Chapters 4 and 5 supports four principal interpretations.
First, finite-window censoring is a substantial property of the archive: 39.6% of the
cleaned CDF penetration trajectories reach their nozzle-specific field-of-view limit.
The three-parameter SGQR fit addresses the resulting target-construction problem
more robustly than the four-parameter two-branch candidate, increasing the post-
fit-shielding pass rate from 61.3% to 90.0% while retaining a median accepted-fit
RMSE of 1.27 mm. This evidence supports its use as a smooth trajectory compressor
and target generator, but does not validate physical continuation beyond the camera
window.
     Second, the time-resolved scaling analysis shows that the effective pressure-
difference exponent is close to 0.5 over the well-supported early-to-mid transient
window before moving towards the classical post-breakup asymptote. Relative to
the Δ𝑃0.25 alternative, the adopted Δ𝑃0.5 scaling produces an approximately 6.6×
tighter collapse of the representative trajectory population. This result provides the
empirical basis for the amplitude scaling and residual-pressure feature design used by
the surrogate; its physical interpretation is developed further in Section 6.3.
     Third, on the primary uncensored CDF protocol (540,367 held-out points per
seed from the nine-nozzle archive), the production MLP achieves a five-seed mean
RMSE of 4.185 mm and MAE of 2.978 mm. Under this dataset, protocol, and the
refitted baseline definitions, this is a 58.9% RMSE reduction relative to Hiroyasu–
Arai (10.192 mm) and a 54.6% reduction relative to Naber–Siebers (9.212 mm). On
the same fixed table, the seed-42 SVGP reference attains lower RMSE and CRPS
(4.087 mm and 2.063 mm, versus 4.185 mm and 2.301 mm for the MLP), whereas
the MLP produces wider in-domain intervals (1𝜎/2𝜎 coverage 0.888/0.990 versus
0.717/0.946) and supports the onset output and regime-aware distillation chain used by
the screening workflow. Because the seed protocols are unmatched, this comparison
is descriptive rather than a statistical-significance claim, and conservative coverage is
not a false-negative safety guarantee.

their operational boundaries.


    Fourth, the five-fold LONO study defines a clear transfer boundary. After excluding
Nozzle 0, the four-fold mean RMSE is 7.00 mm for the MLP and 5.95 mm for the
SVGP, indicating useful but imperfect transfer within the evaluated design lineage. On
Nozzle 0, the corresponding errors rise to approximately 35 mm and 27 mm. Nozzle 0
is a factory-fresh reference injector outside the prototype design lineage, so this fold
combines operating-condition extrapolation with an injector-population shift; it should
not be interpreted as an ordinary in-family fold or generalized into a claim about all
unseen hardware families.
    Table 28 consolidates the quantitative evidence and the boundary attached to each
interpretation.
    The intended industrial value of this workflow is as an early screening layer
that reuses existing optical archives at low cost, narrows the set of nozzle and
operating-condition candidates advanced to CFD simulation or bench testing, and
flags geometrically high-risk cases for closer inspection, though the magnitude of
that narrowing has not itself been measured in this thesis (see the cost-savings caveat
below). Its scope should be stated clearly. Quantifying those savings is an engineering
task for the deployment phase, once the screening layer is integrated into an industrial
decision process and operational data have accumulated; it falls outside this thesis’s
scope.




                                              143
    The extracted spray boundaries represent the liquid-phase envelope observed in
Mie imaging under a specific optical protocol, not the complete in-cylinder liquid-
phase ground truth [35], [36]. The model is primarily tailored for interpolative
prediction within the training operating condition domain, as opposed to unconstrained
extrapolation; moreover, it is not intended to supplant CFD, engine testing, or validated
physical models of spray-wall impingement, but rather acts as an auditable early-stage
engineering screening tool to narrow the design space before initiating higher-fidelity
validation.
    The target definition rests on shape priors rather than physical mechanisms: the
reduced-order fitting model extrapolates each spray penetration trajectory in time
using growth dominated by the quarter-power law (𝑡 1/4 ), and the neural surrogate
is constrained to be monotonically increasing via regularization; neither is derived
from droplet evaporation or breakup physics. These shape priors make trajectory
growth intuitively reasonable and correct short-term extrapolations at the tail end
of the observation window, but they do not model the imaging mechanism of Mie
scattering. This thesis further hypothesizes that, in room-temperature, non-reacting
Mie experiments, the late-stage attenuation of the image signal primarily originates
from droplet breakup, spatial diffusion, localized reduction in droplet number density,
scattering intensity dropping below the detection threshold, and detection side-effects
induced by the brightness multiplier mask. Rapid evaporative disappearance of the
fuel within a millisecond-scale window is not the primary driver. Consequently, even if
the plume leading edge progressively becomes invisible before reaching the boundary
of the observation window, this thesis still interprets it as a decline in the visibility of
the liquid-phase geometric envelope, rather than the physical termination of droplet
motion. This interpretation accords with the physical intuition of relatively slow diesel
evaporation at room temperature, though this thesis lacks synchronous measurements
of droplet size, mass flux, or evaporation rate to validate it directly. Thus, the derived
penetration should be construed as the continuation of the Mie-defined geometric
envelope, rather than direct evidence of a robust liquid-phase spray tip, localized liquid
mass, droplet size, phase state, or the potential for wall film formation [35], [36].
    This extrapolation hypothesis still lacks direct comparative validation. The
originally planned validation pathway was to use wide-field single-plume Mie videos
with an approximately twofold field of view, identical nozzle types, and similar
operating conditions provided by an industrial collaborator, to comprehensively
observe the penetration trajectory until the plateau region; subsequently, the field
of view would be artificially truncated, fitting the SGQR model solely with the pre-
truncation time series, and comparing the extrapolated curve pointwise with the full
measured trajectory that remains visible post-truncation. This experiment would
directly scrutinize the core hypothesis of the knowledge distillation (KD) chain in this
thesis: that the parametric form 𝜎((𝑡 − 𝑡 0 )/𝑠) · 𝑘 1/4 𝑡 1/4 remains a credible extension
beyond the boundary of the field of view, rather than a deviation primarily governed
by shape priors. Due to the collaborator’s inability to provide such single-plume data,
this validation could not be completed within the scope of this thesis; therefore, the
reliable time range for extrapolation using the SGQR hypothesis remains an open
empirical question.

                                           144
     The aforementioned boundaries are critical for downstream wall-impingement
screening. The interaction between the spray and the wall is not invariably deleterious:
if the majority of the liquid phase has evaporated prior to reaching the wall, the
residual wall interaction might be less detrimental, and under certain conditions,
it might even facilitate entrainment and mixing. What truly warrants prioritized
attention is direct liquid droplet impingement and continuous wall film formation
[10], [11], [12], [81]. From this perspective, the present room-temperature, non-
reacting, non-evaporating Mie dataset can be interpreted as a conservative stress case
for liquid-envelope persistence: if a candidate spray remains well separated from
representative metal surfaces under these cold optical conditions, faster evaporation
in an engine-representative hot environment would generally strengthen, rather than
weaken, the early low-risk indication. This argument is useful for upstream screening,
but it is not a proof of engine safety. Real in-cylinder operation also introduces
moving boundaries, pressure waves, squish, swirl, crossflow, temperature gradients,
and spray deflection; these mechanisms can change the relative path between the
liquid envelope and nearby metal surfaces even while evaporation shortens the liquid
lifetime. Accordingly, the model in this thesis should be more accurately interpreted as
a conservative screening surrogate for the probable proximity of the Mie-defined spray
envelope to the wall, rather than a phase-resolved predictor of harmful liquid-state
impingement. The correspondence between the liquid-phase envelope geometry and
temperature, evaporation, local droplet mass flux, and wall film formation has not
been modeled or validated in this thesis; an elevated wall-interaction score merely
denotes a higher risk of geometric proximity, and cannot be directly equated with
harmful liquid impingement or the quantity of wall wetting. Likewise, conservative
coverage of penetration uncertainty is not identical to false-negative safety: a ranking
tool can be cautious in its geometric envelope while still missing harmful wall wetting
if evaporation, droplet inertia, cone-angle evolution, or in-cylinder flow changes the
actual liquid mass reaching the surface.




                                          145
    The downstream engine geometric coupling is similarly a simplified model.
The current prototype exclusively translates the piston wall mask using slider-crank
kinematics, without back-incorporating time-varying chamber pressure, pressure
wave dynamics, or large-scale bulk gas motion into the penetration prediction. This
simplification is inherited from the OSCC measurement itself, as every injection
event occurs in a rigid chamber with a fixed back pressure; consequently, what the
surrogate learns is the penetration under quasi-static chamber conditions, rather than
the fully coupled in-cylinder transient state. Considering the sensitivity of diesel
spray penetration to ambient gas density, this decoupling should be apprehended as
a pragmatic modeling choice, rather than a proof that pressure and gas flow effects
are negligible [14]. In the target scenario of this thesis, namely short pilot injection
windows near top dead center on a millisecond timescale, this approximation remains
acceptable as a first-order engineering screen: under the current toy piston setup,
the crown displacement is less than 1 mm within a 5 ms window, and the present
requirement is geometric ranking, not crank-angle-resolved in-cylinder ground truth
prediction. When the engine speed increases, the injection timing deviates further
from top dead center, or the in-cylinder transient pressure and gas velocity significantly
alter the ambient conditions perceived by the spray, the current wall-interaction score
can only be construed as a decoupled screening proxy.
    Another pertinent limitation relates to the spray cone angle. Although a cone-angle
proxy was extracted during the image processing phase, this thesis neither systematically
analyzed it across different campaigns, nor elevated it to a learning target for the
final surrogate. Therefore, the half-cone angle 𝛼 in the downstream wall-interaction
model is a fixed input, rather than a validated, time-varying observable. Existing
spray literature indicates that the spreading angle varies with transient development,
ambient gas density, injection conditions, and nozzle geometry [14], [30]. The current
geometric screening solely learns the uncertainty in the axial penetration direction;
the transverse envelope determined by 𝛼 remains postulated rather than predicted.
    The remaining boundaries fall into three categories: generalization and out-of-
distribution limits, statistical and sample-independence caveats, and comparative-
baseline scope.

Generalization and Out-of-Distribution Bounds

    • The surrogate is strictly trained within the operating condition domain jointly sup-
      ported by experimental and synthetic data. Under genuinely out-of-distribution
      pressures, nozzle geometries, ambient temperatures, or injection strategies, its
      predictions should not be accorded equivalent credibility.




                                          146
   • The leave-one-nozzle-out evaluation is dominated by the Nozzle0 fold (the sole
     factory-fresh reference nozzle, with injector-to-injector variation not modeled),
     on which every Stage 3 variant collapses (RMSE ∼ 35 mm, systemic under-
     estimation; per-fold breakdown in Figure 46); the five-fold mean RMSE of
     12.55 mm is therefore a worst-case figure carried by this one out-of-design-
     family fold. With that fold excluded, the in-family four-fold mean (7.00 mm)
     falls below both physical baselines under the identical protocol (Chapter 5).
     The interpretive point is that the model’s cross-family risk is localized to gen-
     uinely off-design geometry, not to in-family penetration prediction. The image
     evidence of Nozzle 3 (Figure 48) further correlates the residual errors within
     the design family to leading liquid slugs, chasing and overtaking main jets,
     inter-hole asymmetry, and step-wise penetration. This trajectory morphology
     is consistent with the wake-driven chasing/overtaking mechanism reported in
     the split/multiple-injection literature. However, as this thesis possesses neither
     synchronous gas velocity measurements nor internal flow visualization or liquid
     slug identity tracking, it can only interpret Nozzle 3 as image evidence consistent
     with this mechanism; mechanical causes such as needle wobble or transient
     nozzle hole throttling remain equally viable candidate explanations that the
     data cannot exclude. The principal cross-family risk of the current model is the
     inadequate generalization to geometries genuinely outside the design family
     (Nozzle0), rather than a holistic failure to support average penetration prediction
     within the design family.

Statistical and Sample-Independence Constraints

   • Seed-to-seed RMSE is stable across the five anchor-off repeated training runs (std
     0.039 mm, Chapter 5), so the headline mean is not a single-seed artefact. It is still
     not a full statistical certification, however: the refreshed fixed-table CSV reports
     cluster-bootstrap intervals for RMSE, MAE, and bias, while condition-level
     confidence intervals remain to be established.

   • The held-out RMSE and MAE are computed based on 540,367 point-wise
     samples, but the temporal point errors within the same trajectory are mutually
     correlated. Thus, the number of independent samples is significantly smaller than
     the number of point-wise samples, and any confidence statements based on point-
     wise counts should be understood in conjunction with this correlation. Condition-
     level confidence intervals, preferably clustered by recording, condition, and
     nozzle, are still required before the tool can support formal acceptance thresholds.




                                          147
Comparative-Baseline Boundaries

    • Two categories of baselines are included in the comparison: physical empirical
      correlations (H-A and N-S) and a non-parametric probabilistic baseline (SVGP).
      Under the same evaluation protocol, both machine learning methods substantially
      outperform the physical correlations. Between the two, SVGP achieves slightly
      higher point accuracy (RMSE 4.087 mm vs. 4.185 mm), while the MLP
      produces more conservative uncertainty coverage (1𝜎/2𝜎 = 88.8%/99.0% vs.
      71.7%/94.6%).
      Lightweight regressors such as random forest, gradient boosting, or NARX have
      not been systematically included in the comparison; this thesis therefore makes
      no claim that the MLP’s point accuracy is superior to all machine learning
      alternatives.
      The choice of MLP, however, is not based on point accuracy. It rests on three
      structural capabilities: (1) a joint mean-and-uncertainty output head that directly
      supports the probabilistic screening interface; (2) a knowledge distillation chain
      that mixes synthetic right-censored targets with raw observations during forward
      propagation; and (3) a lightweight backbone that makes a full grid of 60+
      ablations × LONO × multiple training seeds feasible. None of these capabilities
      can be replicated at low cost by random forests, gradient boosting, or NARX.
      Without any one of them, the design rationale for the screening interface does
      not hold.

    • This chapter’s censoring rationale for the three-stage curriculum (Section 4.5)
      is not confirmed by a controlled ablation that trains the surrogate end-to-end
      directly on the raw, censored Dataset #1 without the SGQR-fitted intermediate
      stages. The censoring audit of Section 4.2.1 motivates the expectation that
      such a single-stage baseline would exhibit saturation bias near the field-of-view
      boundary, but this expectation is not empirically demonstrated within the present
      computational budget and is left as future work.

MLP vs. SVGP: Accuracy Trade-off and Model Selection

The accuracy trade-off is established in Chapter 5 and synthesized in Table 28: on raw
point accuracy, the SVGP holds a marginal advantage (RMSE 4.087 mm vs. 4.185 mm,
a gap of 0.098 mm) and transfers more gracefully to unseen nozzles under LONO,
while the MLP leads on uncertainty coverage (1𝜎/2𝜎: 0.888/0.990 vs. 0.717/0.946).
The MLP was therefore selected not for overall accuracy superiority, but for three
structural capabilities the kernel baseline cannot easily provide: the onset-CDF output
head required by the screening interface (Section 4.8), the regime-aware censoring
chain that mixes fitted synthetic targets with right-censored raw observations, and
the design-axis ablations covering stage curriculum and KD form. These structural
choices do not exist in the SVGP framework and therefore cannot be ablated there
(Section 5.6).



                                          148
    The optional per-family residual adapter illustrates a possible extension of this
payoff. Adding a small correction head to the frozen trunk reduces in-family LONO
RMSE by approximately 31% (7.44 → 5.16 mm) and removes the mean bias (Chap-
ter 5). The limit is equally clear: a nozzle outside the training design space cannot be
recovered by a lightweight adapter: Nozzle0 plateaus well above the 5 mm in-family
band, marking the point at which new trunk capacity is required rather than a new
head. Kernel baselines do not naturally support the same adapter paradigm: adapting
an SVGP to a new family requires re-optimising the full set of variational parameters
and inducing points, which is closer to a full retrain than a lightweight fine-tune.

6.3 Time-Varying Nature of Empirical Scaling Exponents and
    Its Implications for Feature Engineering
The time-windowed log-log regression in Chapter 4 (Figure 28) and the feature
engineering LONO ablations (Section 4.4.2) collectively unveil a physically significant
finding: within the observable window of short pilot injections, the proportionality
exponent in classical empirical penetration laws is not a global constant, but a
monotonically time-varying quantity.
    Specifically, the Δ𝑃 exponent smoothly descends from ≈ 0.58–0.68 during the
early injection phase (𝑡 ≈ 0.3–0.4 ms) to ≈ 0.25 at 𝑡 ≈ 1.0 ms, which is the Hiroyasu–
Arai post-breakup asymptotic value; the exponent for the ambient density proxy 𝑃ch
also drifts from −0.25 towards a smaller absolute value within the same window. This
pattern aligns with the stage-wise transition of spray physical mechanisms: the early
injection is dominated by orifice flow and pre-breakup momentum, with the exponent
biased towards the Bernoulli value of ≈ 0.5; as droplet breakup and entrainment
progressively dominate, the system asymptotes to the H–A post-breakup self-similar
solution.
    The second finding originates from the regression under fixed Δ𝑃: 𝑃ch still exhibits
a non-zero independent coefficient (≈ −0.21 to −0.27), indicating that injection
pressure and back pressure exert independent effects on penetration beyond what can
be described by their difference. This comports with the physical picture of orifice
throttling, cavitation, and internal nozzle flow: 𝑃inj and 𝑃ch independently affect the
internal flow state of the nozzle, and are not solely coupled to the external spray via
Δ𝑃.




                                          149
    The above two findings concurrently demonstrate that any global constant exponent
assumption (including the Δ𝑃1/4 of H–A and the empirically selected Δ𝑃0.5 of this
thesis) is a mean-field approximation, valid only within a specific temporal and
conditional domain. For short pilot injections, the training mass window is dominated
by the transitional momentum phase (the effective observation interval for most
trajectories is concentrated between 0.3 and 1.0 ms), rather than being governed by
the post-breakup asymptotic phase. This is precisely the empirical motivation for
selecting Δ𝑃0.5 instead of Δ𝑃0.25 as the magnitude scaling group in this thesis, and
it concurrently elucidates why the LONO ablation achieved consistent gains when
log 𝑃inj and log 𝑃ch were input as independent residuals: the network needs to learn a
time-varying, condition-dependent residual structure superimposed on the fixed Δ𝑃,
and this is precisely the degree of freedom that a single global exponent fails to afford.
    It is imperative to note that this finding does not negate the physical validity of the
Hiroyasu–Arai relation as a post-breakup asymptotic law. The contribution of this
thesis here is to document that: within the specific observable time window of short
pilot injections, the H–A asymptote has not yet been fully established, transitional
momentum physics dominates, and this conclusion carries quantifiable practical
significance for the feature design of the surrogate model.

6.4 Future Validation Pathways for the Probabilistic Wall-
    Impingement Screening Prototype
The wall-impingement screening prototype delineated in Section 4.8 realizes the prob-
abilistic proxy advocated in the literature review, but the frame-by-frame impingement
probability score 𝑃hit (𝑡) has not yet been benchmarked against physical spray-wall
observations. This gap constitutes a well-defined subsequent endeavor rather than
an open-ended limitation, as the requisite reference data already exists within the
experimental dataset of the industrial collaborator.
    For industrial deployment, five criteria should be satisfied before the prototype is
used as anything stronger than a rank-oriented pre-screen. First, the SGQR-based late
continuation should be validated against wide-field single-plume Mie recordings by
artificially truncating the field of view and comparing the extrapolated trajectory with
the full visible measurement. Second, engine-representative optical wall data should
establish whether the geometric interception score detects the first observed wall-
contact frame and ranks cumulative spray exposure correctly. Third, a CFD-annotated
benchmark should quantify screening recall, false-negative rate, and computational
savings for the proposed triage workflow. Fourth, uncertainty summaries should be
reported at condition or campaign level with independent confidence intervals rather
than relying only on correlated point-wise samples. Fifth, the fixed half-cone angle
should be replaced or bounded by a learned, time-varying cone-angle model once the
cone-angle proxy extracted in Chapter 3 has been validated.




                                           150
    The most direct validation target is high-speed optical experiments under repre-
sentative piston geometries, namely, frame-by-frame imaging of the injection process
under engine-representative boundary conditions. Such experiments can directly
observe the expansion of the spray in the vicinity of the piston crown and compare
it with 𝑃hit (𝑡): The first frame in which spray contact is observed provides an em-
pirical boundary for impingement onset, and the cumulative spray exposure ∫       within
the injection window can be juxtaposed with the integrated score 𝐸 = 𝑃hit d𝑡. This
comparison will scrutinize the axial penetration surrogate under dynamic geometric
conditions that the training dataset cannot provide.
    Another complementary pathway is CFD numerical simulation. High-fidelity
spray calculations can resolve liquid-phase distribution, wall liquid film mass, and
local droplet concentration, whereas the surrogate defined by Mie imaging merely ap-
proximates these physical quantities with a rotated Gaussian envelope. The positioning
of this surrogate is not to supplant CFD, but to serve as a low-cost upstream screening
layer: operating conditions where the impingement probability exceeds the threshold
proceed to full computation, while those safely below the threshold are filtered out.
Validating the screening recall rate, false negative rate, and computational savings
of this stratified workflow necessitates a set of CFD-annotated reference operating
conditions; the current dataset does not yet possess these. Only after such a benchmark
can conservative geometric coverage be translated into a defensible industrial safety
margin.
    Both validation pathways will resolve a structural open question in the current
prototype: whether a fixed half-cone angle 𝛼 is acceptable, or whether it is necessary
to construct a time-varying transverse broadening model using the cone-angle proxy
extracted in Chapter 3. Only after acquiring frame-by-frame matched reference data
can the sensitivity to 𝛼 be reliably assessed.




                                          151
7     Conclusions
Chapter 1 framed this thesis as an end-to-end engineering pipeline (from a raw
optical video archive to an uncertainty-aware design-triage tool) for marine multi-fuel
pilot injection screening. The principal novelty of this thesis is not a new spray
law or neural-network architecture, but an integrated, censoring-aware workflow that
connects finite-FOV Mie observations, reduced-order trajectory targets, heteroscedastic
surrogate learning, and geometric risk-oriented screening. The validation evidence
and its interpretation are presented in Chapter 6; this chapter distils the resulting
contributions and main findings.

7.1    Novelty and Contributions
The primary contributions of this thesis are structured across three levels:

    1. Core Methodological Contribution: An end-to-end censoring-aware learning
       chain that unifies finite-FOV observation auditing, Sigmoid-Gated Quarter-Root
       (SGQR) target construction, raw/teacher hybrid supervision, and heteroscedastic
       neural prediction. This formulation mitigates the risk of mistaking optical image
       window exit for physical spray saturation.

    2. Downstream Methodological Contribution: A geometric screening interface
       that projects predicted penetration probability distributions into time-resolved
       wall-interception scores (𝑃hit (𝑡) and cumulative exposure 𝐸). This serves as a
       continuous geometric proxy for early-stage design triage ahead of computational
       fluid dynamics (CFD) simulation.

    3. Engineering Contribution: An automated, CUDA-accelerated video pro-
       cessing and checkpointing pipeline capable of ingesting raw multi-hole Mie
       scattering archives (processing 2,992 recordings in approximately 72 minutes)
       into auditable plume-wise temporal observables.

7.2    Main Findings
First, finite field-of-view censoring is too prevalent to treat boundary-reaching trajec-
tories as physically saturated. The SGQR formulation provides a robust, smooth target
representation for this setting, while remaining a shape-based continuation hypothesis
rather than a validated physical spray law.
    Second, the pressure-scaling exponent is time-dependent over the observed short-
pilot-injection window: the early-to-mid transient is better represented by a value
near 0.5 before the behaviour moves towards the classical post-breakup asymptote.
This finding motivates the surrogate’s amplitude scaling and its use of injection and
chamber pressure as separate residual features.




                                          152
    Third, the staged surrogate substantially improves penetration prediction over
the refitted empirical correlations within the primary evaluation domain. The MLP
and SVGP results reveal a genuine architectural trade-off rather than an unqualified
winner: the SVGP gives slightly stronger point prediction, whereas the MLP provides
the output structure, staged distillation pathway, and more conservative in-domain
coverage required by the proposed screening interface.
    Fourth, transfer is useful but bounded within the evaluated nozzle design lineage.
The failure on Nozzle 0 shows that an injector-population shift combined with
operating-condition extrapolation cannot be treated as routine interpolation and
requires new supporting data or model adaptation.

7.3   Scope
These conclusions remain limited to the room-temperature, non-reacting, constant-
volume-vessel conditions and the Mie-defined liquid-envelope observables represented
in the archive. The downstream wall-interception score is a simplified geometric
proximity proxy, not a phase-resolved prediction of harmful liquid impingement.
Wide-FOV continuation, physical wall-contact ground truth, and clustered confidence
intervals therefore remain the principal validation needs identified in Chapter 6.

7.4   Closing Statement
By establishing an end-to-end, censoring-aware workflow, this thesis demonstrates
how archival optical Mie scattering recordings can be systematically repurposed into
an uncertainty-aware pre-screening tool. The resulting methodology provides an
auditable, reusable framework with the potential to narrow candidate nozzle geometries
and operating conditions prior to resource-intensive CFD simulations and engine
validation trials.

                                                156
B Methodology Primer for Non-Machine-Learning
  Readers
This appendix is intended for readers whose primary background is in fluid mechanics
or mechanical design. It briefly introduces four categories of techniques involved
in this thesis: computer vision, robust optimization, GPU parallel computing, and
neural network training. The main text only invokes these concepts when necessary to
explain methodological choices; more fundamental terminological explanations are
consolidated here to prevent the literature review from assuming a tutorial function.

Computer Vision and Signal Processing.            Computers store images as numerical
matrices, where each pixel corresponds to an intensity value in the matrix. Image
processing is essentially performing mathematical operations on these matrices.
Convolution is one of the most common operations: a small template window slides
across the image pixel by pixel, performing a weighted sum at each step. Different
convolution kernels can be used for smoothing, edge extraction, or texture enhancement.
The Sobel operator is a class of convolution kernels used to estimate local brightness
gradients. It responds strongly to abrupt intensity changes such as liquid-phase
boundaries, making it suitable for spray boundary localization. The polar coordinate
transformation converts images from pixel row-column coordinates to a coordinate
system with angle and radial distance as axes; by applying this transformation around
the nozzle center, the directional structure of a multi-hole spray can be collapsed into
a one-dimensional angular signal. The Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) decomposes
signals into frequency components, making periodic structures appear as frequency-
domain peaks; this study uses it to identify the rotational periodicity of the multi-hole
spray and estimate the nozzle hole angle offset.

Robust Optimization and Curve Fitting.          Least-squares fitting seeks a curve that
minimizes the sum of squared residuals between the observation points and the curve.
When a small number of frames are affected by optical noise or quantization errors,
the squared penalty amplifies the influence of these points. The Huber loss retains the
squared penalty when the residual is small, and transitions to an approximate absolute
value penalty once the residual exceeds a threshold, thereby reducing the leverage
effect of outliers [84], [85]. Gradient descent in neural network training calculates
the partial derivatives of the loss function at the current parameters and iteratively
updates the parameters in the direction that reduces the loss.




                                          157
GPU Parallel Computing.          CPUs typically have fewer but more complex cores,
suitable for serial logic; GPUs possess a large number of simpler cores, ideal for
performing the same operation simultaneously on many data elements. Convolution,
thresholding, angular binning, and frame-by-frame aggregation in image processing
perfectly fit this paradigm. CUDA is NVIDIA’s parallel computing platform; CuPy
provides a Python interface similar to NumPy, allowing the processing pipeline in this
study to offload the primary array operations to the GPU. Its engineering significance
lies in transforming the processing of terabyte-scale video archives from a lengthy
task, which is difficult to iterate repeatedly, into a workflow that can be re-run as
calibrations, thresholds, and objective definitions are adjusted [52].

Neural Networks and Deep Learning.            The Multilayer Perceptron (MLP) used in
this study consists of several linear layers and non-linear activation functions, taking
operating condition features and time as inputs to output penetration predictions.
Dropout randomly masks a portion of neurons during training, reducing the risk of
overfitting under sparse operating condition support [90]. AdamW decouples weight
decay from the adaptive gradient update, helping to improve generalization [91].
Smooth activation functions such as SiLU/Swish make derivative transmission more
continuous, facilitating the use of physical derivative penalties [89]. Mean Squared
Error (MSE) corresponds to conditional mean regression; heteroscedastic Gaussian
Negative Log-Likelihood (NLL) allows the network to output both the conditional
mean and conditional variance simultaneously, providing error bands of varying widths
for different operating conditions [70]. Knowledge distillation first trains a teacher
model that performs stably on clean data, and then uses its soft outputs to guide the
student model to learn in regions where labels are sparse or unreliable [73].




                                          158
Figure 57: Cleaned trajectories and SGQR fitted curves for condition T24 on Nozzle 1
(cleaned CDF penetration). Solid lines represent the frame-by-frame raw penetration
observations, and dashed lines are the corresponding 𝑡 1/4 single-branch (SGQR) fitted
curves. The fitted curves smoothly extrapolate to 3 ms after the observation ends,
providing a censoring-aware target for the surrogate training.

C.1    Archive Scale and Fitted-Target Example
The extraction and fitting workflow generated 295 cleaned fitted trajectory files across
the processed campaign archives after converting a high-throughput Mie video archive
into structured CSV/JSON tables, checkpoints, fitted targets, model artifacts, and
diagnostic plots. Across the broader archive, the workflow covers 9 nozzles, 266
condition subfolders, and 2,992 recording files; its practical value lies in making
terabyte-scale raw optical inputs reproducible and auditable rather than leaving them
as isolated post-processing products. Figure 57 shows a representative fitted-target
example: the cleaned curves retain the plume-to-plume variation, while the fitted
trajectories provide a smooth and censoring-aware target that remains faithful to the
observed liquid-phase envelope.

C.6     Residual-Adaptation Detail
Nozzle 1 through Nozzle 8 and Nozzle 0 use different injectors (see the comparison in
Section 5.5, Figure 47 in the main text): the first eight share a single refurbished injector,
while Nozzle 0 is a new, factory-fresh reference unit. Under the same 𝑡 1/4 (𝐴-scaling)
regularization, Nozzle 0’s early-time penetration response is systematically higher than
Nozzle 1–8. This physical difference motivates the residual/FiLM/SVGP post-hoc
adaptation experiments in this section: injector identity is used as a conditioning
variable to test whether lightweight post-training adaptation on top of the frozen
shared Stage 3 backbone can partly repair this nozzle-dependent systematic bias; the
architecture comparison across arms is now shown at the start of the corresponding
main-text subsubsection (Figure 51).
    The tables below are the refreshed detail behind the post-hoc adaptation summary
of Chapter 5: the training and evaluation protocol disclosure for the evidence lineages,
the injector-conditioning design comparison, the full residual climb with calibration
columns, and the injector-family slice of the controlled chain. Except for the disclosure
table, all are regenerated on the QC-gated uncensored CDF protocol; they supersede
the Level-2-era versions of the original study. The figures that follow are archived
from the 2026-06-08 adaptation study: their annotated values reflect the Level-2-era
protocol of that study, so the refreshed equivalents in the tables take precedence where
they differ.

Table 41: Injector-family slice of the controlled chain (evalrun_20260713_123732,
QC-gated uncensored CDF table, all values in mm). The pooled baseline is nearly
unbiased on the majority (reference) family and leaves the systematic over-prediction
to the refurbished family; the residual bias repair happens almost entirely on the refur-
bished family (+1.223 → +0.253; within-family paired ΔRMSE −0.072, significant)
at the cost of a small but significant degradation on the reference family (+0.063), so
the flat overall RMSE is a net of two opposing family effects. FiLM’s mean correction
is nearly identical on both families (−0.293/−0.290); its gains are concentrated in the
probabilistic metrics.
